#4976. DarkSword iPhone Exploit Drains $4M in Crypto Theft

Home » Crypto Market News » DarkSword iPhone Exploit Drains $4M in Crypto Theft

Zero-click browser vulnerability drains millions from unpatched devices by targeting stored recovery phrases.

A silent zero-click exploit chain targeting iPhones has drained more than $4 million in cryptocurrency across 365 TRON wallets, according to a report by Bitquery.

Dubbed DarkSword, the sophisticated attack kit compromises unpatched iOS devices through Safari—allowing hackers to extract private keys and seed phrases without requiring a single transaction signature or app download.

DarkSword, a kit for hacking iPhones, chains six iOS vulnerabilities to hijack devices running iOS 18.4 through 18.7. It compromises vulnerable iPhones through malicious websites opened in Apple’s Safari browser.

The exploit chain combines multiple security vulnerabilities to gain access to sensitive device information without requiring users to download an application or authorize a wallet transaction.

Google’s Threat Intelligence Group (GTIG), together with cybersecurity firms Lookout and iVerify, published technical analyses of the exploit on March 18, 2026. Their findings describe an attack chain capable of accessing passwords, messages, and cryptocurrency wallet information stored in Apple’s Keychain.

A separate investigation by cybersecurity firm Censys identified modules targeting 18 cryptocurrency wallet applications. The toolkit can reportedly search photos and note-taking applications for recovery phrases, potentially exposing wallets whose credentials were stored on the device.

Targeted applications reportedly include MetaMask, Phantom, Trust Wallet, and Coinbase. The vulnerability lies in the iPhone’s operating system rather than the individual wallet applications.

Unlike conventional phishing attacks, DarkSword does not necessarily require victims to connect their wallets or approve suspicious transactions. Attackers who obtain a private key or recovery phrase can use another device to sign and broadcast transactions.

Bitquery’s investigation identified a network of 43 collection addresses connected through a shared fee wallet. The addresses received funds from 365 TRON wallets between October 5 and November 4.

The investigation found that at least $4.06 million in USDT was transferred. Of the 365 wallets affected, the median loss was $1,029. Eight wallets lost more than $100,000 each, accounting for 63% of the total USDT losses. The attackers reportedly sent small amounts of TRX to victims’ wallets shortly before draining their balances.

Bitquery traced the transfers back to an initial transaction whose amount and timing matched those of a publicly reported iPhone theft. However, blockchain records cannot reveal which devices or wallet applications individual victims were using.

Apple has released security updates addressing the vulnerabilities used by DarkSword. Google recommends installing the latest available iOS version or enabling Apple’s Lockdown Mode when an update is not possible.

Changing wallet passwords or revoking token approvals is not sufficient if attackers already possess the recovery phrase. A compromised seed requires generating a new one and transferring the assets.

DarkSword device-level attacks bypass wallet approval checks, so exposed seed phrases can lead to total loss without a single signature from the victim.

#4974. Ripple's XRP Ledger Lending to Receive Repayment Fix

The XRP Ledger's lending protocol is in for a tidy-up of how loan repayments get rounded. In plain terms, the balance after each payment would match the schedule, the update has been discribed in the official Github pull request.

After an overpayment, TotalValueOutstanding is reset to the scheduled value of the new PeriodicPayment.

Under the LendingProtocolV1_2 amendment, each on-time LoanPay would bring TotalValueOutstanding down to the scheduled value with one payment fewer, capped at the rounded PeriodicPayment.

The first commit moves the scheduled-value calculation out of computeLoanProperties into a separate scheduledValue function, with no behavior change.

That way, LoanSet and LoanPay round it the same way. Zero-interest loans also need a floor on the principal part, because their value is rounded to nearest while the principal is rounded up. Tests run on Legacy, CashBasis and FixedPrecision vaults, with and without the amendment.

Today, each payment's value part is calculated on its own, so rounding can build up across payments. The last payment can then need one unit more than the rounded PeriodicPayment, and the value parts can alternate between payments.

With the change, a method called telescoping makes the value part of every on-time payment either the floor or the ceiling of PeriodicPayment, and at least one unit. The last payment becomes exactly the rounded PeriodicPayment.

There's little to no effect for users and investors today. The extra unit on the final payment changes little. Older loans that already carried one unit above their schedule simply keep it, so the last payment still needs the PeriodicPayment and a single unit, unless the borrower overpays first.

Small overpayments are mostly ignored; the transaction goes through successfully and charges no extra XRP whatsoever.

Activation date is yet to be determined: 80% of validators agreement is needed for two weeks in a row for the amendment to go through. No public vote for LendingProtocolV1_2 has been announced yet, so the timeline remains uncertain.

XLM is trading back near levels seen before some of the recent institutional announcements.

The gap between network metrics and token price has drawn direct questions from observers: who is required to buy more XLM as tokenized assets and DEX volume grow, and what mechanism turns that activity into sustained demand for the native token?

Institutional products on the network often settle in other assets, and much of the on-chain trading runs through a small set of protocols whose fees and incentives do not automatically translate into broad XLM buying.

#4971. Hyperliquid Strategies seeks futures registration for perps

The HYPE treasury company spent $785 million on tokens during the quarter and wants to bring Hyperliquid-based perpetual futures to the US.

A subsidiary of Nasdaq-listed HYPE treasury company Hyperliquid Strategies has applied to become a futures commission merchant (FCM), seeking to bring Hyperliquid-based perpetual futures to the US.

“Owning HYPE is how we aim to capture economics associated with the Hyperliquid protocol; setting up an FCM is how we intend to help bring markets deployed on Hyperliquid onshore,” said Hyperliquid Strategies CEO David Schamis in the company’s results for the quarter ended Sept. 30, published on Thursday.

Hyperliquid’s HIP-3 framework allows independent operators to launch custom perpetual futures markets on the protocol’s onchain infrastructure by staking 500,000 HYPE tokens ($38.8 million). Proposed HIP-3 features would allow operators to create permissioned markets that restrict participation to approved addresses.

The subsidiary would handle US customer accounts for permissioned markets deployed on Hyperliquid, the company said. Both the FCM application and the proposed markets require regulatory approval.

Intercontinental Exchange, the parent company of the New York Stock Exchange, announced an agreement with Trade[XYZ] on Oct. 29 to bring Hyperliquid-based perpetual markets to the US, subject to regulatory approvals. ICE plans to operate a dedicated market and clearing structure.

Related: Hyperliquid, Pump.fun account for nearly 90% of record $638M crypto buybacks: FT

“Hyperliquid is winning the parts of onchain finance that generate real fees, and nearly all of that value flows back to HYPE holders through programmatic buybacks,” Schamis said.

#4970. Canadian Man Extradited to US Over Plot to Hijack University Supercomputers for Crypto

Canadian Man Extradited to US Over Plot to Hijack University Supercomputers for Crypto

A Canadian man has been extradited to the United States to face charges that he helped hack into university computers to steal login credentials and secretly mine cryptocurrency, the Justice Department said on Tuesday.

A federal grand jury in the Western District of New York indicted Ryan James Roach, 35, of Saskatchewan, and Mathiew James Stubbings, 41, of Ontario, on charges of conspiracy to obtain information from and damage a protected computer, obtaining information from a protected computer, and intentionally damaging a protected computer. The charges carry a maximum sentence of 10 years in prison and a $250,000 fine.

Canadian man extradited to the U.S. for his role in computer hacking scheme targeting universities... @FBIBuffalo @USDOJ_Intl https://t.co/vY8IacXdZZ pic.twitter.com/NsSZ5BIkIf

Between June 2016 and December 2019, the pair targeted U.S. educational institutions with supercomputers, researching security weaknesses in their networks before breaking in, according to the indictment. Once inside, they allegedly deployed malware, stole login credentials and forced the victims' computers to mine cryptocurrency for their own benefit.

Victims included institutions in New York, Tennessee, North Carolina and Massachusetts, among them a university in the Western District of New York, prosecutors said. The pair allegedly used virtual private servers in several countries, registered under false names, to send malware and mining software to victim computers and store stolen credentials, and routed attacks through computers compromised at other victims to hide their identities.

Roach was extradited from Canada on October 28 and arraigned in Rochester, New York, where he was held pending a detention hearing. Extradition proceedings against Stubbings are continuing in Canada.

The scheme is a form of cryptojacking, in which attackers use other people's computers to mine crypto while the victims bear the electricity costs and slower performance. The Justice Department did not say which cryptocurrency the pair allegedly mined.

But hackers looking to profit from stolen computing power may be turning elsewhere. In a report published Monday, Google's Threat Intelligence Group said it expects attackers to "increasingly prioritize LLMJacking over traditional resource theft like cryptojacking," hijacking companies' access to AI models and passing on the bills.

Unlike the gradual drain of cryptojacking, LLMjacking can run up huge cloud charges within hours, Google said. In incidents investigated by its Mandiant unit this year, fraudulent charges topped $100,000 in a single day, and some organizations racked up $1 million before access was cut off. Attackers are reselling stolen access through underground services that Google calls "LLM Mixers."

We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

An anonymous Tornado Cash whale sent $7 million to Hyperliquid to open a large long on Bitcoin this morning. According to Lookonchain and Onchain Lens, the wallet went in with aggressive 20x leverage with the plan to profit from Bitcoin crushing Ether.

Large holders usually run funds through mixers to move capital privately or to get ready to cash out. This case is unusual as the movement of these funds had just previously been tracked to a chain of addresses belonging to the hackers who drained the derivatives platform Drift Protocol of $290 million in April 2026.

Some blockchain researchers warn against jumping to conclusions about where the funds came from. There's still no direct evidence: Tornado Cash covers its tracks well, and the link between the wallet and specific hacks rests only on circumstantial matches in volume.

After starting as a brand-new address that withdrew 3,100 ETH from Tornado Cash, the unknown later swapped all of the Ether through CoW Swap on the spot, locking in $6.92 million in USDC. From there, the money went to the decentralized exchange Hyperliquid as collateral for future trades.

On the exchange, the whale didn't try to guess where the market was headed and just split the collateral in half, turned on 20x cross leverage and built a position worth about $26 million in total. The position consisted of $12.84 million at an entry price of $75,762.2 and an Ethereum short of 5,740 ETH worth $12.83 million.

So far, the bet is working. Bitcoin is holding near local highs, and the gain on the BTC long fully covers the drawdown from altcoin short. Add to this the earnings on funding, collecting a regular premium from other traders for holding the ETH short.

This is a textbook dominance pair trade, where the investor couldn't care less whether the industry as a whole goes up or down and the only thing that matters is the spread - the price gap between the two largest coins.

If Bitcoin rises faster than Ether, or if Ether falls harder than Bitcoin in a broad market crash, the trade profits.

The math is elegant, but 20x leverage is always a high risk. Should Ethereum rip higher while Bitcoin stays stuck in a range, the margin will be wiped out in seconds as Hyperliquid's cross-margin system will force-close the orders, and the investor will lose the entire $6.92 million. The liquidation price is set at $37,014.09 for BTC and $3,343.60 for ETH.

#4966. Binance Cuts Off SignalPlus API Keys Over 'Potential Security Issue' at Trading Platform

Binance Cuts Off SignalPlus API Keys Over 'Potential Security Issue' at Trading Platform

Binance has removed all API keys linked to SignalPlus, a third-party crypto trading platform, after SignalPlus reported a "potential security issue," the exchange said on Thursday.

"Binance's own systems are unaffected. User funds remain safe," the exchange said, describing the move as an "immediate precautionary measure."

Following a potential security issue reported by third-party platform SignalPlus, we have proactively removed all API keys linked to SignalPlus as an immediate precautionary measure.

Binance's own systems are unaffected. User funds remain safe.

If you used SignalPlus, please… pic.twitter.com/0HYYkTAW2p

API keys let third-party apps and trading tools connect to a user's exchange account and place trades on their behalf. Removing them cuts off that connection, meaning any automated strategies running on Binance through SignalPlus will stop until users set up new keys.

Binance urged SignalPlus users to log into their accounts directly to review their positions and open orders. It recommended that they wait until SignalPlus confirms the issue is fully resolved before reconnecting, and that they always enable IP whitelisting, which restricts an API key to approved internet addresses.

The exchange also called on all third-party platforms integrated with Binance to review their security controls, watch for unusual activity and contact it immediately if they spot any concerns.

SignalPlus, a Hong Kong-based platform that provides trading tools for crypto options and derivatives, connects to several exchanges, including Binance.

The incident comes a week after Binance was hit by the largest hack in crypto history, in which attackers drained about $6.4 billion from its hot wallets on October 29. The FBI has attributed the theft to North Korean hackers it calls TraderTraitor, a claim Pyongyang has denied.

Binance has said the attack began when a fake recruiter persuaded one of its engineers to download a malware-laced coding assignment, and that the attackers then used stolen credentials to reach an internal wallet signing service.

Binance said on Wednesday that it has replenished about 98% of the stolen assets from its own funds, and is still buying the rest, mostly less liquid tokens, through market and over-the-counter channels. It has also taken a new proof-of-reserves snapshot covering 44 tokens, up from 10, which users will be able to verify once its zero-knowledge proof is generated in about 50 to 60 hours.

The exchange is still restoring services. On Thursday, it began reopening withdrawals for more assets in phases, prioritizing tokens based on user demand over the past week. Deposits remain open.

Treasury says the network concealed ownership and sustained Prince Group operations, while US rules require covered assets to be blocked.

The US sanctioned four people and 41 entities on November 5, 2026, targeting a corporate network that Treasury says sustained Prince Group's operations after earlier sanctions. The designations subject covered assets to US blocking and reporting requirements, with restrictions also reaching companies that meet OFAC's ownership threshold.

The Office of Foreign Assets Control (OFAC) said the network helped Prince Group, which it has designated a transnational criminal organization, manage offshore structures, conceal beneficial ownership and maintain commercial activities. It links the group's affiliates to digital asset investment scams, romance scams and other online fraud, with many operations relying on trafficked and coerced workers in Southeast Asian compounds.

The action follows earlier Prince Group sanctions on October 14, 2025 and June 23, 2026. The newly sanctioned individuals are Qiu Yong, Zhao Mengyao, He Yujing and Visal Pang, according to the OFAC designation record.

Binance, world's biggest cryptocurrency exchange, officially reopened Hyperliquid and Aptos withdrawals, giving users a freeway to operate with HYPE and APT tokens. What lies behind the announcement is the conclusion of week-long retaliation with the HYPE community as holders of native Hyperliquid token needed to wait longer than others to be able to withdraw from Binance.

💁🏻‍♀️ Withdrawals have now been re-enabled for the following networks and assets: New: ✅ HYPE ✅ APT

The predecessor of this story is the biggest cyberattack in history when, according to the FBI and Chainalysis, the North Korean hacking group TraderTraitor emptied Binance's hot wallets for about $6.3 billion in a matter of 20 minutes. In the wake of the cybersecurity crisis the pause was still interpreted as corporate vengeance. This makes Yi He's words and the demonstrative relaunch of withdrawals a sign that obligations for clients take priority over market rivalry.

#4962. Zcash NU7 upgrade goes live, leaves 12,224 ZEC stranded in Sprout pool

Zcash’s NU7 upgrade has rendered about $15 million in legacy Sprout funds unspendable, although a future network upgrade could potentially restore access.

Zcash’s NU7 network upgrade activated Friday, making about $15 millionin ZEC held in the network’s legacy Sprout shielded pool unspendable.

The upgrade took effect at block 3,543,405 at 02:34 UTC, according to blockchain tracker ZecStats, which lists the Sprout pool balance at about 12,224 ZEC.

The upgrade cuts Zcash’s target time between blocks from 75 seconds to 25, although actual block intervals will adjust toward the new target as mining difficulty changes.

Under Zcash Improvement Proposal (ZIP) 2003, NU7 rejects version 4 transactions, the only format that supports spending Sprout funds. The remaining coins cannot be transferred under the new rules, but have not been burned.

The specification leaves open the possibility that a future network upgrade could restore access, either by re-enabling version 4 transactions or introducing another mechanism to retrieve the funds. However, it warns that “the ability to spend Sprout funds may never be re-enabled.”

NU7 also introduces a Network Sustainability Mechanism that removes 60% of transaction fees from circulation for eventual reissuance through future mining rewards, starting around February 2031.

The upgrade preserves Zcash’s 21 million ZEC supply cap and halving schedule, while introducing limits on shielded activity designed to reduce wallet synchronization demands.

#4961. Beyond Visa's Speed? XRP Expert Explains Bigger Scaling Opportunity Ahead

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

A discussion regarding the throughput of major blockchains emerged after an X user, 'Minus Wells,' claimed that 1,500 TPS is not the future of finance, not even 5,000, adding that this is roughly Visa's average throughput on a normal day.

"1,500 TPS is not the future of finance, not even 5,000. Sorry, they lied to you for engagement. That's roughly Visa's average throughput on a normal day. Congrats on matching the legacy system," Wells wrote.

It's a process. 1) Not every transaction needs to be settled in seconds immediately. Payment channels or higher layers like Dassie (Interledger) are much better suited for mass (micro) payments. MasterCard is looking to do more hackathons afaik with the XRP Ledger after the successful one at Swell leveraging this and AI. Batched settlement of institutions is good enough in the meantime to be very beneficial vs tradFi. Until: 2) I strongly believe we'll scale the L1s very sharply, not only XRPL, thanks to AI helping us to advance this tech even further. We are continuously pushing throughput higher and there is a lot of potential (!!!) for significantly more.

While Visa's network (VisaNet) has a technical capacity of 65,000 to over 83,000 transactions per second (TPS), its actual average volume runs at approximately 8,100 to 8,500 TPS. The post attracted attention in the XRP community given that the XRP Ledger sustains a throughput of 1,500 transactions per second (1,500 TPS) on commodity hardware.

Wells clarified he was not personally attacking XRP, claiming that XRPL, Avalanche, Stellar, Tezos L1, Cardano's Leios experiments, and OP Mainnet swap tests have all published throughput figures or results below 5,000 TPS. "Those aren't equivalent tests or necessarily current hard ceilings. But the question applies to everyone: can the infrastructure handle the demand future finance could bring?" he asked.

Hussein Zangana (Vet), XRP Ledger Foundation director of community, responded to claims regarding L1 blockchains' scalability with respect to XRP, saying "it's a process."

Vet made a case for the comparatively lower TPS of decentralized blockchains, noting that not every transaction needs to be settled in seconds. He pointed out that payment channels or higher layers like Dassie (Interledger) are much better suited for mass (micro) payments.

With respect to XRP, Vet noted that Mastercard is looking to do more hackathons with the XRP Ledger after a successful one at the Swell event while leveraging AI.

Batch, which allows multiple transactions to be bundled into a batch that is processed all together and was launched on the XRPL mainnet in October 2026, stands to benefit the XRP Ledger in this regard. "Batched settlement of institutions is good enough in the meantime to be very beneficial vs. TradFi," Vet added.

The XRP Ledger Foundation director of community highlighted massive scaling potential for the XRP Ledger and L1 blockchains.

"I strongly believe we'll scale the L1s very sharply, not only XRPL, thanks to AI helping us to advance this tech even further. We are continuously pushing throughput higher and there is a lot of potential (!!!) for significantly more," Vet wrote.

Bitget users in Thailand face a December 5, 2026 access block, with the country's Securities and Exchange Commission urging customers to consider action on assets held on the exchange before the restriction takes effect.

Thailand's SEC set out the deadline in its November 5, 2026 notice after filing a criminal complaint over alleged unlicensed exchange operations. It said the Ministry of Digital Economy and Society had been notified to block bitget.com, other Bitget domains and the exchange's application. The scheduled restriction concerns access from Thailand. SEC announcement

The SEC identified BG Limited as Bitget's platform provider. It alleged that the exchange charged trading fees and targeted Thai users during 2022-2025 through a Thai-language website, baht-value displays and Thai-language promotions.

The complaint also names Kritsana Kritsananuwat, whom the SEC accused of helping promote the exchange through Facebook and YouTube. The allegations remain subject to the criminal process. The regulator described investigation by inquiry officials, prosecution by public prosecutors and adjudication by the courts as subsequent steps.

The SEC has asked customers to consider their holdings before access is blocked. Its notice specifies no asset freeze, compulsory sale or separate withdrawal cutoff, leaving customers to decide how to handle assets on the platform ahead of the scheduled restriction.

#4958. Stablecoins Go Mainstream as Samsung Wallet Adds USDC on Solana

Stablecoins Go Mainstream as Samsung Wallet Adds USDC on Solana

Samsung is putting digital dollars in the same app where its users keep their credit cards and boarding passes.

Stablecoin accounts went live in Samsung Wallet on Thursday for eligible U.S. Galaxy users, letting them buy, hold, send, and receive Circle’s USDC on the Solana blockchain without downloading a separate crypto app or managing private keys. The feature reaches about 82 million compatible Galaxy devices in the U.S., according to the announcement.

“For a decade, stablecoins have proved the technology works,” said Lily Liu, president of the Solana Foundation. “What they lacked was a way to reach the mainstream.”

Samsung first announced the feature on Oct. 7, when the electronics giant tapped Solana for USDC transfers. Samsung Wallet first teased plans to support the stablecoin, which is pegged to the U.S. dollar, over the summer.

The product is built with Bastion, a licensed stablecoin custodian and infrastructure provider, and Coinbase, which safeguards the USDC as Bastion’s sub-custodian through its Coinbase Prime Vault. Samsung’s disclosures stress that the account is a crypto wallet, not a bank account.

Users can send USDC to other Samsung Wallet users in the U.S. and to compatible outside crypto wallets with no Samsung transfer fee, though other providers may charge one. They can also send money to qualifying bank accounts in more than 60 countries, where recipients may receive local currency, with fees that vary by destination.

Topping up with a credit card, including through Samsung Pay, carries a 4% fee, and withdrawals to a bank account or debit card also come with a fee. New users who make a first top-up of $50 or more can receive $25 in USDC. The service requires Android 13 or later and is open to U.S. residents 18 and older, except those in New York.

"Stablecoins have the potential to make moving money around the world faster and easier, and we want Galaxy users to be able to take advantage of that without having to navigate the complexity of traditional crypto tools,” said Woncheol Chai, who leads the digital wallet team in Samsung’s mobile business. “Solana helps us bring that experience to Samsung's scale."

Samsung said it expects to explore online and in-store stablecoin payments and to expand to additional markets, subject to local regulation.

#4957. 'We Have Big Plans for 2027': Ripple Engineering Director Teases XRP Ledger Overhaul

Ripple's director of engineering Viijay Khanna directly confirmed that the company is readying 'big plans' for XRP Ledger in 2027. The remark came amid the debates around the blockchain's speed, when critics put the XRP network's stats (around 1,500 transactions per second) against the average daily load of Visa, labeling the XRPL "an outdated copy of legacy payment systems".

"We have big plans for performance improvements lined up for 2027", said Khanna, effectively ending speculations.

Ripple's engineering department is aiming to exit the race for beautiful numbers behind the TPS metric. The logic is that the basic Layer-1 does not need to digest millions of small transactions directly. Retail microtraffic then goes to the second layer like Dassie (Interledger). The task of the main layer thus becomes maintaining large funds of banks and institutions.

The first step to this kind of independence from "heavy blockchain metal" was made this month with the fresh release of xrpld 3.3.0 which cut the appetite of nodes to RAM by 13-23%.

Technical audits show that the issue was not in simple code flaws, but the caprices of the system's allocator: the software was literally withholding the server's resources after the cache emptying. Bug fix therefore allowed validators to radically decrease infrastructure costs.

For further improvement of the current operational environment on November 4, the network got a fixCleanup package.

Khanna's short commentary is not an attempt to snuff out critics, but a marker of an ecosystem's overhaul for the needs of commercial banks. Ripple's president Monica Long has already announced a plan to relay the big chunk of the company's clients' payments directly on XRP Ledger. Cross-border remittances will be tied to new lending infrastructure as well.

The overhaul of XRP Ledger is a preventive hit on the network's future overload. Ripple ecosystem builders rewrite the basis, using AI teams for strict testing and code optimization. XRP Ledger is being readied for the moment when transactions with RLUSD stablecoin, tokenized stocks trading, and interbank lending become a routine and not just pilot projects.

#4956. Dogecoin Cofounder Highlights Shiba Inu Popularity in Japan

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

Dogecoin cofounder Billy Markus shared a lighthearted observation about Japan's unique dog culture in a post on X, pointing out the popularity of Shiba Inu dogs in Japan.

"Like, 50% of the dogs I see in Japan are Shiba Inu," wrote Markus, who goes by the online handle Shibetoshi Nakamoto.

like 50% of the dogs i see in japan are shiba inu i love japan

The Dogecoin cofounder's comment is based on his own observation when he was in Japan, where the Shiba Inu breed has deep cultural roots and is still one of the country's most recognizable native dog breeds. Markus' observation is noteworthy, as he is the cofounder of Dogecoin, a cryptocurrency that draws its origins from the Shiba Inu dog.

On December 6, 2013, Dogecoin was launched, taking influence from the famous Doge meme: an image of a very adorable Shiba Inu dog with multi-colored Comic Sans text that appears as the dog's "thought bubbles."

This famous meme was based on a 2010 photograph of a Japanese female Shiba Inu dog, Kabosu, adopted by Japanese kindergarten teacher Atsuko Satō from an animal shelter in 2008. This helped establish the dog-themed cryptocurrency trend that later inspired several other tokens, including Shiba Inu (SHIB).

Kabosu, a Japanese Shiba Inu, inspired a generation of online jokes and became the face of Dogecoin cryptocurrency. Kabosu was the face of the breezy Doge meme, her quizzical expression surrounded by an inner monologue of choppy phrases in bad grammar written in rainbow-colored Comic Sans font.

In 2010, two years after adopting Kabosu from a puppy mill where she would otherwise have been put down, Ms. Satō, a teacher from Sakura, east of Tokyo, took a picture of her pet crossing her paws on the sofa. She posted the image on her blog, from where it spread to online forum Reddit and became a meme that bounced from college bedrooms to office email chains.

This inspired Dogecoin, which was started as a joke by two software engineers, Billy Markus and Jackson Palmer, and is now the 12th largest cryptocurrency, with a market capitalization of $11.17 billion, according to CoinMarketCap data.

Kabosu, the dog behind the "doge" meme, died in May 2024 after 14 years of internet fame.

#4955. Best Crypto-Friendly Banks: 5 Options for Crypto Investors and Businesses

Home » Cryptopedia » Best Crypto-Friendly Banks: 5 Options for Crypto Investors and Businesses

Which banks will support your crypto habits?

Finding a crypto-friendly bank in 2026 is not as simple as opening an account with a traditional financial institution. Some banks allow transfers to cryptocurrency exchanges like Coinbase, while others offer built-in crypto trading, digital asset custody, or specialized banking services for Web3 businesses.

The best option depends on where you live, whether you are an individual investor or a crypto business, and which digital asset services you need. Some providers also impose restrictions on specific exchanges, transaction limits, or crypto-related business activities.

In this guide, we compare crypto-friendly banks and fintech providers, examining their supported services, availability, fees, and limitations to help you find the right banking solution in 2026.

A crypto-friendly bank makes it easier for individuals and businesses to interact with the cryptocurrency market through traditional financial services. Depending on the provider, this may include allowing transfers to and from crypto exchanges, offering crypto trading or payment features, or providing banking services to eligible crypto companies.

Not all crypto-friendly providers offer the same features. Some let customers buy, sell, and hold digital assets directly, while others simply support transfers to selected platforms like Kraken or provide financial services to Web3 businesses. Some are licensed banks, while others are fintech companies that partner with banks to deliver their services.

Before exploring the best crypto-friendly banks, consider the main advantages and potential drawbacks.

If you’re looking for the best crypto-friendly bank to join in 2023, you’re in the right place! This list breaks down some of the top banks for cryptocurrency from a variety of fields, including traditional banking, business banking, and personal accounts.

Revolut remains one of the more accessible options for people who want to manage traditional money and cryptocurrency through a single financial app.

Revolut is a digital banking platform that combines traditional financial services with cryptocurrency features. In supported markets, customers can use its app to buy, sell, hold, and transfer cryptocurrencies, including Bitcoin and Ethereum. Available assets and features depend on the customer’s country and account eligibility.

Revolut also supports crypto withdrawals and deposits through selected external wallets and blockchain networks. However, transaction fees, supported assets, and withdrawal limits vary. Its cryptocurrency services are provided by separate legal entities from its banking services, and crypto holdings are not covered by standard bank deposit protection.

Wirex flips the script amongst the other ‘best crypto-friendly banks’ contenders. While other platforms began their story as traditional banks and slowly moved to cryptocurrency acceptance, Wirex set out to be the world’s leading crypto bank from day one.

Wirex is a global fintech platform combining traditional currencies, cryptocurrency services, and payment cards. Users in eligible markets can buy, sell, exchange, and hold supported digital assets, as well as use available payment services to spend their funds.

Wirex also hosts a cross-chain bridge, offers crypto-linked cards, and a Cryptoback rewards programme, which advertises rewards of up to 8% on eligible purchases.

Actual rewards depend on the card programme, subscription, and applicable conditions. Wirex is not a traditional bank; its services are provided through regulated entities and financial partners, depending on the customer’s location.

Mercury is a US-based financial technology company providing business banking services through partner banks. It is particularly relevant to eligible cryptocurrency and Web3 startups, DAOs, and investment funds that need traditional financial infrastructure to manage their operations.

Businesses can use Mercury to manage fiat deposits, payments, expenses, and transfers to supported cryptocurrency exchanges, including Coinbase and Gemini. However, Mercury does not hold cryptocurrencies or provide crypto trading services.

It supports most eligible US-registered businesses, including those founded by international entrepreneurs, but does not accept applications from crypto exchanges or money services businesses.

Monzo is a UK-based digital bank that allows customers to use their accounts with selected cryptocurrency exchanges.

Although it does not offer native cryptocurrency trading, customers can transfer money to supported platforms, including Coinbase, making it an option for investors who want to manage traditional banking and crypto purchases separately.

Monzo applies transaction monitoring and restrictions to cryptocurrency-related payments. For example, it currently blocks payments to Binance while allowing transfers to a range of other exchanges. Its cryptocurrency payment allowance is also limited, so customers should check the latest terms before making large transactions.

Ally Bank is a US-based digital bank offering traditional banking products, including checking accounts, savings accounts, and investment services. Unlike specialist crypto-friendly financial platforms, Ally does not provide direct cryptocurrency trading or cryptocurrency custody through its banking services.

Customers cannot transfer cryptocurrencies directly to Ally accounts, which accept transactions in US dollars. Ally Invest also does not currently support direct cryptocurrency trading, although customers can access certain crypto-related investment products, including cryptocurrency funds and publicly traded companies involved in the industry.

Ally is therefore better suited to customers looking for conventional banking and investment services rather than direct access to cryptocurrency exchanges or digital assets.

Choosing a crypto-friendly bank involves more than finding a financial institution that allows cryptocurrency transactions. The right option depends on your location, investment activities, and whether you need basic exchange transfers or more advanced crypto-related services.

Here are five important factors to consider:

Check whether the bank supports transfers to your preferred cryptocurrency exchanges, such as Coinbase, Kraken, or Binance. Some financial institutions restrict payments to specific platforms or apply additional security checks to crypto-related transactions.

Compare account maintenance fees, international transfer costs, currency conversion charges, and any limits on cryptocurrency-related payments. Some providers also charge separate fees for buying, selling, or withdrawing digital assets.

Look for providers with appropriate regulatory authorisation and transparent security policies. Check whether eligible fiat deposits are covered by a recognised deposit guarantee scheme and understand that cryptocurrency holdings generally receive different protections.

Crypto-related banking services are not available everywhere. Before opening an account, check whether the provider accepts customers in your country and whether its cryptocurrency features are accessible in your region.

If you operate a Web3 startup, blockchain company, or cryptocurrency-related business, check the provider’s business eligibility requirements. Some platforms, including Mercury, support eligible technology and Web3 companies but exclude certain activities, such as operating a cryptocurrency exchange or money services business.

Crypto-friendly banks and financial platforms make it easier to manage traditional money and cryptocurrency in one place. However, their safety depends on regulation, banking partnerships, and security measures.

An important distinction is that eligible fiat deposits may be protected by deposit guarantee schemes, while cryptocurrencies generally are not. Crypto assets also carry risks such as market volatility, custody issues, and potential losses.

Not every crypto-friendly platform is a licensed bank. Providers such as Wirex and Mercury may operate through regulated entities or partner banks. Before choosing one, check its licences, account terms, and what protections actually apply to your funds.

Several financial institutions and fintech platforms support cryptocurrency-related transactions. Revolut and Wirex offer crypto services in eligible markets, while Monzo supports transfers to selected cryptocurrency exchanges. Mercury provides banking services to eligible Web3 businesses, whereas Ally Bank primarily offers traditional banking and investment products.

The best option depends on your needs. Revolut is suitable for individuals seeking integrated crypto services, Wirex focuses on cryptocurrency payments and spending, and Mercury caters to eligible Web3 businesses. Monzo can be useful for UK customers transferring money to supported exchanges.

Yes, many banks and financial platforms allow transfers to Coinbase, although availability depends on the customer’s location, payment method, and account restrictions. Always check your bank’s current cryptocurrency payment policy and Coinbase’s supported funding methods before initiating a transfer.

Yes. Banks can restrict or decline cryptocurrency-related payments for security, fraud prevention, regulatory compliance, or internal risk-management reasons. Some institutions also block transactions involving specific exchanges. Customers should review their provider’s terms and conditions before making crypto-related transfers.

Eligible deposits held with licensed banks may be covered by national deposit protection schemes, depending on the institution, account type, and applicable limits. However, cryptocurrency holdings are generally not covered by traditional bank deposit insurance. Fintech platforms may also use partner banks, so customers should identify the institution actually holding their deposits and verify the applicable protection.

#4954. Gold is still 25% below its peak even as demand reaches $188B in Q3

October's losses show why knowing who's buying gold is only part of understanding its price.

Central banks bought more gold in the third quarter of 2026 than consumers worldwide bought for jewelry.

According to the World Gold Council's latest demand report, central banks and other official institutions acquired an estimated net 321 metric tons, while jewelry consumption came to 313 tons.

In the same quarter a year earlier, jewelry buyers had consumed about 150 tons more than central banks acquired.

By September, the institutions buying gold for their reserves had overtaken the people buying it to wear, although roughly 63% of estimated official demand couldn't be traced through publicly reported country-level purchases.

The price barely kept pace with Bitcoin, which gained 42.7% during the quarter versus gold's 3.84%. Both attracted money through exchange-traded funds, so this wasn't a straightforward case of investors ditching one for the other. They bought both and got different results.

However, Bitcoin's lead came crashing down in October: gold fell 6.3%, while Bitcoin lost 7.3%. Governments kept accumulating bullion, and investors kept believing in scarce assets, but neither belief did much for that month's returns.

The buyers behind those beliefs, and their reasons for holding on, explain rather more than the safe-haven label does.

Global gold demand reached $188 billion in the third quarter, up 22% from a year earlier. That sounds like an impressive shopping spree until you weigh the purchases.

Buyers took home 1% less metal, at 1,369 tons, while the average quarterly price was 23% higher. Much of the extra spending covered the price increase rather than buying more gold.

That was especially apparent in jewelry, where consumption fell 17% to 313 tons, the weakest third quarter in the council's records since 2000. Buyers in China and India cut purchases or chose lighter and lower-carat pieces, adjusting what they bought to what they could afford.

Physical investment wasn't immune either: demand for bars and coins fell 19% to 268 tons. Paying more per ounce didn't make every buyer more eager to own one.

Central banks' net purchases rose 42% to 321 tons, while gold-backed ETFs added 211 tons, reversing the previous quarter's withdrawals.

Those purchases helped offset weaker demand from jewelry buyers and physical investors, giving the market support from buyers who wanted gold as a financial holding even as others balked at the cost.

The categories don't divide neatly into institutions buying and households retreating. Individuals own gold through ETFs, and institutions can own bars and coins.

But the numbers do show that demand weakened in traditional consumer and physical-investment markets, while official reserves and listed funds absorbed more metal. Gold was becoming a harder purchase to justify at the counter and an easier allocation to defend in a portfolio.

Of the estimated 321 tons in net purchases, only about 120 tons appeared in publicly reported country-level activity.

China disclosed additions of 63 tons and Poland 40 tons, making them the largest reported buyers, but the country disclosures leave approximately 201 tons of the council's estimate unaccounted for in the public figures.

Reporting delays and purchases governments don't disclose can explain activity missing from the country data, and the broader estimate may be revised. The gap alone also doesn't establish secret, coordinated purchases.

We have a much better estimate of the total than a complete account of who bought it, which limits how confidently we can interpret the buying.

Q3 2026 net official demand, using rounded inputs: 321 metric tons estimated in total, of which 120 were publicly reported. The remaining 201 are calculated by subtraction, not added to the total. Unreported activity can include delayed or undisclosed purchases; estimates may be revised.

Source: World Gold Council, Q3 2026 central-bank demand

Central banks acquired an estimated 675 tons in the first nine months of 2026, exceeding their purchases over the same period of 2025.

Their appetite is large enough to offset weakness elsewhere, but incomplete disclosures make it harder to attribute that appetite to particular governments or motives. The survey evidence offers more detail on the latter, with some important limits of its own.

Quarterly totals. Jewelry measures consumption; bars and coins, ETFs, and official institutions measure net demand. Negative ETF values mean net withdrawals. Official institutions include central banks. These selected channels do not exhaust total gold demand and do not separate household from institutional investors.

Source: World Gold Council / Metals Focus / Refinitiv GFMS, Q3 2026, Table 1

In the World Gold Council's 2026 central-bank survey, 74% of respondents expected the US dollar's share of global reserves to be lower in five years. That gives some basis for the argument that reserve managers want less dependence on the dollar.

The IMF's second-quarter foreign-exchange reserve data put the dollar's share at 56.7%, down from 57.18% in the preceding quarter. It remained the dominant reserve currency, with dollar holdings largely steady as holdings in other currencies expanded.

This series also excludes monetary gold, so it can't tell us how much gold replaced dollars.

Even the funding of gold purchases is less straightforward than the familiar sell-Treasuries-buy-bullion argument allows. Half the survey respondents said they'd finance new gold purchases through domestic programs using local currency, while 38% said they'd sell existing reserve assets.

Buying locally produced gold with local currency doesn't require a corresponding sale of US government debt, and the quarterly demand totals can't identify what financed every purchase.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

Reserve managers can want more gold without wanting out of everything else. Bullion gives them an asset whose value doesn't depend on an issuer honoring a financial obligation, making it a way to reduce reliance on other governments' currencies and debt.

However, the bargain still includes a market price, and that price can fall enough to make the word “protection” feel rather generous over shorter periods.

Gold finished October at $3,913 an ounce, down 6.3% for the month and roughly 25% below its January peak, according to the World Gold Council's November market commentary.

Central bank demand hadn't spared holders that loss, because even a persistent buyer can't dictate the price everyone else trades at. Futures positions and the need to raise cash can overwhelm the reassurance of a government adding bullion to its reserves.

Global gold-backed ETFs attracted $2.7 billion in net inflows during October, adding 19 tons and taking holdings to a record 4,275 tons. Their combined assets under management still fell 6% to $542 billion, as the lower gold price more than offset the new money.

The funds held more metal and were worth less, an entirely possible outcome that gets lost when inflows are treated as a verdict on returns.

The broader point is consistent with a July 2026 IMF study, which found that gold's ability to hedge market risk depends on the shock and the economic conditions around it. Gold can help diversify a reserve portfolio over time without reliably sheltering it during extreme stress.

Someone reducing exposure to sovereign credit over several years and someone trying to avoid a loss this month may buy the same asset, but they're asking it to do different jobs.

Bitcoin gave investors a much better third quarter, gaining 42.7% to end September around $83,570, according to 21Shares' quarterly analysis.

US spot Bitcoin ETFs added about 79,800 BTC, worth about $6.2 billion, reversing roughly $4.8 billion in second-quarter withdrawals.

Their purchases were nearly twice the estimated 41,300 BTC newly mined over the period, giving the rally a measurable source of demand through funds available to professional and individual investors alike.

That comparison with new supply is striking, although newly mined coins are only one source of Bitcoin available to buy. Existing holders own a far larger stock, and some were reducing their positions while ETFs accumulated.

21Shares estimated that balances in the long-term-holder cohort fell by 248,000 BTC during the quarter. The figure isn't a direct count of exchange sales, since coins can leave the cohort through transfers or other classification effects, but it prevents ETF buying from being mistaken for a market in which everyone was adding to their holdings.

Global gold-backed funds attracted a record $31 billion in third-quarter inflows and added 211 tons of bullion, even as the metal's price gain fell well short of Bitcoin's.

The dollar totals cover different markets: the gold figure is global, while the Bitcoin figure covers US spot ETFs. Their respective market sizes and investor bases differ too, so treating the totals as a head-to-head contest would tell us little.

Investors added exposure to both assets, but their quarterly returns differed by nearly 39%, which offers little support for a simple account of Bitcoin rallying because money was leaving gold. It also limits what ETF data alone can explain.

Listed funds supplied demand in both markets, while trading elsewhere, including derivatives positions and borrowed money, helped determine how far each price moved.

October narrowed that enormous performance gap in an unwelcome way. Bitcoin fell 7.3% to about $77,500, while gold lost 6.3% over the same month.

Reported October 2026 returns in US dollars. Gold uses the LBMA PM benchmark through Oct. 30, the last business day; Bitcoin trades continuously. These are price returns, excluding fees and income, rather than ETF returns. Similar losses in one month do not establish a lasting correlation or a common cause.

Source: World Gold Council / Bloomberg / LBMA, October 2026, Table 1 · 21Shares, November 2026 Bitcoin outlook

Gold ETFs continued to attract net investment globally, led by Europe and Asia, while North American funds recorded modest withdrawals. On Nov. 4, Reuters reported that the largest Bitcoin-backed funds tracked by LSEG/Lipper lost $215 million in October.

While the totals don't establish that investors moved money from Bitcoin to gold, they show that net buying persisted across global bullion funds while the tracked Bitcoin funds lost money to withdrawals.

The World Gold Council pointed to reductions in speculative positions, revised expectations for Federal Reserve policy, and rotation between assets as possible contributors to gold's decline.

Bitcoin contended with weaker tech stocks, tighter credit conditions, the Binance hack, and the US midterm elections. Different sellers had different reasons to act, even though holders of both assets ended the month with losses.

Scarcity offers little defense against someone who needs cash or wants less exposure to volatile markets. Central-bank purchases can absorb gold without guaranteeing its price, just as Bitcoin's supply limit can constrain issuance without guaranteeing buyers at the price its holders would prefer.

October put both propositions on display, even while some investors continued to add bullion to their portfolios.

The sovereign comparison needs a careful approach, because a government owning Bitcoin doesn't necessarily mean it has entered the market to buy it.

The US established a Strategic Bitcoin Reserve in March 2025, formally giving Bitcoin a place among assets the federal government can retain and manage.

However, the executive order creating the reserve directed that it be funded primarily with Bitcoin already held through criminal or civil asset forfeitures.

Officials were also authorized to devise budget-neutral ways to acquire additional Bitcoin without incremental taxpayer costs. That permission and the decision to retain existing coins have policy importance, but neither establishes a recurring purchase program comparable with central banks adding gold to their reserves.

That's why putting seized Bitcoin into a reserve can happen without buying a single additional coin on the market.

The World Gold Council's quarterly figures estimate net additions to official gold holdings. The US reserve order governs, in large part, what happens to assets the government already owns.

Gold already has an established role in central bank balance sheets and reserve management. Bitcoin's newer sovereign role could evolve, but ETF access, government recognition, and official purchases each affect demand through a different route.

Investors can't assume progress on one will produce the buying associated with another, however attractive the reserve announcement sounds.

Interest-rate expectations and access to cash will continue to test both markets. Stronger US employment or inflation readings can encourage expectations of tighter Federal Reserve policy, increasing the appeal of interest-bearing assets relative to gold and potentially making financial conditions less accommodating for Bitcoin.

Weaker readings can support expectations of easier policy, although a deterioration severe enough to prompt fears of an economic slowdown can also leave investors wanting cash.

Neither the gold-demand report nor Bitcoin's ETF inflows can settle how investors will react to the next release. They can tell us more about who has been buying and how readily those buyers might leave.

Central banks can accumulate gold over several years, while ETF investors can cut exposure much faster. Consumers can buy lighter jewelry, and traders using borrowed money can face demands to reduce positions precisely when they'd prefer to wait.

That mix helps explain why persistent belief in an asset can coexist with a sharp fall in its price.

The reserve manager diversifying away from another government's obligations may have little reason to sell bullion during a correction, while an investor facing losses elsewhere may sell the same metal because it's readily tradable.

Bitcoin's fixed supply doesn't remove that need for cash, and official recognition doesn't give its holders the resources or mandate to ride out every downturn.

Central banks overtaking jewelry buyers shows how gold demand is being redistributed, and October's losses show the limits of what those purchases can promise other holders.

Someone buying for protection needs the asset's behavior to fit the risk they're trying to reduce and the period they can afford to hold it.

Gold can serve a reserve manager well over years and disappoint an investor over weeks, while Bitcoin can deliver a spectacular quarter without becoming a dependable place to wait out the next sell-off.

#4953. Vitalik Buterin Declares Ethereum (ETH) a Store of Value, Not Fee-Burning Token

Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

Ethereum's intrinsic economics is rebuilding itself in motion with tech bugs and the macroeconomic picture overlaying each other in the span of 48 hours. The week wraps up for a major alternative crypto project with a sudden Glamsterdam hard fork stop after testnet launch revealed a dangerous bug in the fee recalculating mechanism, brutally breaking the logic in smart contracts.

As the Ethereum community debated the ability of the network to sustain on old power, Vitalik Buterin showed up with a manifesto which completely zeroes out all the panic around mainnet's decreasing revenue. The "red line" behind Vitalik's updated vision is that the community should stop being fixated on the idea of ETH as "fee-burninng token". For a basic blockchain layer worth $270 billion, attempts to build evaluation on the amount of gas burned are a dead end. For Vitalik, the novel of "ETH as store of value (SoV)" is much more viable amid all the global uncertainty around.

This U-turn, taken by a key visionary of the ecosystem, happened at $2,240 for ETH and may drastically change all the plans developed with a focus on Glamsterdam architecture. The hard fork was initially projected for the logic of blockspace surplus, not the deficit. Ecosystem builders are already readying expansion of L1 capacity with the help of EIP-8037 (tariffs calibration) and EIP-7928 (parallel production) with an aim to raise gas limits up to 200 million units. The logic is that the excess of free space will cheapen the transactions, while token burning rates will gradually be cut.

The market saw a risk of deflation in this concept. Vitalik took a front-step. By his estimates, the historical peaks of burning through EIP-1559 "never were as strong as they seemed". They were dependent on inner cyclical activity of crypto speculations. And it itself was falling once every bear market started.

EIP-1559 is not going anywhere, and Vitalik openly says "don't be sad" if the volume of gas burned will forever stay below the 10% mark. The source of value for Ethereum lies in completely another dimension.

Solana's co-founder, Anatoly Yakovenko, unexpectedly aligned with Buterin on this matter. "You can't focus on fees as the products", he said, calling such an idea "absolutely meaningless". According to Yakovenko, the blockchain network must generate real value for the end users, not control the price of getting into the block.

Vitalik is absolutely right that you can’t focus on fees as the product. That’s absolutely meaningless. You need to pick a product, the thing that brings value to people, and do that really well.

There is lots of technical stuff and philosophy around, but what the missed deadline on the testnet and Vitalik's manifesto reveal is that Ethereum opts for long-term, secure, and even a bit boring stability of the basic layer. Infra doesn't try to flirt with the market through deflationary records.

#4952. Binance’s $6.3B hack leaves questions over hot wallet safeguards

Experts say blockchain records show repeated wallet drains but cannot establish Binance’s internal signing controls or transaction limits.

Binance’s $6.3 billion security incident has left unanswered questions about how the exchange managed its hot wallets and limited the amount attackers could access.

Binance co-CEO Richard Teng said on Oct. 30 that the attack affected the exchange’s own hot wallet. Most customer assets were held in offline cold storage and moved to hot wallets only when users requested withdrawals.

Wallet balances offer only a partial picture of an exchange’s exposure to an attack. Public blockchain records can show funds moving but cannot establish the internal controls governing those transfers, CertiK analyst Jonathan Riss told Cointelegraph.

In its initial report Tuesday, Binance said attackers accessed an internal wallet-signing service to move funds outside its normal withdrawal process. Binance said some technical details would remain confidential to prevent further attacks.

The exchange did not respond to Cointelegraph’s questions about its wallet balances and safeguards.

Rivals including Coinbase, Gate and Upbit have disclosed their cold-storage ratios following the attack.

Riss said the $6.3 billion figure represented the cumulative value of assets affected across 10 blockchains. On Ethereum, attackers repeatedly drained a hot wallet as new deposits arrived.

When attackers can obtain valid signatures, their potential access depends on what the signing system allows them to transfer over time, he said. A wallet’s balance at a single moment does not capture that exposure.

Related: EIP-7702 didn’t cause Binance hack, but may have amplified losses

Transaction limits enforced by the application requesting a signature can be bypassed if that application is compromised, Riss said. Limits enforced independently by the signing system may still restrict transfers.

“A comparison with other exchanges isn’t really possible from the outside,” Riss added. “Public information on internal signing architecture is limited everywhere, and reserve distribution and proof-of-reserves attestations show what sits where, not how a withdrawal is authorized.”

BitGo chief product officer Eugene Hahr also declined to speculate about Binance’s internal safeguards.

“The breakdown of custody arrangements is a question Binance is best placed to answer,” he said.

Idan Ofrat, co-founder and chief product officer at Fireblocks, said exchanges typically keep enough funds in hot wallets to cover a few days of expected withdrawals, often representing a low single-digit percentage of assets under management.

Exchanges generally hold the bulk of customer assets in cold storage, while some use intermediate wallets that require manual approval for transfers to hot wallets, he said.

Related: Binance traces $6.3B hack to fake job interviews, compromised AI tool

Alex Tobol, chief technology officer at Telegram-based crypto wallet Walt, said holding billions of dollars in hot wallets is not inherently unusual for an exchange of Binance’s size.

Hot wallets generally process withdrawals automatically, Tobol said, but replenishing them from cold storage should require a separate treasury decision and manual approval.

Hahr said controls applied before signing, including transaction limits, approved withdrawal addresses and additional authorization requirements, can help contain losses when a system is compromised.

Magazine: Binance hackers raced to move billions before it could be frozen

#4951. Morning Minute: Democrat Sweep Pumps Crypto as Clarity Act Resurfaces

Morning Minute: Democrat Sweep Pumps Crypto as Clarity Act Resurfaces

Morning Minute is a daily newsletter written by Tyler Warner. The analysis and opinions expressed are his own and do not necessarily reflect those of Decrypt. And check out our daily news show 'FOMO HOUR' covering all of the top stories and market action.

🗳️ Democrat Sweep Pumps Crypto as Market Structure Bill Resurfaces

Bitcoin spent Tuesday night under $76,000, a seven-week low, with traders bracing for whatever a Democratic Congress meant for crypto. By Wednesday afternoon it was back above $80,800, up 4% on the day.

The easy story is that uncertainty lifted and crypto exhaled. And that could be part of it. But oil helped. WTI fell 9% to $88 a barrel, down almost 17% on the week, as Iran’s parliament speaker said voters had “said NO TO WAR.” The DOW jumped 3% on the day and Nasdaq +1.6% in what was a huge green day across markets.

James Butterfill of CoinShares put it plainly: “For Bitcoin, real yields, dollar liquidity and ETP flows still carry more weight than the elections.”

But the policy news that landed Wednesday afternoon matters more than the candles.

Eleanor Terrett reported that House Democrats are discussing expanding Maxine Waters’ Crypto Investor and Consumer Bill of Rights, originally an amendment to the CLARITY Act in 2025, into a full market structure bill next Congress. The draft would fold in Democratic CLARITY provisions that the House rejected last year, plus provisions Senate Democrats and Republicans had already agreed on before the September cloture vote that killed the bill. Three industry sources described the talks to her.

That’s a genuine surprise. Twenty-four hours ago the consensus was that market structure legislation was dead for two years. Waters is the likely incoming chair of House Financial Services, and she is apparently planning to write a bill rather than hold hearings. But the bill will have some changes.

The new proposals under discussion would give the SEC a larger role supervising crypto markets and leave the CFTC with a smaller one. The draft is expected to carry ethics provisions restricting the crypto activities of Trump and other federal officials. Those provisions are what Democrats wanted all along, and what many thought would kill any chances the bill has of getting signed.

So one of the first courses of action from this Democrat-led Congress is to enact some reasonable changes to the Clarity Act to try to get it passed in 2027. That feels like a very bullish first step.

#4950. Flare CEO Pushes Back Against XRP 'FUD'

Flare CEO Hugo Philion has expressed his frustration about recent fear, uncertainty, and doubt (FUD) around XRP and reiterated that the future of the cryptocurrency should not be centered solely around Ripple.

The Flare CEO recently addressed in an X post the constant habit of XRP holders to evaluate the future of the cryptocurrency through the prism of what Ripple is doing and what it isn't.

"The value and narrative of XRP should never have been viewed as being dependent on the actions of a single actor," the Flare CEO said.

The Flare CEO emphasized that XRP holders should instead concentrate their efforts on finding ways to improve the usability of the cryptocurrency.

In this context, Philion said that XRP holders need to consider three main questions.

The first question is what XRP holders are actually doing to utilize their XRP holdings. The second is how they can convince potential investors to hold XRP.

Finally, the third point that the Flare CEO mentioned is that the XRP community should find a way to transform XRP from a predominantly speculative asset into usable capital.

"And that's part of the reason we created Flare because we feel like there's always been more that can and should be done with XRP," the Flare CEO added.

Besides, the Flare CEO also invited XRP holders to try Flare. According to Philion, it's a way to make XRP less dependent on Ripple.

The Flare CEO's statements come amid increasing frustration around XRP and the lackluster performance of the cryptocurrency.

Over the past year, the price of XRP has gone down by around 50%, and at press time, the price of the cryptocurrency stands at $1.16.

Recently, Ripple's former chief technology officer David Schwartz also faced some criticism over the poor performance of XRP. Schwartz noted that his influence on the XRP price was very limited.

One X user raised a question about whether FLR holders are doing enough to demonstrate the value of the cryptocurrency, especially when it's possible for XRP holders to use Flare's decentralized finance ecosystem without purchasing FLR.

Still, Philion dismissed the claim that there is no need for FLR when using FXRP (a tokenized version of XRP on Flare).

"It's incorrect btw that you can use FXRP without needing FLR," the Flare CEO clarified.

According to Philion, what Flare Smart Accounts do is simply abstract away the need to manage FLR and make the experience more convenient. At the same time, FLR is used to pay transaction fees on the underlying networks.

Besides, the Flare CEO also underlined that in the long term, there should be more use cases for the token apart from transaction fees.

"Usage is important. Need to get fee generation going in a broader way in the ecosystem," he added.
