#4919. Rule to Allow Crypto in 401(k)s Arrives at White House for Final Review

A final DOL rule that would ease alternative assets such as private equity, real estate and cryptocurrency into 401(k) retirement plans arrived at the White House on Thursday for review, Bloomberg Law reported, a procedural step that usually precedes public release.

The Office of Information and Regulatory Affairs logged the package on Nov. 5 under the title “Fiduciary Duties In Selecting Designated Investment Alternatives,” RIN 1210-AC38. The filing lists the DOL’s Employee Benefits Security Administration as the authoring office, marks the stage as a final rule, flags it as economically significant and notes that there is no legal deadline for completion.

The DOL unveiled the proposal on March 30. In that draft, plan fiduciaries who follow a documented process when adding private assets to a 401(k) menu would get a legal safe harbor under the Employee Retirement Income Security Act of 1974.

The six factors named in the proposal are performance, fees, liquidity, valuation, performance benchmarks and complexity. Satisfying them does not force plans to offer crypto. It lowers the litigation risk for employers that decide they want to.

JUST IN: 🇺🇸 The White House is now reviewing a new rule which will allow Americans to invest in crypto through their 401(k) retirement plans 👀 pic.twitter.com/uXGGIXEzdC

President Donald Trump’s Aug. 7, 2025, executive order, “Democratizing Access to Alternative Assets for 401(k) Investors,” directed the Labor secretary to reexamine ERISA guidance and, where appropriate, propose rules with “appropriately calibrated safe harbors.” The order defined alternative assets to include private-market equity and debt, real estate, holdings in actively managed vehicles that invest in digital assets, commodities, infrastructure and lifetime-income strategies.

The March proposal implements that order. A DOL fact sheet said prudence under ERISA is process-based and that fiduciaries retain discretion to select any designated investment alternative if they follow the outlined review. Deputy Labor Secretary Keith Sonderling cast the approach as neutral: “The department’s days of picking winners and losers are over.”

That stance reverses a prior chill. In May 2025 the DOL rescinded its March 2022 compliance release that told fiduciaries to use “extreme care” before putting cryptocurrency on a 401(k) menu. The department said that phrase was not found in ERISA and restored a context-specific standard.

Later, it was reported that Sens. Bernie Sanders and Elizabeth Warren pressed the DOL to abandon the crypto-friendly path. House Republicans have separately pushed to codify Trump’s crypto order in statute.

OIRA review under Executive Order 12866 is the last interagency checkpoint before a final rule can be published in the Federal Register. Bloomberg Law said the arrival signals the package is nearing completion. The same office cleared the proposed version in late March before the DOL published it; that earlier pass took roughly two months after the January submission, per prior Bloomberg coverage of the roughly $12 trillion 401(k) market.

What is not yet public is the final text itself. Whether the six-factor safe harbor survived comment intact, how digital-asset vehicles are described, and what effective date the DOL chose will only be clear when OIRA returns the rule and the department posts it. Comment volume on the proposal was heavy; industry and labor groups have argued opposite sides of the prudence standard for months.

For bitcoin, the stakes sit in plan menus rather than spot ETF flows. Trump’s August order helped frame digital assets as a retirement-access issue for more than 90 million defined-contribution savers. A finalized DOL safe harbor would not put bitcoin and crypto on every lineup overnight. It would give plan committees a clearer map if they want exposure through an asset-allocation sleeve that holds digital assets.

The test now is how fast OIRA clears the economically significant package — and whether the published final rule still gives fiduciaries the process cover the March draft promised, or whether the comment fight rewrote the safe harbor before the White House stamps it.

#4918. Binance hackers had 8-week intrusion window before $6.3B exploit: SEAL

The cybersecurity company flagged similar multi-stage intrusion patterns behind other high-profile crypto heists, including the $388 million Bitget hack.

Updated (Nov. 6 at 05:03 p.m. UTC): This article has been corrected to clarify the timeline of the Binance hack. The article also includes a statement from a Binance spokesperson.

Nonprofit cybersecurity organization Security Alliance (SEAL) estimates that North Korea-linked hackers compromised a Binance employee’s device about eight weeks before the exchange’s $6.3 billion exploit on Oct. 29, although Binance said the broader attack campaign began in June and that the hackers did not gain access to additional wallet infrastructure devices until Oct. 20.

The hacking group compromised a Binance software engineer’s device in September through a fake recruitment scheme, wrote SEAL in a Thursday report, adding that threat actors can navigate compromised systems with a “high degree of stealth over prolonged periods of time” after initial intrusion. SEAL’s eight-week estimate refers to the September-to-October intrusion window, rather than the beginning of the broader campaign described by Binance.

Cointelegraph contacted Binance for clarification on the timeline. A spokesperson said the initial device compromises occurred in June and September, but that attackers did not gain the access needed to reach wallet operations until Oct. 20.

“As outlined in our announcement, the initial device compromises occurred in June and September, but the attackers did not gain the access needed to reach wallet operations until October 20, when they used that foothold to move laterally and obtain access to additional devices and credentials,” the spokesperson said.

SEAL identified at least four “front companies” associated with TraderTraitor’s fake recruitment campaigns in 2026, including citalab.net, citalab.com, bitfit.io and oddium.io.

Binance traced the attack campaign back to June, when a separate employee’s device was compromised after the employee downloaded a tampered Homebrew installation while attempting to install an AI coding tool. The exchange detected the incident and replaced the device, rotating related credentials. However, the attackers had already obtained information about employees working on Binance’s wallet infrastructure, according to a Tuesday X post.

On Oct. 20, attackers used the compromised computer of the engineer targeted in September and an internal AI tool to gain control of two wallet infrastructure engineers’ devices, according to Binance. They subsequently discovered a deployment permission token, which was used during the Oct. 29 exploit.

The cybersecurity organization’s analysis pointed to similar multi-stage intrusion patterns in other high-profile crypto hacks. It estimated that attackers had about six weeks of access before the $388 million Bitget hack, six weeks of access to LayerZero before the vulnerability that led to the $293 million Kelp DAO exploit and about three weeks before conducting the $24 million AFX exploit.

The US Federal Bureau of Investigation attributed the attack to North Korea’s TraderTraitor, in a Saturday report. Kim Yo Jong, sister of North Korean leader Kim Jong Un, dismissed the accusations.

#4914. Strive (ASST) Posts $474.8 Million Common Net Income as Bitcoin Yield Hits 63.2%

Strive reported $474.8 million of net income attributable to common stockholders for the third quarter ended Sept. 30, a print that arrived Friday alongside a company claim that Class A total return had outperformed bitcoin by 103.7% year to date through the same date.

GAAP net income was $509.0 million, or $5.24 a share on a fully diluted basis. Almost all of that came from a $533.7 million unrealized gain on digital assets marked to fair value. Operating revenue for the quarter was just $2.7 million, so the earnings line is a treasury mark, not a fee business.

In a post on X that led with the outperformance figure, Strive also highlighted a year-to-date bitcoin yield of 63.2% and a bitcoin dollar gain of $9.00 per assumed diluted share outstanding, both measured through Sept. 30. Chairman and CEO Matthew Cole tied those metrics to the stock’s relative showing against the asset.

“Strive has continued to successfully execute on its treasury management strategy, delivering a strong bitcoin yield year to date in 2026, which I believe is the primary driver of our common equity’s outperformance relative to Bitcoin so far this year,” Cole said in the earnings release.

Bitcoin yield, as Strive defines it, is the percentage change in bitcoin per share over a period — not a cash yield. On that measure the company posted 18.5% in the third quarter alone and 19.3% from Oct. 1 through Oct. 30, after the SATS preferred debut.

Strive acquired 8,136 bitcoin in the third quarter at an average cost of about $78,885 and 20,373 bitcoin over the nine months ended Sept. 30. Another 15,124 coins followed between Oct. 1 and Oct. 30. Holdings stood at 28,000 bitcoin on Sept. 30 and 43,124 by Oct. 30, keeping Strive among the largest public corporate stacks after recent multi-thousand-coin buys and smaller weekly adds that still trailed Strategy.

Digital assets at fair value were $2.34 billion on the Sept. 30 balance sheet. Cash had risen to $490.4 million by Oct. 30, and Strive still held Strategy’s variable-rate STRC preferred with a fair value of about $50.1 million.

The capital stack that funded the buys is mostly perpetual preferreds. On Oct. 20 Strive closed a registered offering of 7 million shares of its 15% Series A perpetual preferred (SATS) for about $609 million in net proceeds. Preferred dividends of $34.2 million in the quarter are what turned the $509.0 million GAAP profit into the $474.8 million common figure.

Strip the story to nine-month GAAP and the picture flips. For the nine months ended Sept. 30, Strive posted a net loss attributable to common stockholders of $88.4 million. The third-quarter profit is real under fair-value accounting, but it is concentrated in one mark-to-market line.

ASST itself has not moved in a straight line with the company’s bitcoin-yield scoreboard. Shares closed at $20.59 on Thursday, down about 10% on the day, and traded near $20.70 early Friday. That leaves the stock roughly 29% below its level a year earlier, when Yahoo Finance data put the close near $29, and about 33% under its Oct. 21 local high of $30.75.

The 52-week range on Yahoo Finance runs from $7.02 to $34.40. Year to date, the price path from the Jan. 2 close near $17.30 into the mid-$20s is a gain of roughly 20%, with a February low near the 52-week floor and a September push back toward $30. Through Sept. 30 — the cutoff on Strive’s outperformance claim — ASST had risen about 70% from that January close while bitcoin was down roughly 7% over the same window on Yahoo Finance data, a wide gap even before the company’s own total-return methodology.

The longer chart still carries the scar of 2025, when Asset Entities became the public shell for Strive’s bitcoin-treasury reverse merger and the share price spiked into triple digits on weekly closes before collapsing. Investors who held through that conversion saw a different ride than the tidy year-to-date relative-return figure Strive put at the top of Friday’s note.

Cole said the recent SATS issuance leaves Strive “never been in a better position to generate long-term returns in excess of Bitcoin for our common equity shareholders.” The test for ASST from here is whether another quarter of bitcoin yield and preferred-funded buys can reopen the gap to bitcoin after a year in which the stock still sits well below where it traded twelve months ago, even as the treasury has climbed past 30,000 then 40,000 bitcoin.

#4912. Trump-Backed American Bitcoin Turns a Profit as Bitcoin Rise Lifts Books

Trump-Backed American Bitcoin Turns a Profit as Bitcoin Rise Lifts Books

American Bitcoin, the Trump-family-backed mining venture, swung to a roughly $126 million profit in the third quarter, a sharp reversal from the loss it posted three months earlier, as a jump in Bitcoin’s book value and disciplined accumulation powered its best showing yet.

The Nasdaq-listed company, which trades under the ticker ABTC and is majority-owned by miner Hut 8, reported net income of about $126 million for the quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with a $57.2 million net loss in the second quarter.

Adjusted EBITDA came in at roughly $221 million, versus a $45 million loss the prior quarter. Much of the swing came from a $197 million gain on its digital assets, as Bitcoin’s value rose during the period.

The company grew its Bitcoin reserve to about 8,427 BTC, up roughly 425 coins, or 5%, from the end of June, and lifted its “satoshis per share,” a measure of how much Bitcoin backs each share, by about 5%. Notably, it did so without buying any Bitcoin or issuing new shares through its at-the-market program during the quarter, relying instead on its own mining output.

“These are significant results, particularly in a challenging digital asset market,” said Eric Trump, the company’s co-founder and chief strategy officer, touting the profit and the growth in Bitcoin per share. CEO Mike Ho credited “a disciplined, Bitcoin-denominated operating business,” saying the firm continued “mining Bitcoin at a structural discount to spot.”

The mining operation itself had a tougher quarter. American Bitcoin mined about 891 BTC, down from 932 in the prior period, and its cost to mine rose to roughly $42,100 per Bitcoin from $36,500, which it attributed to higher seasonal power costs. Gross margin slipped to 41% from 49%. The company ended the quarter with a fleet of about 89,242 miners and 28.1 exahashes per second of capacity.

The results cap a volatile stretch for the venture. American Bitcoin notched a record mining quarter earlier this year as it crossed 8,000 BTC, but its stock has struggled, hitting new lows and prompting a 1-for-15 reverse stock split in July. Shares have fallen well below their debut price since the company went public through a merger with Gryphon Digital Mining.

The company’s profit, while striking, rests heavily on an accounting gain tied to Bitcoin’s price rather than core mining operations, meaning a downturn in Bitcoin could just as easily swing the figure back into the red.

#4909. Former FBI Agent Pleads Guilty to Stealing $2.47 Million in Crypto From Investigations

A former FBI agent pleaded guilty Thursday to stealing about $2.47 million in cryptocurrency with wallet credentials the bureau had collected in its own cases, the U.S. Attorney’s Office for the Eastern District of Virginia announced.

Patrick Steven Yaroch, 37, of Ashburn, Virginia, entered pleas to wire fraud and money laundering in federal court in Alexandria. U.S. District Judge Patricia Tolliver Giles accepted the plea. He faces up to 20 years in prison and is scheduled for sentencing on March 4, 2027.

Court papers put the illicit haul at $2,474,380.66. Prosecutors say Yaroch made roughly 100 fraudulent transfers using credentials tied to 35 separate FBI investigations, drawing on wallets controlled by investigation subjects, adversarial nation-state actors and at least one wallet the FBI itself had created for an operation.

The Justice Department Office of the Inspector General had flagged the case in August after Yaroch’s arrest on a criminal complaint. That earlier account described a smaller set of transfers — about 10 moves totaling roughly $1 million — and noted that Yaroch self-reported the activity to two department employees on July 29, 2026. The plea figures are larger.

From 2017 through early 2025, Yaroch worked in the FBI Boston Division on a squad focused on an adversarial nation-state. From February 2025 through July 31, 2026, he served in the FBI’s Counterintelligence and Espionage Division at headquarters, with access to systems that stored cryptocurrency credentials gathered in investigations.

Beginning no later than December 2024, Yaroch used that access to move coins out of those wallets and into ones he controlled. After the transfers, he swapped the stolen cryptocurrency into other digital assets and ran further transactions meant to obscure its source, nature and ownership.

Between Nov. 25, 2025, and Jan. 13, 2026, he moved about $93,082 into one of his accounts, then shuffled funds among other accounts to cover debts, including credit cards, car payments and loans, the office said.

FOX 5 DC, citing the same U.S. Attorney’s Office release, noted the Portugal planning that appears in the plea papers. Yaroch asked ChatGPT how to maximize investment returns and where to live in the European Union. After the chatbot suggested buying a vineyard in Portugal, he gave Portuguese lawyers a power of attorney on June 15, 2026, to register him with the Portuguese Tax and Customs Authority, obtain a tax identification number and collect the password to his finance portal. He arranged travel to Portugal for September 2026.

The agreed statement of facts goes beyond the dollar figure. Yaroch’s conduct compromised multiple FBI investigations into criminal groups and nation-state adversaries, the office said, and it compromised FBI sources and methods used in foreign intelligence collection and criminal cases. It also “deprived FBI employees of their ability to carry out their functions to protect public safety and national security.”

First Assistant U.S. Attorney Theophani K. Stamos and Daniel S. Wierzbicki, special agent in charge of the FBI Washington Field Office’s Counterintelligence Division, announced the plea. Assistant U.S. Attorneys Kathleen Robeson and Russell Carlberg are prosecuting, with help from the National Security Cyber Section of the Justice Department’s National Security Division and the DOJ inspector general’s Mid-Atlantic region. The case is 1:26-CR-208 in the Eastern District of Virginia.

The admission lands in a week when federal crypto-crime stories keep stacking up — from a failed bitcoin kidnapping plot that produced its own guilty plea last month to FBI tallies putting U.S. crypto fraud losses above $11 billion in 2025, a figure the White House has used to frame a wider crackdown.

What sentencing will do with the gap between Yaroch’s July self-report and the $2.47 million plea figure is still open. So is how many of the 35 FBI cases can be repaired after an insider treated the bureau’s own wallet credentials as a personal ATM. March 4, 2027, is when Judge Giles gets to answer the first question.

#4907. Solana’s Shanghai event halted over unauthorized promotion: Report

Solana’s promotional campaigns through domestic entities allegedly violated China’s crypto-related restrictions, an unidentified PBOC official told China’s Financial News.

Chinese authorities halted the Solana Accelerate event in Shanghai over alleged unauthorized promotional activities involving domestic entities, China’s state-backed Financial News reported on Friday.

The outlet cited an unidentified official from a department of the People’s Bank of China (PBOC), who said Solana had promoted its brand through domestic entities, including by placing advertisements on a building in Shanghai’s Lujiazui financial district.

The official also claimed that Solana provides technical services supporting the issuance and trading of cryptocurrencies, which he described as illegal financial activities under Chinese regulations.

It marked the second reported disruption of a Solana event in mainland China in the past two years, highlighting how the country’s restrictions extend beyond digital asset trading.  An October 2025 Solana event in Shenzhen was also cut short over venue capacity and public-safety concerns, according to an X announcement by Solana’s Chinese-speaking community.

In an Oct. 16 X post, Solana Foundation president Lily Liu said organizers postponed the Shanghai event to “comply with local requirements” after an event registration unexpectedly quadrupled, exceeding the event cap pre-registered with local authorities.

In a February notice, the PBOC and several other agencies doubled down on crypto-related restrictions and included a new ban on the unauthorized issuance of yuan-pegged stablecoins.

#4906. OpenAI Found 948 Linux Security Flaws—And Figured Out How to Exploit Them

OpenAI Found 948 Linux Security Flaws—And Figured Out How to Exploit Them

OpenAI says its autonomous security agents discovered 948 distinct vulnerabilities in the Linux kernel, developed working exploits and drafted fixes for review—all for roughly $1.2 million in API credits.

“We chose Linux to study how effectively frontier models could find vulnerabilities and demonstrate their security impact in a codebase of this scale and importance,” OpenAI said in results published Wednesday.

The Linux kernel powers cloud servers, Android devices and much of the world's critical computing infrastructure. The flaws identified in OpenAI's experiment were local privilege-escalation vulnerabilities: An attacker would first need limited access to a vulnerable machine, but could then potentially obtain root privileges and take full control.

OpenAI's agents scanned about 2.8 million lines of kernel code, or roughly 7% of the project. “Our prototype identified and analyzed 2,308 candidate Linux kernel findings, dynamically reproduced 1,724 vulnerabilities, and produced 948 local privilege escalation (LPE) exploits that could allow an unprivileged local user to gain root access” OpenAI said.

After grouping exploits by their underlying cause and proposed fix, researchers counted 948 distinct flaws.

The October run cost $1,213,430 in API credits, equivalent to around $1,279 per vulnerability. OpenAI compared that with historical $20,000-to-$70,000 payouts for Linux privilege-escalation exploits at hacking competitions, suggesting a hypothetical value of $18.9 million to $66.3 million if the same results were found manually.

An earlier GPT-5.6 Sol campaign found 166 unique privilege-escalation vulnerabilities in July for $513,690. A newer, unreleased cyber model found more than five times as many in October, even after the kernel had undergone months of fixes.

OpenAI also said its agents used at least 10 exploitation techniques unfamiliar to the researchers and outside experts they consulted—not merely existing tricks retrieved from training data.

AI companies finding critical bugs are just another proof of how systems are capable of performing advanced cybersecurity research achieving results that previously required significant human expertise.

Last week, Anthropic disclosed that an unreleased version of Claude had discovered and exploited serious vulnerabilities in widely used software, including Chrome and Linux. Some of those exploits could execute malicious code remotely with little or no user interaction.

The economics of this research were already changing before OpenAI's announcement.

Researchers at Vidoc Security reproduced several vulnerability discoveries associated with Anthropic's powerful Mythos AI using publicly accessible models for less than $30 per file.

Those tests showed that finding vulnerabilities was becoming increasingly accessible, although reproducing a flaw is different from developing a reliable exploit capable of compromising a protected system.

OpenAI's latest experiment goes further by automating much of that exploitation process, creating a potential advantage for defenders trying to patch software before criminals can take advantage of the same weaknesses.

The company warned that attackers with limited access to an outdated operating system may increasingly be able to escalate their privileges with AI assistance.

OpenAI is attempting to restrict access to its more capable cybersecurity models through verified security programs. Its Daybreak Red initiative provides approved defenders with access to specialized AI tools for vulnerability research, exploit testing, and mitigation development.

#4904. The 6 Best Undereye Patches, Tested and Reviewed by Dermatologists and Beauty Editors

The entire Women's Health team swears by them.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

I’m always up late doomscrolling TikTok and The White Lotus subreddit for season 4 theories. (Natasha Rothwell hive, stand up!) The only problem? Those late nights mean I have dark circles and puffiness in the morning (boo!). While I'm a huge fan of undereye creams, sometimes I get bored and need to switch things up. Enter undereye patches. They're a fast fix for dryness, puffiness, and dark circles—and even help me fake eight hours of sleep. "The skin under the eyes is prone to changes related to aging skin, such as fine lines, and conditions like dryness and discoloration," says dermatologist Brendan Camp, MD. In just 10 to 20 minutes, undereye patches temporarily hydrate the delicate skin and help me look refreshed. Plus, they're super cute and add a fun touch to my selfies, even if they're shared to just my Close Friends circle on Instagram Stories.

Like any other skin care product, eye masks really get their power from their ingredients, some of which are typically hyaluronic acid, vitamin C, niacinamide, and caffeine.

“Eye patches incorporate key ingredients like hyaluronic acid to boost moisture, peptides to firm, ceramides to strengthen the skin barrier, caffeine to help reduce puffiness, and niacinamide to reduce discoloration,” says dermatologist Marisa Garshick, MD.

As with any beauty product, there are always a few things to consider before making the investment. Since these masks are mostly advertised for their ability to soothe and hydrate, you'll want to check the hydrating and firming ingredients, and keep your expectations realistic since results are usually temporary. Here's what to look out for.

For starters, look for key ingredients such as aloe, chamomile, cucumber, green tea, glycerin, or hyaluronic acid. You're less likely to come across vitamin C and retinol, but it's a plus if you do. Patches with a cellulose gel material can also prevent them from sliding off your face (a con I often deal with and try to avoid as much as possible).

Not only is the skin under your eyes thinner and more delicate than in other areas of your face, but it’s also where lymphatic fluid drainage occurs, says dermatologist Lauren Penzi, MD. It can also show more blood vessels, and in turn, make dark circles appear more visible, says Dr. Garshick. Most eye masks are created to reduce fine lines, undereye darkness, and wrinkles while giving your skin the boost of moisture it needs. The overall effect of these patches is to revive and hydrate, which is why so many people use them to get a more refreshed look.

Many people complain about looking tired, and eye patches are a nice, quick fix. Still, it's important to be realistic about the kind of results you'll get in just one or two uses. So, you’ll want to zero in on the reason you’re buying patches, says Dr. Camp. For example, you can target puffiness by rocking undereye patches infused with caffeine or green tea while getting ready for work, says Dr. Camp.

As a member of the beauty team at Women’s Health, I’m constantly on the hunt for the best products and spend a lot of my time testing and reviewing the latest drops. I frequently consult skin experts, which is exactly what I did to kick off my selection process here. I consulted five New York-based dermatologists and had them give their recommendations with insight on why these undereye masks are so splurge-worthy. Then, I tested several of their picks and compared them to some of the other undereye masks available in our beauty closet.To narrow down the list even further, I got a few other members of team WH, including Addison Aloian, Lily Wohlner, Brian Underwood, and Danielle Jackson, to get in on the testing process. Ingredients, price, and overall efficacy were taken into account to determine which would be best for a wide range of needs and preferences.Here are the fruits of our labor.

After testing tons of undereye masks, this one takes the cake for best overall. Why? It's the most hydrating option on this list and it doesn't slip off your face, which is super hard to find in an undereye mask.

ICYMI, Wander Beauty basically started the undereye mask trend. This OG mask is made with peptides, amino acids, and hyaluronic acid to improve your skin's texture, tone, and hydration. It's also expert-approved by Dr. Garshick and dermatologist Dendy Engelman, MD. "I love that this mask is made with three different layers that allow the formula to penetrate the skin deeply and prevent it from sliding off the face, so you can multitask while you wear it," says Dr. Engelman.

Wander Beauty patches are as effective as they are Instagrammable. I’ve been a fan of the rose gold and yellow gold patches for five years, and all of my beauty editor friends gush over them. They brighten, hydrate, and plump up my fine lines. They also contain calendula extract and aloe vera to help reduce puffiness. Ahem, hi holiday parties and upcoming travel—I’m looking at you.

These pretty patches have three unique layers: the surface's foil retains heat and prevents evaporation, the middle grips to prevent slippage, and the base delivers moisturizing glycerin and depuffing calendula extract. For an extra soothing experience, I like to leave mine in the fridge. Cool temperatures help shrink blood vessels under the eyes, so dark circles appear lighter and puffiness minimizes, dermatologist Marisa Garshick, MD, said in our guide to undereye skin care.

That said, the construction of this mask consists of super light and flexible material versus the conventional plastic material found in a lot of other eye patches that were tested, giving this product a high-quality feel.

I would be remiss if I didn't mention my personal fave patch from Patchology. It contains hyaluronic acid (hi, hydrated, plump skin) and feels super cool under the eyes to de-puff and smooth out that area.

Patchology is fantastic for targeting lines, says dermatologist Anar Mikailov, MD. He mentions that there's resveratrol, a natural antioxidant found in grapes that helps improve barrier function, and other antioxidants that provide protection against oxidizing agents that can visibly age the skin by causing wrinkles. There’s also antioxidant-rich strawberry extract, which helps brighten dark circles.

After using this patch, I also noticed my undereye area was less puffy and more luminous. My only con was that the mask slides out of place after a while, but it’s still epic to use and totally worth incorporating into your routine. My hack is to use these patches while lying down in bed to prevent any slipping.

This undereye mask is basically an expert at reducing puffy, tired-looking eyes thanks to its cooling effects. It also temporarily plumps up my fine lines with hydrating hyaluronic acid.

This option comes with a lot of serum, so it may slip down your face over time. But, feel free to go ahead and massage that extra serum into your undereye area—that little bit of massaging can help assist with lymphatic drainage and further reduce puffiness. Need even more convincing? Celebs like Jessica Alba post about this mask on her Instagram story time and time again, noting that she's a major fan.

Most masks on this list only need 10 minutes on the skin, but this one should stay on for up to 20. Although, 20 minutes fly by when you have a good book in hand (pro tip!).

These eye masks from Live Tinted are packed with copper peptides and banana extract (hi, Ashton Hall!) to reduce puffiness and smooth fine lines. They also reduce puffiness after just a few uses and leave skin looking glowy and feeling hydrated.

And because these patches help soften and smooth your delicate undereye area, Aloian relies on them for makeup prep. "Not only is the patch super cute—it's shaped like a sun—but it also genuinely hydrates and brightens my undereye area," she says. These patches make Aloian feel confident going makeup free because of the glow they leave behind, but it's just as helpful on days when she wants a dewy base before applying concealer.

Along with a mix of peptides to help stimulate collagen production, this eye mask contains niacinamide, bakuchiol, caffeine, and vitamin C. Bakuchiol is a great retinol alternative that helps stimulate collagen production, says Dr. Mikailov.

Wohlner also gave this patch a try and loves how flexible it is under the eye. However, she did note that it does tend to slip down the face because it's super thin.

This vitamin C-packed eye patch is perfect for helping brighten up the undereye area while reducing signs of aging and puffiness with the help of caffeine.

Yeah, it's a little pricier, but trust that it's totally worth it if you have the budget. The gel patches are super easy to apply. Plus, they can reduce discoloration and fine lines within just a few weeks. Sometimes I'm wary of products with vitamin C in them because the smell can be a bit aggressive, but these are just right.

As the name suggests, this patch is meant to soothe tired-looking eyes. So, for those of you who have a lot of sleepless nights or are just night owls at heart, this is your winner. Infused with caffeine, niacinamide, and hyaluronic acid, these work overtime to reduce puffiness and dark circles.

#4902. Thailand SEC Files Criminal Complaint Against Bitget Ahead of Dec. 5 Access Block

Thailand's SEC filed a criminal complaint against Bitget operator BG Limited for unlicensed Thai trading from 2022 to 2025.

Thailand’s Securities and Exchange Commission filed a criminal complaint Thursday against BG Limited, the operator of cryptocurrency exchange Bitget, alleging it ran an unlicensed digital-asset exchange for Thai users from 2022 to 2025, and notified the government to block the platform from Dec. 5.

The complaint, lodged with the Economic Crime Suppression Division, also names Mr. Kritsana Kritsananuwat as an alleged supporter who promoted Bitget through Facebook and YouTube channels, the SEC said in statement No. 241/2026.

From 2022 through 2025, the regulator found, Bitget offered a trading system in the ordinary course of business, charged fees, ran a Thai-language website that could show values in baht, and solicited Thai users on social channels including “Bitget Thailand” on X, “BGB Thai Holder” on Telegram and “bitgetthailand” on TikTok.

That conduct amounts to operating a digital-asset exchange under Section 3 of the Emergency Decree on Digital Asset Businesses B.E. 2561 (2018) without a license, a violation of Section 26 punishable under Section 66, the SEC said. The case now moves to an inquiry official, then prosecutors and the courts.

Separately, the SEC submitted Bitget’s website, other domains and its app to the Ministry of Digital Economy and Society under a 2025 royal decree that authorizes access blocks on unlicensed trading platforms. From Dec. 5, the public “will be unable to access the platform,” the statement said, and investors still holding assets there were told to consider acting before the cutoff.

The enforcement push lands weeks after Bitget announced a planned acquisition of GMO-Z.com Cryptonomics (Thailand), a Bangkok digital-asset exchange and brokerage that already holds Thai licenses, through its interest in BTGT Management (Thailand) Ltd.

Under that deal, GMO Internet and its Thai holding company would transfer their entire stake in GMO-Z — about 49% and 50.94%, respectively — to BTGT or a designee at completion, a Sept. 24 GMO Internet filing showed. Bitget said completion remains subject to regulatory approval and, after a change-of-control nod, it intends to introduce its trading platform to Thai users under local rules.

“Thailand is an important part of Southeast Asia’s digital asset landscape, and this acquisition gives us a great opportunity to establish local operations,” Bitget CEO Gracy Chen said in the Oct. 20 release.

The timetable has already slipped. GMO Internet Group pushed the effective date of the share transfer from Oct. 31 to Nov. 30, citing careful work on Thai regulatory applications and closing procedures. That revised date sits five days before the planned Bitget access block.

CoinDesk reported that Bitget’s own terms of use, updated in October, already list Thailand among jurisdictions where its services are prohibited, and that the SEC has not said whether the criminal complaint will weigh on the GMO-Z change of control.

Thailand has used the same access-blocking tool before. In mid-2025 the SEC referred Bybit, OKX and three other unlicensed platforms for a June 28 cutoff, telling users to move assets first. The Bitget action follows that template — and adds a criminal complaint against the exchange operator and a named promoter.

Licensed venues still sit at the center of Bangkok’s policy mix. Thailand has approved a multi-year capital-gains tax break only for trades on SEC-regulated platforms, and has been expanding regulated crypto products in derivatives and capital markets. Unlicensed platforms get the opposite treatment: investor alerts, access blocks and, now for Bitget, a police referral.

What is not yet clear is whether a criminal case against BG Limited for the 2022–2025 period bars, slows or leaves untouched BTGT’s bid to take over a licensed local operator. The SEC statement does not mention GMO-Z. Bitget has not publicly answered the complaint in a statement posted with the acquisition release.

For Thai users still on Bitget, the practical clock is Dec. 5. For Bitget, the test is whether Nov. 30 closes a regulated door into Thailand — or whether the complaint filed Thursday makes that licensed path the next question the ECD and the SEC have to answer.

#4900. Zcash Upgrade Could Lock $16 Million in Crypto Forever

Zcash Upgrade Could Lock $16 Million in Crypto Forever

At recent market prices, those holdings represent roughly $15 million to $16 million that could become effectively frozen when the network activates its NU7 upgrade.

“NU7 (block 3,543,405) makes v4 transactions invalid, so what remains in Sprout is unspendable: frozen, not burned,” Zecstats says in its information card for Sprout.

The upgrade is scheduled for block 3,543,405, expected Thursday evening in the United States. The exact deadline depends on how quickly the blockchain processes new blocks, rather than a predetermined clock time.

Unlike Bitcoin, where transactions are generally visible to anyone examining the blockchain, Zcash allows users to conceal transaction amounts and the addresses involved. It accomplishes this through technology called zero-knowledge proofs, which can verify that a transaction is valid without revealing its private details.

NU7 changes that by eliminating version-4 transactions, the only format capable of moving coins out of Sprout. According to the network's technical specification, transactions using that format will become invalid after activation.

The decision addresses a problem familiar to anyone maintaining old software: supporting obsolete technology creates additional complexity and potential security vulnerabilities.

Developers haven't technically destroyed the coins. They will continue counting toward Zcash's circulating issuance calculations, and a future network upgrade could theoretically restore access, but that is not guaranteed, and the team has not shown any interest in doing so.

In fact, the specification explicitly warns that spending those funds may never be re-enabled.

Legacy holders are the people most exposed to the change. Anyone whose ZEC remains in Sprout must move it through a compatible withdrawal mechanism before the upgrade activates, or risk losing access indefinitely.

Sprout addresses begin with "zc," which can help users recognize old wallet records. Regular ZEC holders using modern supported wallets do not need to migrate their coins out of Sprout, although keeping wallet software updated remains important.

The blockchain also cannot reveal who owns the remaining Sprout balance, meaning nobody knows how much belongs to active users and how much is already inaccessible because its owners lost their keys.

Meanwhile, NU7 brings a benefit for everyone else: Zcash's target block interval falls from 75 seconds to 25 seconds, meaning transactions should receive their first confirmation approximately three times faster, according to the Zcash Foundation.

For Sprout holders, however, the relevant number is block 3,543,405 which will happen later today. As of the time of publication, the latest available block is 3,543,027. Once the network reaches block 3,543,405, any remaining coins in the old pool will be frozen under the new rules.

#4898. Ethereum devs won’t fork Glamsterdam on Hoodi until smart contract risks resolved

Ethereum developers are investigating concerns tied to eight smart contract invariants tied to Glamsterdam’s EIP-8037 and EIP-8038.

Ethereum core developers delayed the Glamsterdam upgrade’s implementation on the Hoodi testnet until security concerns tied to eight smart contract invariants are resolved.

Some existing Ethereum projects rely on assumptions that certain operations would cost a specific amount of gas, which would be invalidated by Glamsterdam’s EIP-8037 and EIP-8038 because they change how gas is charged for certain operations, the Ethereum Foundation said in a Friday blog post.

The decision followed an Oct. 13 report from smart contract auditor ChainSecurity, which found that the two Ethereum Improvement Proposals (EIPs) could break smart contracts that rely on fixed gas-related assumptions when external calls fail.

Delaying the Hoodi testnet fork gives developers more time to identify potential smart contract issues and determine whether changes are needed before Glamsterdam progresses toward mainnet deployment. The Hoodi fork will not proceed until the issues are resolved, added the Ethereum Foundation’s post.

Ethereum’s Glamsterdam upgrade went live on the Sepolia testnet on Oct. 6, in one of its final major rehearsals. The upgrade aims to advance Ethereum’s layer-1 scaling roadmap with enshrined proposer-builder separation, block-level access lists and changes to gas pricing that better reflect the cost of execution, the Ethereum Foundation announced in a Sept. 28 blog post.

#4896. Bitcoin Magazine Books Opens 333-Copy Genesis Book Collector Edition for 3rd Anniversary

Bitcoin Magazine Books opened a third-anniversary campaign Friday for Aaron van Wirdum’s The Genesis Book, offering a redesigned Collectors Edition limited to 333 hand-signed, individually numbered hardcovers in an exclusive Genesis Block slipcase.

Each Collectors Edition hardcover is hand-signed and hand-numbered by van Wirdum, the former Bitcoin Magazine editor-in-chief whose book traces the people, projects and ideas that preceded Satoshi Nakamoto’s peer-to-peer cash design. The standard package is priced at $221 and pairs the book with a limited trading card carrying a Certificate of Authenticity, a collector pin, a sticker sheet, the slipcase and a digital package.

Two one-of-one packages sit outside the ordinary number queue. The #1 package is listed at $5,000 and the #21 package at $2,500; each includes the matching numbered copy, a matching one-of-one collector card and the same accessories as the standard collector package.

Standard collector numbers are assigned in purchase order starting at #2 and skipping #21, the campaign FAQ says. Once preorders close, the signed Genesis print run will not be reproduced.

#4893. Mercado Bitcoin Said Its Hack Was Contained, But Blockchain Data Tells a Different Story

Mercado Bitcoin Said Its Hack Was Contained, But Blockchain Data Tells a Different Story

An attacker drained Mercado Bitcoin-linked deposit addresses nearly four hours after the Brazilian exchange said it had contained a security breach, new blockchain research shows.

Bitquery's analysis, updated Thursday, traces more than $48 million in stolen assets across 11 blockchains. Its findings challenge the exchange's claim that it stopped the attack quickly.

The Bitcoin exchange announced at 9:56 a.m. Brazil time on November 2 that it had quickly contained the incident. Bitquery says 36,042 Ethereum customer deposit addresses were emptied between 9:27 a.m. and 1:56 p.m. Brazil time.

Those addresses held roughly $266,000, a small fraction of the total theft. But the attacker could still move those funds after the exchange’s announcement.

A customer deposit address works much like a personal receiving account for cryptocurrency. The exchange later gathers those coins into a shared online wallet, called a hot wallet, to process everyday transactions. Moving funds from a deposit address requires its private key, the secret that authorizes transfers.

Bitquery says the withdrawals were authorized directly by the deposit addresses, suggesting the attacker had access to their private keys or to a system capable of signing transactions on their behalf.

“That needs a key for each address, or a way into the system that signs for them,” the researchers explained. They warned that exchanges typically generate deposit keys from a single master key, meaning the attacker may still be able to access other customer addresses.

The amount stolen is also disputed. Initial estimates put the amount above $40 million, while Mercado Bitcoin's updated FAQ now puts affected assets at about $36.3 million. Bitquery estimates that $48.6 million was stolen across 11 blockchains.

Mercado Bitcoin's public updates have not explained the gap between its containment claim and the later transfers. The company assured customers it would cover user losses with its own funds.

Bitquery warns customers against reusing old deposit addresses until the exchange confirms they are safe. Mercado Bitcoin's reopening plan begins November 9 with balance viewing only; deposits, withdrawals and trading have no restart date.

#4890. U.S. Treasury Cuts Off MEXC From U.S. Financial System Over Alleged $16 Billion in Illicit Flows

The U.S. Treasury’s Financial Crimes Enforcement Network issued a final order Friday that will cut offshore exchange MEXC Global off from the American banking system over alleged links to sanctions evasion by Iran, North Korea and Russia, the department said.

FinCEN’s order, published as a PDF pending Federal Register placement, finds MEXC a “primary money laundering concern in connection with Russian illicit finance” under section 9714(a) of the Combating Russian Money Laundering Act. Thirty days after that Register notice, covered U.S. financial institutions will be barred from any funds transfer — including convertible virtual currency — to or from MEXC.

Reuters reported the same cut-off Friday afternoon. Treasury framed the move as part of Operation Economic Outcast. It is not an OFAC sanctions designation: the order says it does not rewrite Bank Secrecy Act duties or OFAC rules, and it is a FinCEN funds-transmittal ban rather than a Section 311 proposed rulemaking.

Treasury alleges MEXC facilitated at least $16 billion in illicit flows from May 2021 through May 2026, including nearly $1.5 billion tied to Russian sanctions evasion and about $22 million linked to Iran’s Central Bank and the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps’ Qods Force. The platform was still processing trades with sanctioned Garantex and successor Grinex as recently as June 2026, more than four years after OFAC designated Garantex, the release says.

The order, signed by FinCEN Deputy Director Jimmy L. Kirby, covers MEXC Global and named affiliates operating outside the United States, including Seychelles-incorporated MEXC Global Ltd / MX Global Ltd, Estonia’s MEXC Estonia OÜ, Switzerland’s GIDA Technology AG and the U.K.’s MXC Global Ltd, plus successors. Covered institutions face civil or criminal penalties for willful violations.

Until the Federal Register publishes the notice, the 30-day clock has not started. As of Friday evening eastern time the Register’s public index still showed no MEXC document, matching the order’s own cover note that publication is pending.

Founded in 2018 and formerly branded MXC, MEXC ranks among the world’s 20 largest centralized crypto venues by volume. Treasury puts daily turnover above $1 billion and reserves near $5 billion as of mid-2026, with access claimed across more than 170 countries. The exchange already lists the United States as a prohibited jurisdiction in its user terms, so Friday’s action mainly hits any remaining dollar rails, banking partners and counterparties that still touch U.S. institutions.

Regulators elsewhere have been circling for years. The U.K. Financial Conduct Authority put MEXC Global Ltd on its warning list in March 2024 as unauthorized. Hong Kong’s Securities and Futures Commission warned the same month that the platform was marketing to local investors without a licence. Seychelles’ Financial Services Authority said in May 2026 it took action against MX Global Ltd for unlicensed virtual-asset operations — footnoted in FinCEN’s order.

Those compliance gaps matter because the illicit-flow thesis is specific, not guilt by association. Beyond Russia, FinCEN cites about $1 billion in direct traffic with more than 40 Iranian crypto exchangers, nearly $26 million with the OFAC-sanctioned A7 sanctions-evasion network, and almost $200 million tied to the A7A5 stablecoin. DPRK facilitators and cybercrime flows are also alleged. MEXC has not issued a public response to the Friday order; its main X account kept posting marketing content through the afternoon.

Practically, once the order bites, U.S. banks, money transmitters and other covered firms cannot move dollars or crypto to MEXC wallets or accounts. Offshore users may keep trading peer-to-peer, but dollar on-ramps and any bank that still clears for MEXC become legal risk. The template tracks earlier 9714 cut-offs of venues such as Bitzlato, and it lands beside a busy sanctions week that has already seen North Korea crypto designations, an Iranian exchange action, and Russia’s push to put bitcoin trading on the Moscow Exchange.

Whether MEXC contests the finding, seeks an exemption, or quietly reroutes liquidity is the open question. For now the paper trail is clear: a final FinCEN order under 9714(a), a 30-day fuse after Federal Register publication, and Treasury’s claim of at least $16 billion in illicit volume sitting behind the cut-off.

#4887. Erin and Sara Foster Reveal the Rule That Keeps Their Business—and Sisterhood—Working

The Nobody Wants This producers stop by the latest episode of The Huddle.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

Whether you’ve binged all three seasons of the Netflix comedy Nobody Wants This, presided over a board meeting in your chicest Favorite Daughter blazer, or nodded along to a cringey dating anecdote while listening to The World’s First Podcast, you are no doubt familiar with the work of multi-hyphenate sisters Erin and Sara Foster.

Raised in Los Angeles (dad is famous record producer David Foster), the sisters first gained notoriety with their VH1 mockumentary series, Barely Famous, in 2015. Since then, they’ve gone on to launch several successful businesses, including their clothing line and a production company, Fatigue Sisters Productions. (They even recently served as guest sharks on ABC’s Shark Tank.)

With the season 3 premiere of Nobody Wants This (based on Erin’s own experience of dating and marrying a Jewish man and later converting to Judaism), the dynamic duo stopped by the Women’s Health podcast The Huddle to chat with editors Abby Cuffey and Jacqueline Andriakos about doing business together, setting boundaries, and raising kids.

Erin and Sara launched their clothing line, Favorite Daughter, in late 2020 to fill gaps in their own closets. But as the company has grown and evolved over time, they’ve learned to put their customers at the heart of everything they do. It’s also forced the sisters to staff up with people who are willing to share their true opinions, rather than simply being yes-men or yes-women.

“That's something that is really challenging when you have a talent-focused brand,” Erin says. “It's taken a very long time for us to convince the people who work for us to be honest with us. That’s how people become monsters—no one's telling them the truth.”

“I always say, too, this is a democracy,” Sara adds. “This only works if everyone feels heard. This only works if you're allowed to do the thing that you're hired to do.”

Having a valued third or even fourth opinion also comes in handy when the two can’t see eye-to-eye, which happened often when the brand first launched.

“We’re in a good place today, but we would have blow-up screaming fights in fittings for many years at Favorite Daughter,” Erin says. “It’s tricky for us, because we do not have differentiated, carved-out roles. We have the exact same role, so if we disagree, it's really challenging to find the happy medium.”

In 2021, the sisters’ working relationship expanded further when they launched their podcast The World's First Podcast with Erin & Sara Foster. While the duo aren’t shy about diving into personal topics—be it romantic relationships or raising children—they have set certain boundaries to ensure their friends and family don’t end up embarrassed or as headline fodder.

“I have an idea of where [my husband’s] boundaries are,” Erin says. “He's not really into me talking about exes or other relationships.”

Sara, on the other hand, learned the hard way where one of her daughter’s boundaries lie when she revealed her juvenile arthritis diagnosis—in the hopes of helping other parents—and it was picked up by multiple outlets.

“It never occurred to me that that could happen,” Sara says. “My daughter was like, ‘I do not appreciate that.’ I was like, ‘Oh god, I'm so sorry.’”

Given that they have creative control over the podcast’s content, Erin and Sara do occasionally edit out portions that they think could be taken out of context with the TikTok-ification of media nowadays. “We try to err on the side of keeping things in, but then there's some times that you get a little ahead of yourself,” Erin says.

Speaking of raising daughters, Sara’s got even more wisdom to share on parenting—specifically about telling your kids the harsh truths about yourself.

“Being a mother is just such a exercise in getting humbled,” she says. “My instinct was always like, don't reveal yourself too much. Don't let them see your flaws. Don't tell them about all the times you f---ed up as a teenager yourself. But that's the opposite. You're really supposed to. Maybe some people disagree with this, but as they get older, you’re supposed to reveal mistakes you made, things that you wish you did differently... That has changed the dynamic with me and my older daughter—being honest with her about things that I wish I had done differently.”

Erin agrees: “I think it's probably better for your children to grow up knowing that you are a complicated, flawed person, and then you don't have to fall off a pedestal for them.”

To hear more from the sisters about how they find the creativity to create, their stance on kids and social media, and why they’re obsessed with a certain protein powder, click play on the latest episode of The Huddle above!

#4886. Nigeria sets new crypto licensing rules, bans algorithmic stablecoins

Nigeria has banned algorithmic stablecoins and introduced reserve requirements for issuers as it expands central bank oversight of crypto payment services.

Nigeria’s central bank has introduced new licensing and reserve requirements for cryptocurrency companies, tightening oversight of stablecoins and digital asset payment services in one of Africa’s largest crypto markets.

The Central Bank of Nigeria (CBN) issued the Regulations for the Operations of Non-Security Virtual Assets on Thursday, establishing rules for stablecoin issuers, crypto payment providers, exchanges, custodians and other covered service providers.

The regulations took effect immediately, though existing operators have 180 days to apply for licenses or authorizations. The CBN said the framework aims to support responsible innovation while protecting consumers and financial stability.

The move follows President Bola Tinubu’s July executive order, which sought to coordinate crypto oversight among Nigerian authorities without changing their existing powers.

Under the new regulations, stablecoin issuers must fully back their tokens with reserve assets and maintain an additional buffer equal to at least 2% of their value. At least 40% of reserves must consist of cash or qualifying highly liquid assets.

The rules also set minimum paid-up capital of 10 billion naira ($7.4 million) for naira-backed issuers and 15 billion naira ($11.1 million) for foreign currency-backed stablecoin issuers.

Additionally, companies providing payment-related crypto services, including exchange and custody services, must maintain minimum paid-up capital of 1 billion naira ($742,000).

The regulations also prohibit stablecoins that rely primarily on algorithms rather than eligible reserves to maintain their value, along with tokens that lack enforceable redemption rights.

Nigeria has taken a restrictive approach to crypto trading, previously limiting banking access for exchanges and seeking to restrict peer-to-peer trading using local currency.

The country drew international attention in the crypto industry over its dispute with Binance, the world’s largest cryptocurrency exchange by trading volume.

Binance discontinued its Nigerian naira services in March 2024 amid government scrutiny of crypto trading and allegations of currency manipulation. Nigerian authorities also detained Binance executive Tigran Gambaryan for nearly eight months before dropping money laundering charges against him in October 2024.

More recently, Nigeria’s revenue agency introduced crypto tax collection rules for digital asset platforms and peer-to-peer marketplaces in August 2026.

#4884. DOJ Creates 'Super Intelligence' Task Force to Guard Against AI Abuse

DOJ Creates 'Super Intelligence' Task Force to Guard Against AI Abuse

The U.S. Department of Justice is setting up a task force dedicated to policing the misuse of advanced artificial intelligence, as Washington races to build enforcement muscle around a technology it increasingly frames in near-sci-fi terms.

Announced Thursday, the Super Intelligence Task Force, or DOJ-SITF, will be led by Associate Attorney General Stanley Woodward and Associate Deputy Attorney General Stephen Weiss.

Its stated mission is to make sure American leadership in “super intelligence” isn’t compromised by bad actors, whether foreign governments and their proxies, transnational criminal organizations, or companies and individuals using AI to commit fraud or other crimes.

The department invoked President Donald Trump’s framing that “America stands at the forefront of a new technological revolution in intelligence.”

“SI development will continue to be powered by American innovators,” Attorney General Todd Blanche said in a statement, adding that the task force would work alongside SI Force Chair Jay Clayton to keep critical AI technologies from being “manipulated by bad actors at home or abroad.”

Blanche said the DOJ would keep “identifying and prosecuting individuals whose use of SI violates the law, including by maliciously targeting American taxpayers, critical U.S. infrastructure, or other aspects of our national security.”

Notably, the effort isn’t purely about catching criminals. Weiss said the department also has “a responsibility” to “harden regular reporting on SI risk and response, and ensure companies exercise vigorous internal controls so that SI technology behaves as intended,” signaling that the DOJ wants AI developers themselves to tighten safeguards against models going off the rails.

The term “super intelligence” typically refers to a hypothetical AI that surpasses human capability across the board, and the government’s embrace of the phrase marks an escalation in how officials talk about the technology. Former SEC chair Jay Clayton has been tied to a broader Trump administration push around super intelligence, and the SEC recently added “super intelligence” to its 2027 examination priorities.

The task force joins a widening federal scramble to get ahead of AI risk. Trump signed an executive order setting up a fight with states over AI regulation. Earlier this week, the U.S. banking regulator OCC issued its first AI framework as banks battle rogue agents reaching into customer accounts.

The DOJ did not specify a timeline or detail how the task force would coordinate with other agencies, saying only that its work would “promote innovation and prevent the misuse” of the technology.

#4878. OpenAI Promises to Foot AI’s Power Bill After Voter Revolt. Who Enforces It?

OpenAI Promises to Foot AI’s Power Bill After Voter Revolt. Who Enforces It?

OpenAI promised Thursday that neighbors of its future AI campuses won't foot the bill for their electricity needs, two days after voters dealt data center expansion a blow at the polls. It also pledged public energy and water figures and limits on secrecy agreements with officials.

Its National Community Compact covers future campuses (a fancy rebranding for the term AI data centers) OpenAI both owns and operates, according to the company’s announcement. A separate policy blueprint asks legislators and regulators to apply similar requirements across the industry.

“We will pay the full cost of powering our compute campuses and ensure local residents do not foot the bill for our energy needs,” OpenAI said in its “national community compact.”

It also promises financial assurances before costly grid upgrades, so other customers aren't left paying if projects shrink or fail. Each AI data center would get a water plan, annual progress reports and independent noise studies.

The national compact does not describe penalties for noncompliance, a dispute process or independent verification of electricity and water figures. Residents could read OpenAI’s disclosures, but the document does not explain how they could compel corrections or recover losses.

The ownership restriction matters, too. Partners including Oracle build and own much of the infrastructure reserved for OpenAI, Reuters reported. The company has not said its national compact automatically covers those partner-owned facilities.

The proposed blueprint goes further on electricity costs. “Policymakers should direct state public utility commissions to create large-load tariffs that require large electricity consumers to pay the incremental costs they create,” it says.

A large-load tariff is a special electricity rate for major users such as data centers. The blueprint also urges mandatory reporting, narrow limits on officials’ NDAs and provisions letting governments reclaim tax breaks when developers miss agreed commitments.

The announcement follows a bruising election for the industry. Voters backed restrictions on data centers in multiple states, while candidates pushing for tighter oversight won key races. The results basically send a message to OpenAI and the broader AI industry that communities want a bigger say in how these facilities are built and how their operations affect the people living nearby.

OpenAI says every future campus it owns and operates will receive its own local compact and an annual public progress report.

#4877. How to Lose Fat and Build Muscle Sustainably as a Busy Mom—Your Realistic 4-Step Plan

A strength coach for moms outlines the key strength training and diet habits to adopt for lasting change.

For many moms returning to exercise after pregnancy, losing fat while building muscle can feel like a daunting goal.

“The odds are good that you lost some muscle mass during pregnancy. Maybe you weren’t training as much as you normally did or you weren’t training at all,” says Bailey, coach and founder of Well Mamas Coaching, in a recent YouTube video.

However, body recomposition is a gradual process, she explains, and consistency—“sticking with it”—matters far more than getting everything right.

“It's not [about] doing it perfectly. It's not having an hour to yourself every single day to do all this magical meal prep and green juicing,” she says.

“It's just jumping back in when you can and not buying into the start-stop cycle of...start[ing] over on Monday and find[ing] a new plan.”

Strength training with progressive overload is a “gold standard” pillar of a realistic body recomposition plan, Bailey says.

Progressive overload means gradually making your workouts more challenging over time by increasing the weight, number of repetitions, or training frequency, or by reducing your rest periods.

“This type of strength training is what signals your body...to adapt, change, and rebuild the muscle fibers that we're tearing down during these workouts, which is what helps you build muscle,” Bailey explains.

She recommends focusing on compound exercises such as squats, lunges, deadlifts, hip thrusts, and the bench press because “you're recruiting multiple muscle groups.”

As for your workout schedule, she reassures viewers that you don't need to train “six times a week...for 45 to 60 minutes.”

“Four workouts a week, 30 minutes or less, with progressive overload and compound moves...is plenty,” she says.

By now, it's well established that muscle growth and repair depend on eating enough protein.

“If you're not eating enough protein to repair the tears that you create in your muscles when you work out, then your body doesn't have the amino acids it needs to build those muscle fibers,” Bailey says.

She recommends aiming for around 30g of protein per meal. That's broadly in line with research showing that consuming around 25-30g of protein per meal is associated with greater lean mass and both upper- and lower-body strength in healthy women.

Good options include lean meat, beans, and legumes, yogurt, cottage cheese, and canned tuna.

“Mild” is the key word, Bailey emphasizes.

A calorie deficit means consuming fewer calories than your body burns each day.

“Of course, you do need to be in some sort of caloric deficit in order for fat loss to occur,” she reminds viewers.

However, the size of that deficit matters.

She recommends a moderate deficit of around 200-400 calories below your maintenance intake.

“That's just enough to prompt your body to use stored fat for energy,” she explains. “However, it's not so much that your body starts cannibalizing muscle.”

Research supports the importance of avoiding an overly aggressive calorie deficit, with one study finding that eating more than 500 fewer calories than required impaired gains in lean mass.

She adds one caveat for breastfeeding moms.

“You want to go above what I would calculate if you weren't nursing—anywhere from 400-800 additional calories per day,” she says.

Bailey acknowledges this can be difficult for moms.

“Often sleep and stress feel like they're not within our control,” she says.

However, sleep is essential because it's during recovery that muscle adapts and grows.

“The muscle...broken down through resistance training...is rebuilt...during sleep, while we're recovering,” she explains.

She also references research showing that sleep duration influences body recomposition, with shorter sleep associated with less fat loss.

If “a solid eight hours” isn't realistic, she suggests finding even a few extra minutes by cutting back on doomscrolling or late-night TV.

She also notes that chronically elevated cortisol levels associated with stress may interfere with muscle-building processes, recommending outdoor walks, deep breathing, or even guided meditation in the shower to create small moments of calm.

Kate puts together fitness content that covers functional and strength training, cardio, workout challenges, interviews and news. She's often doing gym laundry or listening to music.

#4875. Kalshi hit with injunction over sports contracts on California tribal lands

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

A federal judge has granted a preliminary injunction barring Kalshi from offering sports-event contracts on the lands of Blue Lake Rancheria and the Chicken Ranch Rancheria of Me-Wuk Indians.

A federal judge has granted a preliminary injunction barring Kalshi from offering sports-event contracts on the lands of two California tribes.

In a Wednesday order, U.S. District Judge Jacqueline Scott Corley ruled that the injunction covers contracts tied to sports including football, basketball, baseball, soccer, tennis, and Ultimate Fighting Championship events.

The tribes — Blue Lake Rancheria and the Chicken Ranch Rancheria of Me-Wuk Indians — must provide boundary maps by Nov. 13, the judge said.

Kalshi is required to report the status of the steps it has taken to comply with the injunction within 21 days, according to the order.

Sports and gaming attorney Daniel Wallach described the order as the first preliminary injunction secured by tribes against Kalshi nationwide, calling it "a major development in tribal gaming litigation."

The tribes filed the lawsuit in July 2025, alleging that Kalshi's sports event contracts constitute unauthorized Class III gaming under the Indian Gaming Regulatory Act (IGRA) and violate the tribes' gaming ordinances.

Kalshi has argued that its sports event contracts are federally regulated swaps under the Commodity Exchange Act (CEA), and that the CEA's grant of exclusive jurisdiction to the Commodity Futures Trading Commission preempts tribal gaming law.

The judge initially denied the tribes' motion for a preliminary injunction in November 2025. The tribes then appealed, and a Ninth Circuit panel partially reversed the decision in September.

"Reversing the district court's denial of a preliminary injunction in part, the panel held that the tribes were likely to succeed in their claims that Kalshi's sports event contracts on tribal lands violated IGRA and the tribes' gaming ordinances," the panel wrote.

The Ninth Circuit panel also held that the CEA's "exclusive" jurisdiction did not extend to Kalshi's sports event contracts.

The Block has reached out to Kalshi for comment.

Kalshi faces mounting legal challenges, with more states taking action against the prediction markets platform.

On Wednesday, Arkansas Attorney General Tim Griffin sued Kalshi for allegedly operating an illegal gambling platform in the state.

"It doesn't take a genius to understand that wagering money on whether the Razorbacks win or lose is gambling," Griffin said, adding that such practice is "squarely prohibited" by Arkansas law.

Last week, Utah also filed criminal charges against Kalshi and two company executives, accusing them of illegally promoting online gambling. Utah has also sued Kalshi and two Susquehanna trading firms for allegedly operating an illegal gambling business.

#4874. What Democrats’ US midterm sweep means for the CLARITY Act

The CLARITY Act faces a new test as Democrats control Congress, raising questions over consumer protections, ethics and what the crypto industry may have to concede.

Crypto companies contributed $233 million to political committees during the 2026 US midterm cycle, according to Public Citizen’s analysis of federal filings.

Democrats won both chambers of Congress and will take control in January, giving them a chance to reshape the stalled CLARITY Act, a bill to set federal rules for crypto markets.

In Ohio, Democrat and longtime crypto skeptic Sherrod Brown defeated Republican Sen. Jon Husted despite crypto-backed political action committees spending $64 million to defeat him.

Senator Elizabeth Warren, one of crypto’s most vocal critics in Washington and the most senior Democrat on the Senate Banking Committee, is expected to chair the panel in the next Congress.

Stronger consumer protections, tougher scrutiny of political conflicts and a shift in regulatory power are all likely to be on the docket in the next round of negotiations.

But a Democratic majority doesn’t have to kill the prospect of crypto market structure legislation completely.

Democrat Congressman Shri Thanedar remains hopeful that the CLARITY Act can still pass, but says any legislation must include stronger ethics requirements. He tells Magazine:

Can the industry’s political influence still be converted into legislation, and what will it have to concede to get there?

The Senate failed to advance the CLARITY Act in September, with disagreements over ethics provisions contributing to the impasse.

The crypto spent $233 million on the 2026 US midterm elections. Source: Public Citizen

Democrats are already reportedly discussing how to reshape the market structure debate, with House Democrats said to be considering expanding proposals from Representative Maxine Waters into a broader market structure bill.

Related: Trump’s memecoin ventures derailed CLARITY Act, Scaramucci says

That bill could include stronger consumer protections, a greater role for the Securities and Exchange Commission, and new ethics restrictions on public officials’ crypto activities.

That could finally mean separating the industry’s push for clearer rules from the controversies surrounding Trump’s personal crypto ventures.

Democrats have already signaled plans to investigate the Trump family’s business dealings and other corporate interests, with incoming House Oversight Committee Chair Robert Garcia planning to pursue inquiries into corporate corruption.

Hadley Stern, head of institutions at Silence Laboratories and founder of Fidelity Digital Assets, says Democrats are right to “go after” the second, and the industry should want them to; that’s “what’s poisoning the bipartisan deal,” he says.

“A sitting president’s family launching memecoins and a stablecoin is a real governance problem,” he tells Magazine. “If I’d done that at Fidelity, I’d have been walked out the same afternoon.”

He says 2026’s digital asset industry is very different from the last time Democrats “held the gavel,” pointing to its growth from “FTX and a bunch of tokens” in 2022 to a federal law for stablecoins, Treasuries and credit funds onchain and tokenized equities on the way:

The industry may also have to accept more demanding standards if it wants legislation that can survive changes in political control.

Paul Brody, founder and CEO of Nightfall Networks and former global blockchain lead at EY, expects banks “to come out winners,” as they will “push for crypto companies” to be held to the same exacting consumer protection and security standards.

Related: Crypto Biz: CLARITY Act setback puts Coinbase in the spotlight

He says the industry has opposed this, but tells Magazine that “a wise strategy” would be to “accept similar conditions,” given that blockchain-native ecosystems are still “vastly more flexible, programmable and efficient.”

Lindsay Fraser, chief policy officer at Blockchain Association, also argues consumer protection and innovation should not be treated as competing objectives.

“We can protect consumers while ensuring the law reflects how the technology actually works,” she says, adding the goal is “lasting policy that earns public trust, gives builders a future in this country, and strengthens American financial leadership.”

That doesn’t mean it’s all smooth sailing to a potential compromise.

Brody says there’s still the possibility of a presidential veto, and points out that crypto is likely to be a “low priority” compared to “investigations of corruption and battles over budgets and the war.”

The midterm results came as lawmakers were already grappling with the fallout from the Binance hack, the largest hack in crypto history, with over $6.3 billion stolen on Oct. 29, raising fresh concerns about exchange security and customer protections.

Thanedar says while the legislation “wouldn’t solve every potential problem,” it would provide “much-needed certainty” and “security guardrails,” and the hack provides a “clear example of the danger of not enacting appropriate legislation.”

Maylea Ma, deputy general counsel at 1inch, says rather than making a stronger case for passing the latest draft of CLARITY, the incident “highlights problems the legislation does not address.”

The hack involved a “centralized exchange’s hot wallet and signing failure,” Ma says, but the current draft “has no insurer, no loss payee, and no hot wallet cap that would have mitigated the loss for Binance or imposed security standards that would have prevented the hack.”

Amanda Fischer, policy director at Better Markets, said the episode highlighted how “unfit for purpose the crypto ecosystem is for mainstream finance,” and crypto exchanges should not act as custodians, since it creates a “honeypot risk.”

Binance hack shows how ‘unfit’ crypto is for mainstream finance. Source: Amanda Fischer

Ma says the incident is “likely to impact” the next vote on CLARITY and could make lawmakers less willing to pass legislation the industry wants, unless it comes with stronger consumer protections.

Crypto spent years convincing Washington it deserves a seat at the table; now it will need to agree on the obligations that come along with it.

Magazine: Bitcoin turns 18: Has the original rebel grown up?
