#4872. Nigeria’s central bank introduces stablecoin licensing rules, bans algorithmic versions

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The rules took effect on Nov. 5 and cover virtual asset activities conducted in Nigeria, directed at Nigerian users, or affecting the country's financial system.

The Central Bank of Nigeria has introduced new licensing requirements for stablecoin issuers and other non-security virtual asset service providers, according to regulations that took effect on Thursday.

According to a circular issued to relevant stakeholders, the regulations establish separate licensing categories for naira-referenced and foreign-currency-referenced stablecoins. Issuers must also secure approval for each token before beginning issuance.

The central bank said the rules cover activities carried out in Nigeria or affecting the country's payments system, financial sector, or foreign exchange market, even if the operator has no physical presence in the country.

Under the framework, foreign-currency stablecoins can only be offered, distributed, exchanged, or otherwise made available to Nigerians as Class 2 tokens issued through a Nigerian-incorporated entity holding a Foreign Currency Issuer Licence.

The CBN also requires issuers to obtain written approval for each token, covering its classification, reference currency, reserve arrangements, name, ticker, and supported blockchain networks, per the circular.

The rules also enforce compliance with anti-money laundering and counter-terrorist financing obligations. Entities must implement customer due diligence, sanctions screening, transaction monitoring, and Travel Rule compliance across all custodial wallets, peer-to-peer services, and remittance channels.

Meanwhile, some tokens are prohibited under the rules, including algorithmic stablecoins. The central bank said the ban covers tokens that rely primarily on market dynamics, rather than eligible reserve assets, to maintain their value.

The framework gives the CBN authority over stablecoins and virtual assets used for payments, transfers, remittances, foreign exchange, and settlement. The Securities and Exchange Commission retains oversight of securities and capital-market activities.

Companies operating across both areas must obtain the relevant approvals from each regulator. The regulations took effect on Nov. 5, 2026, with existing operators given 180 days to apply for the appropriate licence or authorization, subject to transitional requirements.

#4869. Bitcoin Fixes This? Paraguay President Resigns After Bank Lockout Hits 60% of Country

Bitcoin Fixes This? Paraguay President Resigns After Bank Lockout Hits 60% of Country

Paraguayan President Santiago Peña announced his resignation Thursday after the collapse of digital lender Ueno Bank left roughly three million customers unable to access their accounts, turning a banking failure into a political emergency.

The announcement did not end the confrontation. Paraguay’s lower house voted 78-0 to impeach Peña minutes later, sending the charges to the Senate as Vice President Pedro Alliana prepared to take over, per AP.

The bank’s shutdown has also revived an argument familiar to Bitcoin holders: Money held by a financial intermediary is only as accessible as that institution allows. Self-custodied Bitcoin works differently, although it comes with risks of its own.

As the Bitcoin OGs would say, “Bitcoin fixes this.”

Paraguay’s central bank placed Ueno into resolution after regulators found its solvency ratio had fallen below half the legal minimum. The process, announced on November 3, followed accounting adjustments to the bank’s financial statements and left its app, cards, and transfers out of service.

Ueno claimed around 2.9 million customers, more than 60% of Paraguay’s adults. It also held roughly $800 million in public-sector deposits, including pension and health funds, according to AP, complicating an already painful freeze on ordinary savings and wages.

Peña previously held a stake in a company linked to Ueno’s owners, a relationship that fueled allegations of conflicts of interest. He has denied wrongdoing and said he sold his interest in March 2025, according to Reuters.

“My greatest wish is that the return of those funds be guaranteed, that the Deposit Guarantee Fund fulfill its purpose,” Peña said in his resignation address. The Deposit Guarantee Fund would be the equivalent of the FDIC in the United States.

Mensaje del Presidente de la República, Santiago Peña, al pueblo paraguayo. pic.twitter.com/RBz6IfFV9L

— Santiago Peña (@SantiPenap) November 5, 2026

Outside Congress, the anger was less diplomatic. “Santiago Peña and the Colorado Party have stolen all our savings,” protester Luis Pineda told AP. “We are fed up with 70 years of plunder.”

The central bank says Ueno’s failure is an isolated case, not evidence of a broader banking collapse. It has announced a deposit guarantee of up to 228.3 million guaraníes per depositor, roughly $36,000, with initial payments targeting eligible accounts holding no more than 10 million guaraníes.

Bitcoin was born during another banking crisis. Its first block, mined in January 2009, included a newspaper headline about a second bailout for British banks, a historical detail long cited by supporters who distrust reliance on financial intermediaries.

A Bitcoin holder who controls a wallet’s private keys can authorize transfers from the wallet without asking a third party, like a bank, to unlock an account. That is the principle behind the crypto saying “not your keys, not your coins,” and the distinction from leaving Bitcoin on an exchange that can itself freeze withdrawals.

But Bitcoin is not perfect. Self-custody has its own risks: Forget your keys or lose them and there is no way you can recover your funds, there is nobody who can assist you, and no law enforcement agency who can do anything for you.

Paraguay’s central bank said Thursday its first guarantee payments would go to depositors with up to 10 million guaraníes, no Ueno debts and an account at another financial institution.

#4865. Crypto ETFs see over half a billion dollars in outflows amid post-midterm market slide

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

U.S. spot crypto ETFs saw more than $500 million in combined net outflows on Thursday as crypto and tech markets fell across the board.

U.S. spot crypto exchange-traded funds witnessed more than half a billion dollars in net outflows on Thursday as markets reversed initial gains following the U.S. midterms election results.

Among bitcoin ETFs, Fidelity's FBTC led Thursday's net outflows with $199.9 million, followed by BlackRock's IBIT with $105 million and Grayscale's GBTC with $49.9 million, according to data compiled by The Block. Franklin Templeton's EZBC, VanEck's HODL, and Ark Invest's ARKB also saw net outflows, while the remaining ETFs posted zero flows for the day.

Ethereum funds also witnessed heavy net outflows, led by BlackRock's ETHA and ETHB, with $75.2 billion and $22 billion in redemptions, respectively. Fidelity's FETH and Grayscale's ETHE saw outflows too.

Other major crypto ETFs didn't fare much better, accounting for size. U.S. Solana ETF saw a combined $1.2 million in net outflows for the day, Zcash ETFs $11 million, and Hyperliquid ETFs $3 million, according to SoSoValue data. XRP ETFs were the only outlier, with zero flows for the day.

Major U.S. crypto ETFs have now seen a combined net outflow of more than $1.1 billion so far this week.

Bitcoin Bitcoin (BTC) fell 5.6% on Thursday, more than erasing an initial 4% gain on Wednesday following Tuesday's U.S. midterm elections. Ether Ethereum (ETH) was hit harder, falling 7.8% for the day, with the GMCI 30 index, representing a selection of the top 30 cryptocurrencies, dropping 6.4%.

Tech stocks also suffered, with the Nasdaq Composite falling 5.5%, and the S&P 500 dropping 4.4% as investors continue to weigh the impact of potential restrictions on the AI infrastructure buildout following the Democrats' sweep of both the House and Senate.

Kyle Rodda, senior financial market analyst at Capital.com, said the Democratic sweep added to concerns over the AI investment boom, with investors already questioning technology valuations and the pace of infrastructure spending.

"The regulatory and political risks arise at a time when the proverbial AI trade was already on shakier foundations," Rodda said, pointing to disappointing earnings and signs of slowing AI spending.

Axel Rudolph, chief technical analyst at IG, told The Block that bitcoin has been "dragged into the AI rout as investors rapidly unwind risk across technology and crypto markets."

Rudolph said bitcoin's correlation with technology stocks had left it exposed to the selloff, with more than $1 billion in leveraged crypto positions liquidated in 24 hours as the market continued to decline.

Friday's U.S. jobs report could complicate matters further for markets, particularly if the figures give the Federal Reserve more reason to raise rates.

Economists expect around 60,000 to 65,000 new jobs, according to Daniela Hathorn, senior market analyst at Capital.com. She warned that a stronger jobs report, particularly one showing higher wage growth, could send both Treasury yields and the dollar higher.

Despite the broader market weakness, André Dragosch, head of research for Europe at Bitwise, expects bitcoin to outperform U.S. equities as the AI trade unwinds.

Dragosch said bitcoin is now more sensitive to the dollar and expectations for monetary policy than to economic growth, and could decouple from stocks if financial conditions improve.

Data from TradingView showed BTC/USD dipping below $75,000 to levels last seen in mid-August. The total crypto market cap was down 0.5% on the day.

BTC/USD one-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

US stock markets rebounded as traders took advantage of heavily discounted tech shares. The S&P 500 and Nasdaq Composite Index traded up 0.6% and 1.2%, respectively, at the time of writing.

Nasdaq Composite Index one-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

Certain tech stocks saw much sharper gains, with SanDisk and Micron Technologies both up 6.3%.

US civilian unemployment rate. Source: BLS

Stronger labor-market performance can create a headwind for crypto and risk assets as it suggests that the economy can manage any financial policy tightening, which in turn can reduce liquidity inflows to the market.

On the back of the latest data, expectations of the Federal Reserve hiking interest rates by 25 basis points at its December meeting increased from 66.9% to 76.8%, per data from CME Group’s FedWatch Tool.

Fed target-rate probability comparison for December FOMC meeting (screenshot). Source: CME Group

BTC/USD one-day chart with 50-week SMA. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

In his latest market analysis, Keith Alan, cofounder of crypto trading platform Material Indicators, warned of the consequences of losing the trend line, which has acted as support since mid-September.

“As strong as this support level looks from both a technical and structural perspective, we can’t confirm its durability before the W close,” he wrote on X, referring to the weekly candle close.

Alan saw a drop to as low as $69,000 for BTC/USD should the area around $75,500 fail to hold. A recovery, he said, depended on the pair producing a series of higher highs and higher lows on lower time frames.

#4862. Claude Gets Personal Finance—Anthony Pompliano Accuses Anthropic of Copying His Startup

Claude Gets Personal Finance—Anthony Pompliano Accuses Anthropic of Copying His Startup

Anthropic launched a personal finance feature for Claude on Thursday, letting users connect bank accounts and ask the chatbot what they have spent, what bills are due, and how much money they have left.

The announcement came with an accusation from crypto investor Anthony Pompliano, who says Anthropic tried to get inside information from his competing startup, Silvia, months before the rollout.

Claude can then summarize spending, spot duplicate charges or unexpected fees, explain interest payments, and build charts or bill calendars. Anthropic's example shows a user asking where their money went this month, then following up to find out what remains before payday.

"Access is read-only, and Claude can't move, manage, or invest your money," Anthropic wrote on X. "Like the rest of Claude, Personal Finance has no ads or sponsored recommendations," the company added.

The assistant can see balances, credit limits, account names and types, the last four digits of account numbers, and up to 24 months of transactions from accounts users approve. According to Anthropic's documentation, conversations inside the Personal Finance hub are not used to train its models, but account balances may lag and AI-generated figures should be checked against bank records.

Also, how Anthropic tracks its users’ financial data and uses it beyond model training is not completely clear—and this is extremely valuable information for big data companies.

Pompliano, whose Silvia product bills itself as a personal AI chief financial officer, posted a lengthy complaint on X shortly after Claude's announcement. He alleged that Anthropic personnel began approaching Silvia engineers in April, supposedly because they were interested in the startup's growth and computing needs.

"Anthropic was fishing for details on how we built our product and why it was so popular," he wrote. Pompliano also described an alleged steak dinner involving Anthropic representatives, claiming someone told the Silvia team that the meal was on CEO Dario Amodei's tab.

His engineers declined to divulge sensitive information, he claimed, adding that someone at Anthropic warned his team about the upcoming launch days earlier.

"Today, Anthropic launched a Claude wrapper that is masquerading as a personal finance product," Pompliano wrote. He also claimed Silvia's computing costs are roughly 97% lower when using its own models and hardware instead of frontier AI models, which most likely mean they are using a local model, likely finetuned for the startup’s specific purpose.

That said, Anthropic was not the first general-purpose chatbot maker to move into personal finance, either. OpenAI launched similar bank connections for ChatGPT in May, also using Plaid for read-only access to financial information, and Agents like Hermes, Open Claw, or Grok Bot can also do pretty similar tasks given the proper skills, permissions, and instructions.

Anthropic says Personal Finance is a first step and is exploring partnerships with licensed professionals and regulated financial firms. For now, the beta is restricted to U.S. customers on three paid Claude plans.

#4860. Hypercall to launch first Anthropic-linked pre-IPO options market

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Trading is expected to begin Monday, with calls, puts and more complex strategies available in select non-US jurisdictions.

Hypercall is launching what it says is the first options market tied to Anthropic ahead of its IPO.

The onchain options platform expects trading to begin Monday, founder Jake Sylvestre and growth and marketing head Dan Edlebeck told The Block. The contracts will trade around the clock but will not be available to U.S. customers.

The options are synthetic, cash-settled derivatives. This means that traders will not own actual Anthropic shares or receive any voting rights when a contract expires. Payouts will be calculated on a contract's strike price and reference price at expiry.

The launch would add options to a market that already offers Anthropic-linked perpetual futures contracts. Sylvestre said Hypercall supports everything from basic calls and puts, which he called "vanillas," to more advanced spreads and strategies. "It's a fully featured options platform," he said. "Any kind of option you want to structure, we can do."

Hypercall is built on Hyperliquid, where existing Anthropic-linked perps give market makers a way to hedge the risk of selling options before the company's shares begin trading publicly. Edleback said that gives Hypercall a head start: "We're able to provide options on these assets before anyone else can," he said.

The platform already offers options tied to SpaceX and the S&P 500 as well as major cryptos like bitcoin Bitcoin (BTC) and ether Ethereum (ETH), according to the executives. Sylvestre previously reported that Hypercall handled $536 million in options notional trading volume for September.

The rollout comes as traders become less confident that Anthropic will even go public this month or even before the end of 2026.

On Polymarket, the chances of a November IPO fell from roughly 88% Tuesday to as low as 51% by Thursday, while the probability of a January 2027 debut jumped from less than 1% to nearly 17%. The market had generated around $566,000 in trading volume.

A similar market on Kalshi, with nearly $4.9 million in volume, put the chances of an IPO before Dec. 1 at 60%, down from around 88%. Contracts on a debut before Jan. 1 and Feb. 1 traded at 63% and 85%, respectively.

Technology shares also sold off sharply on Thursday, with the Nasdaq falling around 6% during the session amid growing concerns around AI infrastructure spending.

Sylvestre said the market backdrop had changed his concerns about pricing the options, from a post-IPO rally to a potential slump.

"People that market make options are really pricing volatility," he said. "How fast it drops matters as much as how much it drops."

An option can expire worthless, but a price swing won't automatically trigger a margin liquidation like with trading perps on leverage.

Anthropic filed its S-1 on Oct. 26, with plans to list on the Nasdaq under the ticker ANT. The Claude developer reported $16.3 billion in first-half revenue and $9 billion in compute spending. Its prospectus revealed that its five largest customers accounted for around 25% of revenue through the end of September.

#4854. TeraWulf’s bitcoin mining revenue falls 69% as HPC leasing surges amid Anthropic buildout

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The former bitcoin mining-focused company now generates most of its revenue from high-performance computing, with further data center capacity under construction.

TeraWulf's TeraWulf, Inc. (WULF) bitcoin Bitcoin (BTC) mining revenue fell to $13.2 million in the third quarter from $43.4 million a year earlier, a decline of about 69%, while the Nasdaq-listed company reported $63 million in high-performance computing (HPC) lease revenue, up 774% from $7.2 million in the same period last year.

Total revenue came to $76.2 million, an increase of 51% year over year, according to its latest earnings release on Friday, with HPC leasing accounting for 83% of that revenue compared to just 14% a year earlier.

The company reported $709.9 million in net income attributable to shareholders for Q3, including a $728.4 million non-cash gain from changes in the fair value of warrant and derivative liabilities. Its operating loss widened to $88.2 million from $24.7 million a year ago, while adjusted EBITDA fell to negative $12.8 million.

TeraWulf said it had 102 MW of critical IT capacity generating revenue at its Lake Mariner site in New York as of Sept. 30, while construction is underway on another 336 MW. TeraWulf expects the latest data center building at the site to start generating rent in the fourth quarter and another in early 2027.

TeraWulf previously agreed to a 20-year lease with Anthropic for about 401 MW of critical IT capacity at its Justified campus in Kentucky. The agreement covers approximately $19 billion in contracted revenue over its initial term. Earlier this week, the company announced a Google credit backstop for project financing in support of Anthropic's lease and a definitive engineering, procurement, and construction agreement with Fluor Enterprises, covering three data center buildings. The first phases are scheduled for delivery in late 2027.

Meanwhile, at its Muskie campus in Kentucky, TeraWulf's contracted power capacity has increased from 500 MW to 1 GW, pending regulatory approval.

"Lake Mariner is delivering contracted capacity and generating revenue, Justified has the customer, credit support and construction framework to move forward at scale, and Muskie gives us a contracted path to a gigawatt of power," TeraWulf Chairman and CEO Paul Prager said in the earnings release.

Across its portfolio, the company is targeting another 250 MW to 500 MW of contracted critical IT capacity each year. TeraWulf said it had about $2.4 billion in cash, cash equivalents, and restricted cash at the end of the quarter.

TeraWulf shares are up 8.1% at $10.90 in pre-market trading on Friday following the release.

#4853. Strive buys 15,124 Bitcoin in October, closes in on top treasury holders

Bitcoin treasury company Strive reported $509 million in third-quarter net income and purchased 15,124 BTC in October, bringing its total holdings to 43,124 BTC.

Bitcoin treasury company Strive purchased 15,124 Bitcoin (BTC) in October, bringing its total purchases for the first 10 months of 2026 to 35,497 BTC, according to its third-quarter earnings report released Friday. The purchases brought the company closer to the top of the corporate Bitcoin treasury rankings.

Strive reported that it held 43,124 BTC as of Oct. 30, making it the fourth-largest publicly traded corporate Bitcoin holder, according to BitcoinTreasuries.NET data. It trails third-place Twenty One Capital by just 390 BTC and would need another 877 BTC to surpass second-place Metaplanet, which holds 44,000 BTC, assuming neither company increases its holdings.

Strive’s accumulation follows comments made by CEO Matt Cole in a September interview, in which he said the company could become the world’s second-largest publicly traded corporate Bitcoin holder by year-end, although he acknowledged that the outcome was not his base-case scenario.

Strive’s Bitcoin accumulation over the past month. Source: BitcoinTreasuries.NET

Strive also raised approximately $609 million in net proceeds through an October offering of its SATS perpetual preferred stock, which carries a 15% annual dividend rate. The company has not specified how much of the proceeds it will allocate to Bitcoin purchases.

Strive reported $509 million in third-quarter net income, driven largely by $533.7 million in unrealized Bitcoin gains.

According to its earnings report, Strive is also evaluating opportunities in banking, brokerage, decentralized finance, reinsurance and tokenization as part of a broader strategy to leverage its balance sheet.

Jeff Walton, Strive’s chief risk officer, told Cointelegraph that the company is looking beyond cryptocurrency markets for ways to put its Bitcoin holdings to work.

“We believe that some of the bigger opportunities are crossing the aisle into traditional finance, operating as a structured finance company, and finding yield and distribution that is not correlated to Bitcoin or crypto,” Walton said.

Shares of Strive (ASST) fell 1.97% to $20.19 in early trading Friday, extending their decline over the past month to 32.15%, according to Yahoo Finance data at the time of writing.

#4851. Zcash's NU7 Upgrade Goes Live, Recycling 60% of Fees to Fund Future Block Rewards

Zcash's NU7 Upgrade Goes Live, Recycling 60% of Fees to Fund Future Block Rewards

"NU7 has successfully activated on mainnet. A new era of Zcash is here," the project's official account tweeted.

NU7 has successfully activated on mainnet. A new era of Zcash is here. $ZEC pic.twitter.com/rPaSFe9h7j

"This is a huge milestone for Shielded Labs," Jason McGee of Shielded Labs, the Zcash development organization behind the mechanism, tweeted ahead of the activation.

The NSM tackles a problem Zcash inherited from Bitcoin: whether, as block subsidies decline with each halving, transaction fees alone will be enough to pay miners to secure the network. Under the mechanism, 60% of transaction fees will be removed from circulation and gradually reissued through future block rewards, while preserving the 21 million ZEC supply cap. Reissuance will begin in 2031, with the recycled funds spread along a smooth curve on top of the existing halving schedule.

The NSM was Shielded Labs' first project and builds on an idea that developer Nate Wilcox proposed years ago, originally called the Zcash Posterity Fund, McGee said. A separate proposal under consideration would require Zcash Community Grants to contribute 50,000 ZEC from its treasury to the NSM.

— Jason McGee 🛡 (@aquietinvestor) November 5, 2026

NU7 also limits how much private transaction activity can fit into each block, reducing the data wallets must scan to find their payments, according to the upgrade specification.

Shielded Labs' original design would have replaced Zcash's halvings with a smooth issuance curve. Community panels supported that change, but coinholders did not, so an alternative design that kept the halvings was developed instead.

"We still think the original design was simpler and better, but we respect the process that led us here," McGee wrote. "It should be difficult to change the protocol, and the process should resist capture by any single organization, group, or influential individual."

The upgrade arrives as Zcash's community debates the future of Zcash Community Grants (ZCG) and its share of the Dev Fund, the slice of block rewards that pays for development.

The five organizations that coordinate Zcash's coinholder voting, Project Tachyon, Shielded Labs, Valar Group, the Zcash Foundation and ZODL, have set out an expedited timeline for proposals and polling, saying "tensions were high" and that a drawn-out debate risked "deepening divisions within the Zcash community."

Proposals are due by November 8, with polls for coinholders and community panels running from November 16 to November 30. If rough consensus emerges, the groups will support a further network upgrade, NU 7.1, to implement it.

#4849. Aave Labs buys Avon team in fixed-rate lending push

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Aave has more than $30 billion in deposits and over $12 billion in active loans, according to the company.

Aave Labs has acquired the team and technology behind Avon as it works to add fixed-rate lending to its onchain lending network.

The acquisition includes Avon's fixed- and floating-rate lending protocols, an onchain order book, domains and related assets, according to a statement shared with The Block on Friday. Financial terms were not disclosed.

As part of the deal, Prince Sinha and Ayush Kaul will join Aave Labs' engineering team, while Omar el-Ashaal will join its business development team as an associate.

"Aave’s goal is to bring the best of finance to everyone, and that means offering the kinds of products users already rely on, including fixed rates," Aave Labs CEO Stani Kulechov said. "The Avon team have spent time working on some of the hardest problems in onchain lending, and they will help us bring Aave to institutions, fintechs, and everyday users around the world."

Avon was founded by Sinha and developed lending markets focused on fixed and floating rates, along with an order book designed for new onchain lending structures. Other developments by the company include interest-rate pricing, risk isolation, and lending market design.

Aave said the Avon team will initially work on fixed-rate lending as the network expands the types of loans available to users.

Aave is the largest onchain lending network, according to the statement, with more than $30 billion in deposits and over $12 billion in active loans.

#4847. Former FBI Agent Pleads Guilty to Stealing $2.5M in Crypto From 35 Investigations

Former FBI Agent Pleads Guilty to Stealing $2.5M in Crypto From 35 Investigations

A former FBI agent has pleaded guilty to stealing about $2.47 million in cryptocurrency using wallet credentials the bureau had collected during its own investigations, the Justice Department said on Thursday.

Patrick Steven Yaroch, 37, of Ashburn, Virginia, pleaded guilty to wire fraud and money laundering in federal court in Alexandria. He faces up to 20 years in prison and is scheduled to be sentenced on March 4, 2027.

Former FBI agent Patrick Steven Yaroch, 37, of Ashburn, pled guilty today to wire fraud and money laundering related to using his access to steal cryptocurrency.@FBIWFO https://t.co/jDVtSiAM3H

— U.S. Attorney EDVA (@EDVAnews) November 5, 2026

Yaroch worked at the FBI's Boston Division from 2017 to early 2025 on a squad investigating an unnamed "adversarial nation state," before moving to the bureau's Counterintelligence and Espionage Division, where he had access to FBI systems holding crypto credentials gathered in investigations, according to prosecutors.

Beginning no later than December 2024, he used that access to steal crypto from wallets controlled by subjects of other investigations, by nation-state actors, and from at least one wallet the FBI had created for an operation. He made about 100 fraudulent transfers using credentials from 35 separate FBI investigations, then swapped the stolen funds into other cryptocurrencies and moved them through further transactions to hide their origin. He used some of the money to pay off credit cards, car payments and other loans.

Yaroch also planned a new life abroad. He asked ChatGPT how to maximize profits from investments and where to live in the European Union, and after the chatbot suggested buying a vineyard in Portugal, he gave Portuguese lawyers power of attorney to obtain a tax identification number for him, prosecutors said. He arranged to travel to Portugal in September.

His actions compromised multiple FBI investigations into criminal groups and nation-state adversaries, as well as sources and methods used in foreign intelligence collection, according to the plea agreement's statement of facts.

Yaroch turned himself in. On July 29, he broke down while telling a former colleague at the Justice Department's National Security Division that he had made "very poor decisions" involving crypto wallets, according to an FBI affidavit filed in the case. He then self-reported to FBI headquarters, saying he had become frustrated that the bureau "could not or would not act" against an adversary's crypto accounts and had decided to "take matters into his own hands."

At the time, Yaroch said he had made about 10 to 12 transfers worth under $1 million, far less than the figures in his plea. Agents searching his home two days later seized a Trezor hardware wallet, three passports and a slip of paper bearing seed phrases, according to the affidavit. His Kraken account held about $188,600, mostly in U.S. dollars and the USDC stablecoin, with smaller amounts in Squid (QUID) and Bitcoin. A further $933,800 sat in Suilend, a lending protocol on the Sui blockchain, which he had accessed through the Slush wallet app. Yaroch told agents he chose Suilend "simply because he liked that the logo was a water droplet."

Agents transferred about $925,000 from those accounts to government-controlled wallets, the affidavit said. The roughly $165,600 held as U.S. dollars on Kraken could not be moved the same way.

Decrypt reported on the charges against Yaroch in August.

#4845. Strive’s bitcoin gains drive $509 million Q3 profit as BTC buying ramps up

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Strive added 15,124 BTC in October and ended the month with $490.4 million in cash as it targets second place among holders.

Bitcoin treasury company Strive reported $509 million in third-quarter net income on Friday, entirely driven by paper gains on its BTC holdings.

Those unrealized bitcoin gains topped $533.7 million for the quarter, while diluted earnings came out to $5.24 per share. Strive also reported roughly $2.7 million in revenue, mostly from investment advisory fees and the medical device business it acquired through its January takeover of Semler Scientific.

"Bitcoin is the hurdle rate at Strive," Cole wrote on X following the earnings release, arguing that growth in bitcoin per share has been the biggest driver of ASST's more than 100% outperformance against bitcoin this year.

Cole also pointed to the company's SATA and SATS preferred shares to help bring in returns above bitcoin for shareholders. Strive raised around $609 million in proceeds from its SATS offering in October and later launched another $1 billion program to sell more SATS shares in the future.

#4844. 7 Best Sex Blankets for Mess-Free Fun, According to Sex Experts

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

Let's face it: Any kind of sex is likely going to involve fluids. And while that's totally normal, nothing kills the mood quite like dealing with that dreaded wet spot on your bed sheets immediately after having sex. There is a way to avoid that annoyance completely, though—I'm talking about sex blankets.

Sex blankets let you get as messy as you want during sex without worrying about the cleanup afterward. Aside from the fact that they help protect your sheets from lube, ejaculate, period blood, and more, they can also make sex even hotter by creating a more relaxed environment. "The more comfortable our environment is, the less things we’re worried about [and] the easier it is to relax and release into pleasure," says Jenn Gunsaullus, PhD, a sociologist, intimacy and relationship coach, and author of From Madness to Mindfulness: Reinventing Sex for Women. "A sex blanket can help make us more comfortable, and reduce any stress about being judged, making a mess, or messing up something nice."

While sex blankets come in a variety of styles and sizes, the best ones have a few characteristics in common. Generally, you'll want to look for a blanket that's waterproof and designed to protect your mattress topper/couch/floor/countertop from any fluids. It's also best to look for a product that's machine-washable and a great size for your needs. Ahead, sex therapists and sex educators unpack the top traits to look for in a sex blanket.

What really sets a sex blanket aside from a regular one is its capacity to reduce mess. So, you'll need a waterproof product, and it'll help if the blanket is also absorbent, says Goody Howard, a Dallas-based sex educator. "The best sex blanket is going to be one that has good absorption, not just something that is waterproof and doesn't allow things to get wet." She recommends looking for keywords like 'charcoal' and 'microfiber' in the product description. This will let you know it's actually absorbent instead of just a plastic-lined piece of fabric, Howard says.

Size is also an important feature to consider. Some sex blankets don’t cover the entirety of a bed, so if you’re looking to cover a lot of surface area, you'll want to opt for a larger blanket like the Liberator Fascinator Throw. On the other hand, a smaller sex blanket, like the Playdrop Sex Mat, might be more travel-friendly and easier to toss in the wash. It all depends on your needs.

If you plan on keeping your sex blanket out in plain sight, you probably don't want it to stick out like a sore thumb. "Keep in mind where the blanket will live in your space so that the color or design matches the area," Howard says. A product that matches your sheets, comforter, or pillows might also make the whole experience of using a sex blanket feel sexier and more natural.

For the past year, Women’s Health editors vetted the bestselling, top-rated sex blankets on Amazon and beyond. After consulting two sex experts, we personally tested each blanket by pouring a cup of water on it and watching how the material responded. We also sent blankets home with our team of anonymous reviewers for further testing, and evaluated thousands of customer reviews and ratings to narrow down the best sex blankets for a wide range of needs and preferences.

So, if you’re interested in trying it out (or looking for an upgrade), see below for the seven best sex blankets you can buy right now, curated with help from sex experts and tested by real users.

Though this is the priciest option on this list, our testers and experts agree that this sex blanket is also the most luxurious and versatile. Not only does it feature soft and plush microvelvet on both sides, but the Liberator Fascinator also comes in multiple colors and sizes—all of which are fully waterproof. Plus, it has a protective inner liner that locks in moisture, ensuring your bed stays spotless and dry.

According to testers, it does take a while to dry out after a wash, but other than that they love this blanket. One even said that it dramatically enhanced their sex life because it's much more romantic than the towels they were using before purchasing this throw blanket.

Literally as soft as sheep's wool, this blanket is perfect for couples who like getting cozy… in more ways than one (wink). The plush micro fleece side of the blanket is waterproof, while the sherpa side is not, so just keep that in mind before laying it down on your bed. The fabric is thicker and warmer than most sex blankets, and it also comes in four sizes and nine colors, so you can customize this comfy throw to your style.

Our tester says that when she's not having sex on this blanket, she loves to get cozy underneath it on her couch or at her desk. It really is a great multipurpose blanket if you're looking for something you can use anywhere at any time. No one will know you use it during sexy time, too!

Sure, this is technically a waterproof blanket for pets, but that's actually what makes it perfect for enjoying sex without worrying about fluids ruining your bed. Plus, this blanket is lightweight enough for easy travel, yet thick enough to keep you cozy and warm. It also comes in nine colors and three sizes, with the smallest (30 x 40 in.) costing less than $20.

Howard is also a fan. "It is a pet blanket, but hear me out: It serves the same purpose, comes in a great variety of colors, and is ridiculously affordable," she says. You would never even know this was made for your furry friend.

This blanket from Catalonia looks and feels like one of those lightweight throws that are used for decor and thrown on the foot of a bed. Available in several neutral colors, it easily doubles as a daily throw, so you never have to get ready because you technically always are. Each blanket is reversible, so if one side gets a little too wet and wild, you can easily flip it to the other.

Our tester says she's never had an issue with this blanket not protecting her sheets from fluids. However, she did note that although it's comfortable to lay on top of, it's a bit too crunchy to lay underneath.

Don't want the bulk of another blanket on your bed? This one is more like a flat sheet, so it can cover a lot of surface area. In fact, the California King size is the largest blanket on this list, so if you have a big bed (or just want to be extra safe), this is an ideal option.

But to be so lightweight, this blanket doesn’t have an absorbency layer. Instead, it’s made of a thin, raincoat-like material so any liquids will pool on top of it. Our testers didn’t mind this feature, but just noted that this might make the blanket better suited for camping sex than indoor intimacy. That said, the material is designed with a silky feel, so it's comfortable enough to use for regular ol’ sleeping, too. Then, whenever the mood strikes, your bed is already set up.

Love the feel of natural fibers? Look no further. This circular sex blanket from Dame is made of ultra-soft cotton with an internal waterproof layer. It also comes in three colors (sage, oat, and copper) and with a travel-ready pouch for easy and discreet storage.

Overall, our testers appreciated the lightweight and airy feel of this material and that it was absorbent, rather than water-resistant. And while some liked the circular shape of this blanket, others would’ve preferred if the blanket were square- or rectangular-shaped like the other options on this list.

For on-the-go intimacy, your best bet is this travel-sized reversible blanket with a waterproof core. As with the Dame Thro, the cotton waffle weave is absorbent, not water-resistant, so liquids won’t pool on top of the blanket. And while it’s not entirely made of natural fibers (it’s 57 percent polyester), our testers liked how soft the material felt. They also liked the vibrant color combination of lilac and orange. #4842. Russia finalizes crypto trading rules ahead of Nov. 17 rollout

The rules allow retail investors to trade BTC, ETH and USDT through regulated intermediaries, with Moscow Exchange planning to launch crypto trading in December.

Russia’s central bank has finalized rules allowing exchanges to offer Bitcoin (BTC), Ether (ETH) and Tether USDt (USDT) to retail investors starting Nov. 17.

Under the rules, non-qualified investors can purchase up to 300,000 rubles (around $3,700) in cryptocurrency annually through each intermediary, including brokers, asset managers and crypto exchange providers. Qualified investors face no purchase limits, but all investors must pass a mandatory test before trading.

The directive builds on legislation signed by Russian President Vladimir Putin on Aug. 4 establishing a regulated cryptocurrency market under central bank oversight.

A week later, on Aug. 11, the Bank of Russia published its draft regulations, identifying BTC, ETH and USDT as eligible for public trading based on market capitalization, average daily trading volume and at least five years of price history on foreign exchanges.

The law took effect Sept. 1, and the Bank of Russia subsequently authorized Sberbank, the country’s largest bank, to offer crypto exchange services in October.

The regulatory developments come as Moscow Exchange, Russia’s largest securities exchange and a publicly traded company, prepares to launch cryptocurrency trading on Dec. 1, initially offering the three cryptocurrencies through pilot transactions.

While expanding access to cryptocurrencies through regulated platforms, Russia is also tightening restrictions on transactions outside those channels.

Direct peer-to-peer crypto trading will be prohibited starting July 1, 2027, while transfers from regulated digital accounts to self-custody wallets are already banned, according to the Bank of Russia. The country also maintains a prohibition on using cryptocurrency to pay for goods and services.

#4840. Elliptic says Binance hackers swapped stolen tokens for $124M in bitcoin, exchange plans more withdrawal reopenings

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The laundering operation spans 20,000 wallets and 16 blockchains, but Elliptic says little has likely been cashed out.

The attackers in last week's $6.43 billion breach of the Binance crypto exchange have managed to swap around $124 million in altcoins into bitcoin, according to an update from blockchain analysis firm Elliptic.

This is on top of the $1.48 billion in bitcoin stolen in the attack, which is being attributed to North Korea.

Elliptic added that the laundering operation has thus far used more than 20,000 wallets across 16 blockchains and 65,000 transactions, in an attempt to obfuscate the source of the funds. Despite this, the firm estimated that only a "negligible amount" has likely been cashed out.

The largest portion of the converted bitcoin came from stolen XRP swapped through THORChain. Elliptic's report said that by early Friday, roughly $50 million in XRP had been swapped, primarily into bitcoin, with about $226 million remaining.

The report added that nearly all of the stolen Solana (SOL) has already been mixed, swapped, or bridged into bitcoin.

Around $3.11 billion, mostly in ether and bitcoin, remains in addresses linked to the attack, while $2.85 billion of the total  $6.43 billion has been frozen. It's unclear if the $470 million not accounted for here is due to price changes since the attack, is from funds that have already been cashed out, or is a mix of both.

The Block has reached out to the report's author, Tom Robinson, for clarification.

Elliptic wrote that it expects the attackers to swap the rest of the ether for bitcoin in small batches before trying to cash it out through OTC brokers and Chinese money laundering networks.

The firm also rejected the speculation that AI agents were doing the laundering.

"The laundering is clearly automated, but nothing we have seen shows an AI agent making decisions," Elliptic said.

On Friday, Binance wrote on X that it was working to reopen withdrawals for HYPE, INJ, APT, PLASMA, NEAR and LTC by the "end of next week." It is also aiming to reopen withdrawals "for all tokens on networks that are already restored," including ETH, BSC, Arbitrum and Optimism.

The exchange's own report, published Tuesday, said the attack was carried out over months and involved a tampered developer tool, a fake job interview and compromised internal AI tooling that got the attackers access to Binance's wallet-signing infrastructure.

Binance has since replenished virtually all the assets stolen in the breach.

#4839. Morning Minute: Bitcoin Erases Election Rally as AI Stocks Crater

Morning Minute: Bitcoin Erases Election Rally as AI Stocks Crater

Morning Minute is a daily newsletter written by Tyler Warner. The analysis and opinions expressed are his own and do not necessarily reflect those of Decrypt. And check out our daily news show 'FOMO HOUR' covering all of the top stories and market action.

📉 Bitcoin Erases Election Rally as AI Stocks Crater

On Wednesday, the market decided a Democratic Congress was good news. Stocks had their best day in three months, Bitcoin climbed back over $80,800, and Trump posted on Truth Social that the market “just hit another Record High” with 401(k)s “through the roof.”

On Thursday, the market changed its mind.

The S&P 500 fell 4.36%, its worst session since the Liberation Day tariffs in April 2025. The Nasdaq dropped 5.52% and closed in correction, more than 10% below its record from last month. The Dow shed 1,833 points.

The damage concentrated in one place. The iShares Semiconductor ETF fell more than 12%, its worst day since March 2020. Micron lost 17.5%, AMD 17.3%, Marvell 16.4%, Nvidia 9.3%. The Magnificent Seven shed roughly $1 trillion in market value. The VIX touched 28.09 intraday, its highest since March. By the close, the U.S. stock market had lost $3T in value - more than the entire market cap of crypto.

Notably, the thing that spooked investors was the same election result that lifted everything a day earlier. Several states where Democrats won have active local opposition to data center construction, and the market spent Thursday pricing in slower permitting and tighter oversight of the AI buildout. Ipek Ozkardeskaya at Swissquote put it simply: “We suspect there is going to be some scrutiny on this aggressive AI buildout.” Rate expectations moved too, with December hike odds falling to 70.1% from 82.7%.

Crypto did not sit this one out. Bitcoin fell 5.4% to $75,871, below where it traded before the election. Ethereum dropped 7.6% to $2,239, a steeper fall than the Nasdaq. XRP lost 9.8%, and Hyperliquid fell 4.9%. The CMC20 index fell 6.29%. Almost $1.1B in crypto longs were liquidated on the day, before a slight relief rally overnight.

Friday’s October jobs report is next. After a week where the market read the same political outcome two opposite ways inside two sessions, it is fair to wonder how much anyone actually knows right now. We will find out soon…

#4836. Trump administration moves closer to allowing crypto in 401(k) retirement plans

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

This comes months after the DOL unveiled its initial rule outlining steps those 401(k) plan managers should take when considering incorporating alternative assets such as digital assets.

A proposed rule that would open up 401(k) retirement accounts to alternative assets, including cryptocurrencies, has landed at the White House for consideration.

On Thursday, the Department of Labor filed a proposal with the White House's Office of Information and Regulatory Affairs (OIRA), a division within the Office of Management and Budget that reviews federal regulations before publication, to further President Donald Trump's executive order directing the department to pave the way for including alternative assets in 401(k) plans.

"Such clarification must aim to identify the criteria that fiduciaries should use to prudently balance potentially higher expenses against the objectives of seeking greater long-term net returns and broader diversification of investments," the DOL said in its filing.

This comes months after the DOL unveiled its initial rule outlining steps those 401(k) plan managers should take when considering incorporating alternative assets such as digital assets and private equity into their investment profiles. Trump, earlier, had directed the DOL through his executive order.

The DOL also earlier reversed guidance discouraging retirement managers from considering crypto as an investment option in 401(k) plans after the department, under the Biden Administration, had raised concerns about crypto in those retirement plans.

The proposal to allow for crypto in 401(k) plans has garnered scrutiny on Capitol Hill. Earlier this year, Sens. Bernie Sanders and Elizabeth Warren pushed the DOL to scrap the rule and said it would expose investors to volatile assets, including crypto.

In the filing on Thursday, when describing the costs and benefits, the DOL said it was still considering the costs.

#4834. Race Day Roundtable: Speed Stories From Runners Who Raced in On’s Cloudboom Strike 2

Three athletes competing at three different distances share their experiences—and satisfying finishes.

Running “fast” is objectively subjective. What’s fast for you could be slow for another runner and impossible for someone else. Fast can even mean something different for individual runners depending on factors like where they are in a training cycle, if they’re coming back from an injury, or the distance they’re running.

Some runners equate fast with a specific pace-per-mile. Brooklyn-based fashion designer Florence Canonoy, for example, said it’s about seeing her paces drop on her tracking app month after month. “I just run to feel good, and then I look at my watch and it’s like 8:15 [per mile], and I’m like, ‘oh, I’m getting fast,’” she told Runner’s World.

For others, like Mollie Kaplan from Florida, running fast has nothing to do with the time on her watch. “When I’m feeling confident and happy, I feel like that’s when I produce my fastest times—when I’m not putting as much pressure on myself,” she said.

And for Long Islander Chris Chung, fast is a feeling: “When I’m doing interval workouts, that’s what fast feels like to me,” he said. “It’s feeling the wind in your face, in your hair, and just feeling like you’re floating.”

Regardless of how you define it, most runners would agree that their footwear plays a role in just how fast they feel on a particular day, especially race day. Below, Canonoy, Kaplan, and Chung share what they each learned pursuing personal goals in different races—all while wearing the On Cloudboom Strike 2.

A content creator from Fort Lauderdale, Florida, who played lacrosse in college and has completed four marathons.

Distance raced in the Cloudboom Strike 2: marathon in Chicago

An industrial designer and content creator from Jericho, New York, who’s been running for 10 years and did his first marathon in 2025.

Distance raced in the Cloudboom Strike 2: half marathon in Vancouver, British Columbia

A fashion designer from Brooklyn, New York, who ran casually in high school and college and began taking running seriously this year.

Distance raced in the Cloudboom Strike 2: 5K in New York City

Race goal: Push herself while returning from a hip flexor injury

This interview has been lightly edited for clarity and concision.

Co-created with athletes for marathons and half marathons, featuring our CloudTec Sphere™ cushioning system for a fast ride and Helion™ HF hyper foam for energy return.

Mollie: My first marathon in New York City in 2024 was my fastest. I’ve been wanting to PR since then. But going into Chicago, we had a yellow warning [weather alert] with less than ideal conditions. So I was a little frustrated because after training all summer in the heat, I was really excited to finally do a cooler marathon and was definitely prepared to PR. But I took the pressure off myself the last two weeks before the race. My goal was not to look at my watch. I just wanted to go based on feeling and not to freak myself out.

Chris: This was my first half marathon back from an injury, a stress fracture in my femur. I hadn’t run 13.1 miles in a year, so I was like, ‘I’m just going to let it come to me and see how I feel. Let me just keep it below 1:50.’

Florence: I was taking my last couple races easy, but this was the one where I actually felt really good. So I was like, ‘Okay, let me push for it.’ It was one of my first races where I really took it seriously.

Mollie: I felt pretty strong from the start and I knew that it was going to be a good day for me. I can usually tell from the first mile. And the Cloudboom Strike 2, they kind of give you speed without trying. So they propel you forward a little bit, which is really great.

Chris: I started at the front and got caught up in the wave of people going out quickly. Within the first quarter mile, I was running PR pace at 6:20s and thinking, ‘I’m definitely not holding this because I haven’t trained like this at all.’ So I pulled myself back. I knew there were some hills on the course, so I just kept it effort-based. The Cloudboom Strike 2 maintained its responsiveness even when slowing down a little bit. I really liked how the rocker didn’t feel too aggressive, so it didn’t feel like I had to stay up on my toes the entire race. But at the same time, the roll-off felt really smooth and helped me maintain my cadence fairly well. The upper was comfortable and didn’t feel overly tight. It had some leeway in it and it felt light and breathable.

Florence: At the beginning, I felt really good, so I started running really fast and the Cloudboom Strike 2 had really good return. I felt like I was bouncing the entire time and it was easy on my hips, so that was great. It was also pretty breathable, which helped because that day it was really, really hot out.

Mollie: I saw my mom, my sister, and my boyfriend four times throughout the course, and every time I saw them, I had a boost of energy right after. Mile 20 was a turning point for me because I was like, ‘I’m still feeling so good I could speed up at this point,’ which I’ve never felt before in a marathon. And then when I finally looked at my watch at mile 24, I knew I had two miles to go and was super close to PR. That’s when I started picking it up, and it was just such a great feeling.

Chris: There’s a section of the race where you run along the seawall in Vancouver; you’re literally running alongside the ocean. Since I train solo, sometimes I love no crowds and the quietness of everything. I turned off my headphones for a little bit and listened to my own footsteps, my own breathing, and just took in the view. And you have that wind coming off the ocean a little bit, and you just feel like you’re kind of cruising.

Florence: In the middle and at the end, obviously the people cheering really helped. But I also felt like my stride was really good, where I was just bouncing and flowing, and I didn’t feel any wobbliness or stiffness. I have shoes that are a little bit too stiff, and I feel something in my body or my hip, but Cloudboom Strike 2 was really good towards the end.

Mollie: [In the last third of the race], they felt really good and comfortable and my body was feeling good, which was a really good sign at that point in the race, but the shoes still felt super springy. And when I started to push the pace a little bit, it just felt like a natural stride.

Chris: Other racing shoes can be really firm and aggressive. With this shoe, I feel like that little extra cushion really felt good for where my fitness was. It’s a little deceiving because it can feel like your foot’s sinking in, but at the same time you’re getting that responsiveness back from it.

Florence: I think it did help in terms of the cushion. There was a big hill in Harlem, so that was a little bit difficult, but the shoe helped propel me forward.

Mollie: I PR’d by three minutes, which is super close when you really think about all the little things that could go differently. I’ve cried crossing the finish line of every single race I’ve run, especially those last 100 meters. I get so emotional thinking about what I just accomplished before even the PR. Just completing a marathon is something so incredible and something that I’m so proud of every time I do it. I think about all the time and work that I put in, the months leading up to it. It’s just a very overwhelming feeling as you’re crossing the finish line.

But when I saw my time, I kind of stopped a little bit because I was so excited and so happy. I’ll definitely be wearing Cloudboom Strike 2s again, and I would definitely recommend them to anyone who’s in the market for a new pair of shoes to race in.

Chris: I know I have the speed in me; I just didn’t know if I had that fitness in me. So it was a good feeling to finish in 1:37. I was happy that I was able to push my body a little more than I had in the past year.

Florence: I went into the race not looking at my watch. And when you start to hear the cowbells, that’s when I started feeling really good and I was like, ‘You know what? I’m almost done. I see the finish line right there, so might as well sprint it.’ So that’s what I did. It felt amazing. Everybody was cheering.

Mollie: Something I’m working on is having confidence in myself from the first mile rather than waiting till mile 13. I tend to second guess myself, especially on the start line. I can tell myself, ‘It's okay if you go a little slower, if you don’t hit your goal.’ And anyone else who’s training for a marathon or looking to achieve the same goal, I would say just have confidence in yourself. Enjoy the journey. Celebrate the little wins, especially in your training cycle.

Chris: To get back to that speed, I know I have to put in more speed workouts. And it’s about playing the long game, too. You can’t force it.

Florence: This race taught me the importance of relying on feeling and not paying too much attention to my watch. When I look at my watch a lot, I tend to run a bit slower. But when I just focus on myself, whatever’s playing in my earphones, and just feeling good and just doing it, I run faster. We have our goals, but rely on how you feel. I think the number one thing is just enjoy it.

#4832. Tesla to Wind Down Cybertruck Production as Elon Musk Pivots to Robotaxis and AI

Tesla to Wind Down Cybertruck Production as Elon Musk Pivots to Robotaxis and AI

Tesla will stop making the Cybertruck at its Texas Gigafactory around the middle of next year, CEO Elon Musk said on Friday, as the company shifts its focus to autonomous vehicles.

"Tesla will be winding down production of the Cybertruck at Gigafactory Texas around the middle of next year to make room for increased Cybercab production," Musk tweeted.

Tesla will be winding down production of the Cybertruck at Gigafactory Texas around the middle of next year to make room for increased Cybercab production

Responding to a user who called the Cybertruck the best vehicle Tesla has ever made, Musk replied: "True, but the Cybertruck fleet will be around for a long time." "Cybertruck was an incredible technology development platform and lives on through Cybercab," Wes Morrill, the truck's lead engineer, wrote in response to Musk's post, thanking the truck's owners and "everyone who contributed to making the future looking like the future."

The stainless-steel pickup, first delivered to customers in late 2023, has struggled to win buyers. Tesla delivered 8,295 vehicles in its "other models" category, which includes the Cybertruck, in the third quarter, down 48% from a year earlier, Electrek reported. That puts the truck's sales at roughly a tenth of the 250,000 units a year that Tesla built its production line to make, according to the outlet.

Musk has made the Cybercab, Tesla's robotaxi, central to his push to turn the carmaker into an AI and robotics company. In April 2025, he said Tesla would launch its first robotaxi service in Austin using Model Y vehicles running its Full Self-Driving software, and begin building Cybercabs in Texas the following year.

Tesla began offering rides in the Cybercab, a two-seater with no steering wheel or pedals that was first unveiled in October 2024, in limited areas of Austin on September 3, Reuters reported. The National Highway Traffic Safety Administration said at the time that it was "evaluating the situation," as federal rules typically require cars to have human controls. Tesla began producing the Cybercab in April, and had 45 of them registered in Texas as of the launch, according to the report.

At the 2024 unveiling, Musk said he expects the Cybercab to cost around $30,000, Auto Express reported.

The shift comes as Musk weighs combining Tesla with SpaceX, which absorbed his AI company xAI earlier this year. Last month, Musk said both companies' boards had formed special committees of independent directors to evaluate a merger, which would reshape his AI empire by bringing rockets, satellites, AI models, robots and cars under one roof. A SpaceX filing has since confirmed that any combination would need sign-off from its "controlling shareholder," Musk.

A merger would also create a Bitcoin treasury behemoth, combining SpaceX's 18,712 BTC and Tesla's 11,509 BTC for a total of 30,221 BTC, worth roughly $2.3 billion at current prices.

Both companies have also blocked their shares from being offered on the tokenized stock venue being built by crypto exchange OKX and NYSE owner ICE.

Gnosis Pay disclosed a data breach after unauthorized access to its legacy debit card system on Nov. 3. Funds were unaffected; 3D Secure is now mandatory.

Gnosis Pay disclosed a data breach affecting its legacy system powering Gnosis Pay-branded debit cards, exposing certain customer information after unauthorized access was detected on Nov. 3.

The company said it contained the incident within two hours, replaced compromised security credentials and addressed the vulnerability, adding that customer funds were not affected and its enterprise platform powering white-label card programs was untouched.

Gnosis Pay has made 3D Secure verification mandatory for online card payments until the affected system is retired on Dec. 20.

#4830. US 401(k) proposal leaves door open to altcoins, but hurdles remain

While the proposal doesn’t exclude altcoins and smaller cryptocurrencies, experts say they could face challenges getting into retirement plans.

Smaller cryptocurrencies could become available in US 401(k) retirement plans under a proposed Labor Department rule, but experts say they may face more challenges than Bitcoin (BTC) and Ether (ETH).

The rule reached the White House for final review on Thursday, according to the Office of Information and Regulatory Affairs. It aims to make it easier for retirement plans to offer alternative investments like crypto, while offering more legal protection for employers and plan managers.

The Labor Department’s March proposal neither specifies which digital assets could be included nor sets a minimum market capitalization requirement. However, it does guide retirement plan managers to consider six factors when choosing: performance, fees, liquidity, valuation, benchmarks and complexity.

The proposal could open the door to more cryptocurrencies in retirement plans, however, it does not guarantee they will be offered. And when it comes to smaller tokens, experts say they could face challenges because of their limited history, liquidity and other risks.

Teresa Goody Guillén, managing partner at Bellementis PLLC, said Bitcoin and Ether are likely to be the first cryptocurrencies offered because of their established markets and investment products. Bitcoin and Ether are the two largest cryptocurrencies by market capitalization, at approximately $1.5 trillion and $273 billion, respectively, according to CoinGecko data as of 20:01 UTC on Nov. 6.

Still, that doesn’t automatically exclude altcoins, she said. Instead, those managing 401(k) plans would need to show that their investment choices are sensible.

“The real question isn’t which cryptocurrencies regulators pick – it’s whether an investment meets the same standards of prudence, liquidity, valuation and investor protection as any other retirement option,” Guillén said.

She added that while a digital asset’s market cap matters, it shouldn’t be the deciding factor. “Big doesn’t mean suitable, and small doesn’t mean disqualified,” Guillén said. “Digital assets shouldn’t get special treatment, or be excluded because they use different technology.”

Bernardo Brites, co-founder and CEO of Trace Finance, echoed Guillén sentiment, adding: “Run any token through that list and the answer is short. Bitcoin passes clearly. Ether passes; everything else fails on track record or benchmark before a fiduciary ever gets to market cap.”

Brites explained, in this situation, market cap matters only indirectly. “The practical test is simpler: can a plan committee document a daily, reliable price from a regulated wrapper?” he said. “If not, it is not going in.”

While the Labor Department’s proposal does not limit crypto investments to exchange-traded funds (ETFs) or managed funds, experts said those products will likely be offered before direct crypto holdings.

“ETFs and managed funds will likely come first because they fit existing custody and recordkeeping systems,” Guillén said. Whereas holding cryptocurrencies directly would raise additional concerns about storage, cybersecurity and pricing, Guillén said. Still, she added that those risks should not automatically rule them out.

Brites also expects crypto investments to become available gradually. He said existing brokerage options would likely come first, followed by standalone ETFs and funds that include small amounts of crypto.

He expects some crypto investment options to become available in 2027, followed by ETFs at larger employers later that year. Crypto could become more widely available in retirement funds by 2028, he said.

“The rule changes the legal risk, not the plumbing, and the plumbing is slower,” Brites said. “White House review can take up to ninety days, then publication, then an effective date typically sixty days later.”

Brites further explained that legal uncertainty could also slow adoption. The proposal would offer retirement plan managers some protection from lawsuits, but it would not prevent them from being sued over their investment decisions.

He also warned that future administrations could reverse the rule, a possibility that could make employers hesitant to add crypto to long-term retirement plans.

“The 2022 guidance was reversed in 2025; a fiduciary signing a ten-year plan design has to consider that this rule could be reversed too,” he said. “And with courts giving less deference to agencies, how much weight the safe harbor carries in litigation is itself unsettled.”

#4828. The Surprising Ways Professional Dancers Overcome Performance Anxiety—and How to Do It Yourself

These science-backed strategies will help you enter a calmer state.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

As the audience settles in their seats and the orchestra begins tuning its instruments, Isabella Boylston, 40, is hit with a sense of panic backstage. Even after 20 years of performing in front of audiences, the American Ballet Theatre principal dancer isn’t immune to performance anxiety. “I feel like a tiny furnace of adrenaline is burning inside of me, my brain is working overtime going through steps and scenarios, and I also feel like I get less circulation to my fingers and toes,” she says.

Whether you’re giving a presentation during a meeting, training for a race, or even getting ready for a date, performance anxiety is extremely common. In fact, 63 percent of the general population reports experiencing anxiety around public speaking. “Performance anxiety can show up in any sort of activity that involves being evaluated or where there is some expectation of the level you’re trying to perform at,” says sport and counseling psychologist Natalie Léger, PsyD. Worry and doubt about the outcome are “extremely normal,” she says. But despite how commonplace anxiety is around performance, it can be hard to manage, and even harder to talk about.

Dancers are no strangers to performing under high-stress situations—and oftentimes, performing through less-than-ideal circumstances such as injury, illness, or minimal rehearsal time. That’s especially true right now, as professional ballerinas gear up for one of their busiest seasons of the year, when they perform The Nutcracker for the holidays. That’s why we turned to some of the pros for their best tips to help regain control over nerves. Here’s what they recommend.

“The first step in being able to tackle anxiety is awareness,” says American Ballet Theatre director of repertoire Nancy Raffa, who helps teach the dancers choreography and mental health practices, and holds a degree in psychology. “Anxiety is a built-up fear towards an outcome that hasn’t happened yet, so instead of criticizing yourself that there’s something wrong with you, ask yourself what it is that you’re anxious about to start shifting the thought process.” Then, give yourself grace and know that how you feel is totally valid.

It's in these moments that Léger suggests introducing some short affirmations, reminding yourself that you’re capable and prepared. You can also try instructional self-talk, which helps shift your attention to the present by giving yourself a small assignment to focus on like holding your shoulders back and your head high. “Our emotion center of the brain is really activated within moments like this, and so if you give yourself instruction, that starts to bring oxygen to the part of the brain that’s more related to decision-making and thinking logically,” she says.

You can’t always predict when anxiety will creep in, but having a well-structured routine can give you a sense of security ahead of that anxiety-inducing activity, says Léger. Jasmine Perry, 31, a corps de ballet dancer with Miami City Ballet, focuses on the root of her anxiety, which is the fear that fatigue will interfere with her performance ability. “So, I focus on what makes me feel strong,” she says. Perry prioritizes getting at least seven hours of sleep, eating balanced meals, and making sure that anything she needs for performance day is ready to go the night before.

Sleep CheckInsufficient sleep can make anxiety worse. If you struggle to go to sleep the night(s) before a big talk or performance, develop a routine around sleep. That may look like “staying away from the screens in the hour leading up to sleep, not working out too close to going to sleep, ensuring that your bed is not a place that you’re reading or watching TV,” Léger says.

A growing amount of research points to a correlation between increased social media use and anxiety. So, consider assessing your social media habits to take stock of where your “energy is being pulled or supported,” says Léger. “For example, feeling checked out while doomscrolling when trying to go to sleep is highly unlikely to support optimal performance, while watching a video of a chef make a recipe you’re interested in trying after training can feel like it’s supporting your creative mind, which can be helpful for performance.”

Start by limiting your screen time around your sleep schedule, both before you go to bed at night and when you wake up in the morning, Léger says. Training yourself to resist reaching for your phone as soon as you wake up helps to put you in touch with the present moment, which is the goal ahead of a performance.

During the busiest seasons, Boylston often completely deletes her social media apps. “It helps me feel more focused and there’s less temptation to self-sabotage and compare myself with others,” she says. Can’t commit to a full social media detox? Perry recommends using an app like Opal to limit your use, or you can use the app time limit setting on your phone to set up timers for specific apps.

The night before or in the hours leading up to a stressful task, Perry finds a quiet, calm environment and takes a few minutes to herself. “I try to visualize the thing that I have to do, and in that image in my mind, implement the things I need to consider the next time that I do it,” she shares. “That way, I’ve had time to think, and process, and let it all settle in, so I’m able to allow those things to happen more naturally, versus physically trying to go over everything in the moment in a panic.”

The key to an effective visualization practice is to make the scene you’re envisioning feel as real as possible, says Léger. Ahead of a presentation, that might mean picturing the room you’ll be speaking in, as well as your audience, or even trying on the outfit you plan to wear. “All of these elements, felt through your own eyes, can lead to the brain firing the same neurons it would in a real-life performance, just at a lesser intensity,” she explains. This practice can also boost confidence by making the task feel more familiar.

Breathing from your diaphragm signals a relaxation response in the body and brings the heart rate back down. “If you think about it, if you were actually under threat, you would never slow down to take a deep breath, you would just act quickly,” says Léger. “Through deep breathing, you’re starting to introduce to your nervous system, ‘I’m okay, I’m safe.’”

Try alternate nostril breathing, says Raffa. Inhale through your left nostril as you hold the right side closed, then switch to exhale through the right nostril as you hold the left side closed (and alternate as you repeat the exercise). Or try deep belly breaths: “Put one hand on your chest and one hand on your lower abdomen and do five to 10 deep breaths where you inhale and let the belly expand out, then exhale and let the belly go back in,” she says. If it helps, breathe with a rhythm by doing three counts in, three counts out.

In the hours (and even days) leading up to a stressful task, it can be hard to stop yourself from obsessing over the unknown. That’s when it’s time to change focus and soothe your brain. “I try to do something that’s totally unconnected to what I have to do that evening, whether it’s reading a book or getting outside and looking at nature,” says New York City principal dancer Emma Von Enck, 29. She also has her own playlist of classical music that calms her down—almost like white noise—before she gets ready for a show.

If you feel like your anxiety is more severe or interfering with your day-to-day life, Léger recommends getting in touch with a mental health professional. But leaning on your family, close friends, or a trusted colleague is also key. “When you talk with peers and articulate what your experience might be, a lot of people are going to see how so much of this is just a normal part of the human experience,” she adds. Normalizing performance anxiety and knowing that you’re not alone goes a long way in helping to ease some of the burden.

It may also be comforting to read up on performance anxiety. Boylston recommends The Inner Game of Tennis by W. Timothy Gallwey, which helped her learn how to stay present and not fixate on the outcome of a situation. Or, try Peak Performance by Brad Stulberg and Steve Magness, which examines the practices of top performers across the arts, sports, and business. Von Enck credits it with helping her to hone her own pre-performance routine.

At some point, though, do your best to let go of control. “In anything where you’re trying to excel, you want to control every element, but you have to be flexible in seeing it as a challenge when something isn’t going perfectly,” Von Enck says. And know that it might take some time to master the art of performance anxiety. “Getting to a place where you can quiet the mind is a process,” says Raffa. “It’s just like dancing; to master the technique, you need to repeat, repeat, repeat.”

Meet the experts: Natalie Léger, PsyD, is a sport and counseling psychologist, mental performance consultant, and the owner of The Collective Edge, a sports and counseling psychology private practice in Washington, D.C. Isabella Boylston is a principal dancer with American Ballet Theatre in New York City. Nancy Raffa is the American Ballet Theatre director of repertoire who helps teach the dancers choreography and mental health practices, and holds a degree in psychology. Emma Von Enck is a principal dancer with New York City Ballet. Jasmine Perry is a corps de ballet dancer with Miami City Ballet.
