#4827. $3.1B in Stolen Binance Crypto Has Yet to Be Laundered: Elliptic

$3.1B in Stolen Binance Crypto Has Yet to Be Laundered: Elliptic

Around half of the $6.43 billion stolen from Binance last month has yet to be laundered, blockchain analytics firm Elliptic said in a report updated on Friday.

About $3.11 billion remains in addresses linked to the theft, mostly in ETH and Bitcoin, and those funds are "very likely" still held by North Korea, Elliptic said. A further $2.85 billion, or 44% of the haul, has been frozen or seized, mostly stablecoins that issuers such as Tether and Circle froze within an hour of the theft. That compares with around 3% frozen after the $1.5 billion Bybit hack in February 2025, according to Elliptic.

Binance hack update: the XRP has started moving. About $50 million has been swapped, mostly through THORChain into BTC, with about $226 million still to move.

Bitcoin converted from other stolen assets is now about 1,650 BTC ($124 million), almost twice the figure a day earlier.…

The FBI has attributed the October 29 theft to North Korean hackers it calls TraderTraitor. North Korea has denied involvement.

The laundering so far has been "highly complex and clearly automated," Elliptic said. It has used more than 20,000 wallets on 16 blockchains, at least 36 services and at least 65,000 transactions, roughly one new wallet every 30 seconds.

The 18,270 BTC stolen from Binance, worth about $1.5 billion at the time of the theft, was split into 19 addresses on the day of the hack and has not moved since. The nine largest Ethereum addresses, holding 89% of the attacker's ETH, have not moved since October 30.

Most of the laundering so far, about $220 million in TRX, has run through Tron, where a single distribution wallet sent 684.6 million TRX to hundreds of new addresses over 51 hours, including through the night in Pyongyang. Almost all of it was bridged to Ethereum.

All of the stolen Solana, about $87 million at current prices, has been swapped, bridged or mixed, and the attacker has started laundering the stolen XRP, with about $50 million converted so far and $226 million remaining. In total, about $124 million has been converted into Bitcoin, much of it through cross-chain service THORChain.

The speed has fueled speculation that AI agents are moving the funds, but Elliptic said the evidence points to conventional automation. "The laundering is clearly automated, but nothing we have seen shows an AI agent making decisions," the firm said.

Elliptic expects the attacker to swap the ETH for Bitcoin and then cash out through OTC brokers and exchanges over a period of months. After the Bybit hack, North Korea laundered all 499,000 stolen ETH within 10 days but took months to cash out, the firm noted.

Binance has continued to restore services. On Friday, the exchange said users could now verify that their balances are backed at least 1:1 using the zero-knowledge proof for its latest proof-of-reserves snapshot, and that it aims to reopen withdrawals for all tokens on restored networks by the end of next week.

#4825. US Treasury walks back crypto surveillance ‘travel rule’ proposal

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

FinCEN and the Federal Reserve Board of Governors rescinded the "Travel Rule," which was first proposed in 2020 to require financial institutions to collect information on funds worth $250 or more.

The Treasury Department has withdrawn a controversial proposal that would have expanded reporting requirements for cryptocurrency transactions.

On Friday, the Treasury's Financial Crimes Enforcement Network and the Federal Reserve Board of Governors rescinded the "Travel Rule," which was first proposed in 2020 to require financial institutions to collect information on transactions of $250 or more, decreasing that threshold from $3,000.

In a filing, FinCEN said it was withdrawing the proposed rule because more than five years had passed, the convertible virtual currency industry had changed and additional public input would be helpful.

"The Agencies believe that any future, potential amendments to the Recordkeeping Rule and Travel Rule related to CVC should be considered in light of these recent developments to ensure that such amendments are fit for purpose," FinCEN said.

When the rule was proposed, FinCEN had argued that bad actors were increasingly using smaller transactions to move illicit funds. However, crypto groups, like Coin Center, had warned that lowering the threshold from $3,000 to $250 would mean that more ordinary transactions would be subject to "warrantless financial surveillance."

In a blog on Friday, Coin Center said it was happy to see the rule proposal rescinded, along with another rule proposal that was withdrawn last month that would have imposed reporting and recordkeeping requirements on transactions involving self-hosted wallets.

"This follows Treasury’s withdrawal last month of the proposed 'unhosted wallet' and cryptocurrency mixing rules, both of which Coin Center had also opposed," the organization said. 'Taken together, these withdrawals close the books on several long-pending proposals that would have expanded the Bank Secrecy Act’s already extensive financial surveillance regime."

Crypto Council for Innovation CEO Ji Hun Kim said in a statement that withdrawing the travel rule was the "right call."

"It had not moved forward over the past several years for good reason," he said. "Lowering the threshold from $3,000 to $250 for cross-border transfers would have placed undue burden on institutions."

#4824. Nail Discoloration Can Be a Mystery. Dermatologists Are Here to Solve It.

Research is uncovering all sorts of new causes—and solutions. Here’s your ultimate guide to both.

NAIL DISCOLORATION AND yellowing isn’t exactly the easiest thing to talk about—and, turns out, pinpointing the cause of it isn’t any easier. With a fairly wide range of visual cues to consider, board-certified dermatologist Dana Stern, MD, explains that ‘discoloration’ is a descriptive catch-all for a nail that has “some kind of color variation from what is considered the normal nail appearance. It’s associated with many different nail issues.”

That makes it particularly difficult to treat. After all, you’ve got to know what’s behind your issue before you can tackle it appropriately. What we can tell you for sure: Whether you’re currently experiencing some unwanted color in your nails or are just curious about future indicators, Dr. Stern says that nail discoloration is an under-discussed topic.

Meet the experts: Dana Stern, MD, is a board-certified dermatologist in New York City. Jennifer Hanway is a board-certified holistic nutritionist.

Well, no longer. Ahead, we break down the specifics on nail discoloration, how to treat and prevent it, and a recently discovered connection between nail discoloration and GLP-1 use.

The spectrum of colors that fall under the umbrella term of nail discoloration are plentiful. “It could be a wide variety of colors including brown, black, white, green, red, purple, and yellow,” says Dr. Stern. Below are common associations with each discoloration shade.

The answer: it depends. It’s actually quite common to have discoloration but it’s hard to nail down (pun intended) the rate of occurrence because it’s a symptom of underlying conditions and not considered a disease within itself.

Nail changes and discoloration frequently occur due to age, normal changes in growth rate, and yellowing are extremely common in older adults. Fungal and bacterial infections, causing greenish nails make up a large percentage of nail discoloration or abnormalities.

Despite overall prevalence and with no single culprit at times, it’s important to understand that nail discoloration can be an indicator of your overall health. “Your nails can reflect aspects of your nutritional status, but they don’t tell you exactly what you’re missing,” explains Jennifer Hanway, a board-certified holistic nutritionist. Though she says that certain changes in nail color have been associated with specific deficiencies, “none is diagnostic on its own,” making it difficult at times to pinpoint the cause and treat it. Dr. Stern mirrors that sentiment, saying that since there is such a wide variety of scenarios for discoloration, it’s important to see a dermatologist.

Hanway says that in some cases, vitamin and mineral deficiencies can cause discoloration. “Vitamin B12 can sometimes cause nail pigmentation; iron-deficiency anemia can cause pale nail beds, and in some cases, a condition known as koilonychia, which is when the nail becomes centrally concave.” Other deficiencies, such as zinc, can affect overall nail growth and appearance.

Though your first step should be to speak to your doctor, it’s also important to take clear photos of your nails for reference and to keep notes. Make reference to which nails are affected, when the changes started, and whether you recently changed your diet, lost weight, started taking supplements, or have begun a new medication. “Speak to your doctor about whether testing for nutritional deficiencies or other factors may be appropriate,” says Hanway.

Depending on your symptoms, medical history, and diet, Hanway says that you may need full bloodwork to test for ferritin, vitamin B12, or other targeted tests.

Dr. Stern says that you can prevent white patches or keratin granulations by using a base coat when you have your nails done or by avoiding prolonged polish wear. If you’re someone who has their nails done regularly, Dr. Stern recommends seeing an experienced nail tech to avoid any chance of encountering discoloration. “Pseudomonas tend to occur in moist spaces on the nail, classically in a lifted acrylic or other type of enhancement, which is why having them done by a professional nail tech can lessen the chances of this occurring.

If you hurt a fingernail where significant blood accumulates under the nail, your dermatologist can evacuate the blood by drilling a small hole in the nail can help to remove the pigment, while also helping to save the nail from falling off.

In terms of nutrition, Hanway says that adequate protein and overall nutrient intake matter. “Nails are made primarily of keratin, a structural protein, so getting enough dietary protein supports overall nail health.” Protein options include eggs, fish, greek yogurt, and nutrient dense foods such as red meat, shellfish, which provide you with iron and zinc. “Supplements should be targeted rather than taken on a hunch, and should ideally be with your healthcare professional’s guidance,” she says.

A multivitamin may help to fill the nutritional gaps when food intake is limited, but it won’t exactly replace a balanced diet or guarantee any nail changes won’t occur. Hanway cautions against taking biotin supplements, even though they are often marketed for stronger nails. “The evidence of benefit in people without a deficiency is limited,” she says. Higher doses of biotin can also interfere with certain laboratory tests, potentially producing misleading results, so be sure to let your healthcare professional know if you are taking it before having bloodwork.

If you’re one of the millions of American women on a GLP-1 medication, know that a recent study suggests there’s a relationship between nail discoloration and these drugs (although it’s not entirely clear on the connection or causality). The 2026 European Academy of Dermatology and Venereology study reported that nail discoloration occurred in 46 percent of GLP-1 users, compared with 17 percent of adults with obesity or type 2 diabetes who had never taken these medications. To add insult to discoloration, nail detachment was also reported by 39 percent of GLP-1 users, compared with 9 percent of the comparison group. Though these are noticeable differences, it’s safe to conclude that there may be an association, but it does not prove that the medication caused these changes.

Dr. Stern points out that the full study was not released to the public, and did not report that about half of the GLP-1 users had nail problems prior to treatment. “This would mean that this study population had a high rate of nail disorders prior to treatment and therefore these nail issues were unrelated to GLP-1 therapy,” she explains. “When the investigators looked at nail changes that began after starting GLP-1, the previously reported associations disappeared entirely. This is a situation where it does not seem that association is equal to causation, but it would be great to have access to the whole study.” Until then, these findings should be considered preliminarily until fully evaluated through peer review.

Her work has appeared in New York, Allure, Glamour, InStyle, Cosmopolitan, New Beauty, and Marie Claire, where she regularly covers skincare, plastic surgery, cosmetic treatments, and makeup trends.

#4822. Treasury cuts off MEXC Global over alleged $16 billion in transactions linked to Iran, North Korea and Russia

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

On Friday, the Treasury's Financial Crimes Enforcement Network issued an order against MEXC Global and said it was a money laundering concern.

The U.S. Treasury Department says it has severed crypto exchange MEXC Global from the United States after it allegedly supported $16 billion in transactions connected to Iran, North Korea, and Russia.

On Friday, the Treasury's Financial Crimes Enforcement Network issued an order against MEXC Global and said it was a money laundering concern.

"Financial institutions cannot have it both ways: Either you support the Iranian regime, or you have access to the U.S. financial system," said Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent in a statement. "Treasury will continue to sever the Iranian regime’s economic lifelines and thwart its desperate attempts to circumvent U.S. sanctions using digital assets."

Over the past year, Treasury has implemented a crackdown of sorts on crypto platforms allegedly helping Iran evade sanctions amid its ongoing war against the country. In June, Iran's largest crypto exchange, Nobitex, was sanctioned after Treasury said it processed more than half of Iran's crypto inflows in 2025. In September, the U.S. also sanctioned Iranian crypto exchange BitBank over alleged bitcoin transfers to the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps.

On Friday, Treasury said that though MEXC Global said it maintained anti-money laundering and says it will block the use of people using it in countries including Iran and North Korea, that it had "severe deficiencies" in those AML controls. The department said Friday's sanction will protect the U.S. financial system.

"Today's action will protect the U.S. financial system from the significant illicit finance threats posed by MEXC Global," the department said.

#4821. Binance hack is ‘cyber war,’ TRM Labs executive says

TRM Labs executive Ari Redbord called for governments to take offensive action against North Korean hackers following the record $6.3 billion Binance theft.

The $6.3 billion Binance hack sent shockwaves through the crypto industry. Following a series of increasingly severe cybersecurity incidents, the attack on the world’s biggest crypto exchange demonstrated how far state-backed hackers can, and will, go.

TRM Labs, a blockchain intelligence company, was on the front lines of efforts to stop the theft, releasing a report in its aftermath. The firm was early to attribute the attack to the North Korean hacking group, TraitorTrader, citing “indisputable” onchain and offchain evidence. The $1.5 billion hack of Bybit in February 2025 was attributed to the same group.

Binance said in its postmortem on Nov. 3 that attackers posed as recruiters to plant malware on an engineer’s work computer, then used a compromised internal AI tool to reach its wallet-signing service.

Ari Redbord, TRM Labs’ global head of policy and government affairs, told Cointelegraph that although the industry’s coordinated response to the attack was unprecedented, “we are only going to see much more of this.”

The speed and scale of industry’s response to the hack were among the factors that stood out to Redbord.

Tether froze 971.1 million USDt (USDT) tokens just 37 minutes after the main sweep. Combined, stablecoin issuers froze tokens worth about $2.67 billion, according to TRM. “Nothing like that has ever been done before,” Redbord said. “They were able to claw back 40% right out the gate.”

The race to catch stolen funds before hackers can move them offchain is a critical part of defending against such attacks. TRM’s Redbord explained that the Beacon Network, an intelligence-sharing system TRM launched in 2025, allowed companies to coordinate quickly to immobilize the funds before working to seize them. “Normally I see a few Beacon alerts maybe every day. I’ve seen, I don’t know, hundreds or maybe even a thousand Beacon alerts over the last few days,” he said.

Related: Ethereum devs won’t fork Glamsterdam on Hoodi until smart contract risks resolved

The push for coordination comes as Bloomberg reported on Nov. 4 that the T3 Financial Crime Unit, which TRM formed with Tron and Tether in September 2024, disbanded in late August, with sources citing slow responses and poor coordination. Tether said it would continue working with TRM through Beacon.

Although Redbord called for greater coordination, he warned that such efforts may do little to deter future attacks. He noted that North Korea’s Oct. 29 theft was equivalent to roughly a third of the country’s GDP and accounted for half of the $12.5 billion stolen by state-backed hackers since 2017. Even if attackers recover only $1 billion, the potential payoff remains enormous, he argued.

“North Korea will keep doing this as long as it’s effective,” he said.

Redbord argued that stopping such attacks requires offensive action from governments in response to North Korea’s use of hackers operating as an arm of the state.

TRM’s capacity gap report found that the gap between attackers and cybersecurity defenders continues to widen, driven by hackers’ early adoption of frontier technologies such as AI. He said law enforcement and compliance teams need AI to find and patch vulnerabilities as quickly as hackers can exploit them.

“Every time North Korea hacks, we should be hacking them back,” he continued, calling for offensive cyber operations and action against laundering networks and banks that hackers use to cash out. “This is cyber war,” he said.

#4819. Anthropic IPO Valuation Could Be Slashed in Half to $1T After AI Stock Rout: Report

Anthropic IPO Valuation Could Be Slashed in Half to $1T After AI Stock Rout: Report

Major investors are increasingly doubtful that Anthropic's initial public offering can go ahead at its expected valuation of $1.5 trillion, after a sharp sell-off in AI stocks, The Wall Street Journal reported on Thursday.

Anthropic, which filed last month to list on Nasdaq under the ticker "ANT," has been aiming to go public the week before Thanksgiving and raise up to $100 billion, according to the Journal. But three major investors who plan to take part in the IPO told the paper the valuation will likely need to come down, with one suggesting it could be closer to $1 trillion.

The company could also raise less than planned, perhaps around $80 billion, one investor told the Journal. Another wondered whether Anthropic might delay the offering until market conditions improve. Anthropic executives and advisers are expected to meet in the coming days to discuss the plans, according to the report.

The doubts follow a rout in AI stocks on Thursday, as investors weighed whether Democratic gains in the midterms could bring stricter oversight of data center construction. The S&P 500 closed down 4.4% and the tech-heavy Nasdaq 100 fell 6.3%, both their worst days since April 2025, while the Philadelphia semiconductor index slid 12.3% and Nvidia dropped 9.2%, NBC News reported. Stocks partly recovered on Friday morning: as of 11:21 a.m. ET, the Nasdaq Composite was up 1.4% and Nvidia had gained 3.4%, according to Yahoo Finance.

Democrats are planning bills to shield consumers from rising electricity costs tied to data centers, require independent testing of advanced AI systems and clarify when AI companies can be held liable for harm, Reuters reported, though any legislation would likely face steep odds while Trump remains in the White House. The party also plans closer scrutiny of leading AI companies, including Anthropic, according to the report.

Sen. Bernie Sanders (I-VT) has called for a "national data center moratorium," while Texas governor-elect Gina Hinojosa has pledged to pause data center approvals in a state that is home to more than a quarter of planned U.S. data center capacity, CNBC reported.

A delay to Anthropic's IPO is now an "escalating risk" in the current environment, technology analyst Richard Windsor told The Telegraph. "A postponement would protect against near-term valuation risks and would be more likely if the correction sustains over a longer period," said Nalin Patel, an analyst at PitchBook, according to the paper.

Some investors want to see Anthropic's October results before assuming a cut. The company's annualized revenue run rate topped $80 billion at the end of October, and it had $146.6 billion in cash as of September 30, according to the Journal.

Traders have pushed back their expectations. On Polymarket, the odds that Anthropic lists by November 20 stood at 43% on Friday, while the chance of a listing by the end of the year was 71%. On Kalshi, where traders bet on when Anthropic will officially confirm its IPO, the odds of that happening before December 1 fell 15 points to 59%, while the chance of it happening before January 1 slipped to 68%. On Decrypt Predict, a prediction market developed by Decrypt's parent company, Dastan, traders also put the odds of an Anthropic IPO before 2027 at 71%.

The valuation now in question is already well below earlier expectations. In September, Decrypt reported that a leaked draft filing showed Anthropic was seeking a valuation of around $2 trillion.

This article was drafted with the assistance of Claude, an AI model developed by Anthropic.

#4818. I’m a Run Coach. Here’s Why a Marathon Doesn't Have to Be Your Ultimate Running Goal

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

This time of year, after most fall marathons have ended and our feeds have seen weeks of people crossing finish lines at some of the biggest in the world in Berlin, Chicago, and New York, it’s really hard to avoid feeling like you should run a marathon. I personally have posted countless videos of myself on the internet saying, “I’m never doing this again,” and even I’m tempted to go back on my word every November.

To be fair, I love the marathon. I’ve run 12 of them. I’ve run them very, very fast. And I’ve run them very, very slow. My first one changed my life. It gave me the courage to move to New York City and try to figure out what to do after graduating college. Six months later, I became one of the first running influencers, and I’ve spent the 12 years since building a global community, Badass Lady Gang, to help thousands of women belong and redefine their relationship with strength, power, and what it means to be a runner.

But I also feel complicated feelings about the marathon. As a running coach, I’ve watched the same conversation happen online for over a decade. Somewhere along the way, newer runners are told, or quietly absorb, that the marathon is the ultimate finish line. Which makes sense, right? Our brains love order and hierarchy. When you think about what it means to progress in running, it feels logical: going faster and farther are straightforward ways to show you’ve improved. But that’s not how it works. The marathon isn’t the objective ultimate goal. Or always the most fun one. And, it’s definitely not something that every runner needs to chase.

Every year I watch too many runners struggle through the training not because they lack commitment or drive, but because they’re chasing a goal they didn’t fully want or that their life can’t accommodate right now. And honestly, it’s just a really hard distance to master! A fellow coach and pillar in the community, Peter Bromka, put it best: “The marathon owes you nothing.” I hate that he’s right. You spend months killing yourself (metaphorically) for one huge goal, and then anything can happen on the day. It’s too hot. You get hurt despite doing everything right. You have an off day and watch your goals fly out the window.

We’re in the largest running boom the sport has ever seen, and I think it’s largely because we’re lonely. One in five Americans said they always or often felt lonely or socially isolated in a 2018 study by the Kaiser Family Foundation. And the stakes are real: the U.S. Surgeon General has warned that loneliness carries health risks comparable to smoking up to 15 cigarettes a day. Running is one of the few activities that genuinely makes a dent in the loneliness epidemic because pretty much anyone can be a runner (even though it is easier and more accessible for some more than others). You can be fast, slow, old, young—if you’re lacing up your sneakers and moving, you’re a runner. Come marathon season, we also hear stories of the incredible adaptive athletes who cross finish lines with a range of disabilities, too.

Basically, there’s a reason run clubs are hot right now. They give people a way to put their phones down and find a community. The more you show up, the more you see people of all shapes, sizes, ages, fitness levels, and backgrounds crushing distances you thought weren’t possible for someone like you. Before you know it, you’ve signed up for your first half marathon and everyone is asking when you’ll run a marathon. Once you get on that path, it’s hard to stop.

Loneliness isn’t the only thing fueling the boom. We’ve also lost the sense that we control our livelihoods, our schedules, our futures. A marathon is something you choose. It’s one place where you get to call the shots: you set the goal, decide how to train, and define what success looks like, even when curveballs come. It gives you back agency in a world that feels uncontrollable. And in an age when we produce our lives online, it looks great on a feed. It’s badass to train for a marathon, especially when you feel like you have nothing else exciting to share.

So yeah, it’s not just a feeling that everyone’s running a marathon lately. It’s real. Over 1.1 million people applied to London. Chicago saw a 12.2 percent jump in participation from 2023. New York jumped 15.1 percent in the same time period. Races sell out in hours. Getting a bib is almost as hard as finishing 26.2 miles. That’s not an accident.

I work in this industry, and I can tell you a lot of money is spent each year making you feel like you should run a marathon. Last year, New York Road Runners, the nonprofit behind the New York City Marathon, spent $3.8 million on advertising and promotion. And much of that content is built around one image: a runner crossing the finish line, arms overhead, tears in their eyes, sharing how they became the person they always dreamed of being. It works. It makes you believe transformation is possible, and it is. But the marathon industry has weaponized our deep desires to belong, to achieve, to become, and forced us on 26.2-mile slogs across our respective cities.

The result: This overwhelming pressure that everyone needs to get out there and run a marathon—and an exponentially growing number of sign-ups. Never in a million years did I think we’d get to a place where getting into a marathon was almost as hard as running it. But here we are.

I spent a decade being paid by the biggest brands in the world to promote the marathon. I built my business on being vulnerable online and sharing my own transformation through running. So believe me when I say: Not everyone needs to run a marathon.

Before I tell you why you don’t need to run a marathon, let me explain why I first decided to run one. It was never something I envisioned for myself. In my mind, “real” runners ran marathons. (Kooks, if I’m being honest.) Then, I graduated from college with a theater degree and had to start paying off my student loans. My community scattered. And I couldn’t run from the grief I’d suppressed after losing my brother my freshman year of college in a tragic accident.

On Thanksgiving morning in 2012, I started actually running from my problems. Running was brutal and wonderful in a way that left no room for my feelings. It demanded every ounce of attention just to keep putting one foot in front of the other or fall over and pray for a quick end.

I know. Dramatic. But I was the person who made the jokes about only running from the cops. Or a bear. I missed over 70 days of school my senior year ditching PE to avoid running. But desperate times! Running really did feel like a lifeline.

Running gave me structure and something to feel excited about. Becoming a runner gave me something to work toward and confidence when friends and family asked me what my plans were now that I graduated from college. Instead of saying, “I don’t know if I’ll ever work a day in my life doing the thing I love most,” I could say, “I’m training to run a marathon” and feel pride and purpose. They would look at me gobsmacked and impressed. “YOU! Running a marathon? WOAH!” Yeah, I’d think to myself beaming with pride. ME.

I went from couch to marathon in seven months. The running magazines I turned to for advice told me I shouldn’t do what I was doing. “You should run for at least a year,” “Going from couch to marathon shows you don’t respect the distance.” (Those were actual pieces of advice in articles I remember reading when I was training for my first marathon.) But I wasn’t running for a time goal. I felt like I was running for my life. I had to do it.

Training was horrible in the most fulfilling way. This was 2013 and learning to run was very different from what it is now. There were blogs written by former high school or collegiate runners or message boards with the type of runners who told you you didn’t belong if you loved to hate running like I did or ran slow. I didn’t know what I was doing. I made every single mistake you could possibly make training for and then running a marathon.

The biggest? I wasn’t ready. I grabbed a free plan from an app, ramped up my mileage way too fast, and got hurt almost immediately. I ran in shoes a size and a half too small. I didn’t know how to take energy gels (and ran the race with an absurd number of them sandwiched between my two sports bras). I wore nipple guards made for men, terrified my nipples would chafe.

And despite all the mistakes and missteps, running my first marathon saved my life. When I crossed that finish line, I immediately had this sense of if I could do that, there really isn’t anything I can’t figure out how to do.

I’ll never forget watching my first New York City Marathon from the sidelines the year I moved here. We were near the finish, at Columbus Circle, and I knew immediately that I had to run it the next year. I felt so proud to be one of the crazy people who had run a marathon, and so connected to what the runners were going through. There’s nothing like belonging to a group that has shared something so grueling and rewarding. And so, one marathon turned into two and before I knew it, I was a person who ran marathons for fun.

Coaching changed everything. I’ve watched a mom of three in her 40s break two and a half hours in the half marathon after years of believing she’d never be fast enough. I’ve watched countless women who’d been convinced they couldn’t be runners, because of what they endured in PE class, finally feel strong in their bodies while training to run a fast mile. I’ve watched women facing everything from depression to addiction pick up running as a tool and use it to get through the hardest stretch of their lives. And I realized the same joy, transformation, and belonging happen at every distance. There is no hierarchy.

But, it’s more than that. Oftentimes, when we talk about the marathon, we don’t grapple enough with the reasons it may be easier for some. Back in my 20s, the marathon was genuinely easier. Everything is easier in our 20s, even if it doesn’t feel like it at the time. We have bodies that recover much quicker, time that’s actually our own, and energy that feels endless.

I had all of that when I ran my first marathon at 24. I was braver, more naive (a gift!) and had no idea how to sit still in discomfort. I thought movement was the quickest way through, so I threw myself fully into crossing finish lines. (Funny enough, taking a break from marathons taught me what it means to sit in the hurt and actually do the work to heal.) That doesn’t mean that once you hit 30 or 40, your hopes of running a marathon are out the window, but it does mean that as you get older, you should be kinder to yourself if things feel hard or don’t look as shiny as they may seem online.

Which brings me to something we don’t say enough: a lot of the runners we look up to or love following on social media—the ones who are making us feel like we have to be a part of this—are often younger, and what we see is a story they’re producing. When you’re feeling inspired by a creator, or measuring your own training, chip times, or total races against theirs, remember who and what you’re comparing yourself to. This may be someone whose whole job is to run and work out, who has access to endless training and recovery resources, and who isn’t sharing the full story about their highs and lows. Social media literacy matters. Without it, we’re measuring ourselves against a bar that wasn’t built with us in mind.

When runners come to me and are deciding whether to throw their name into a marathon lottery or sign up, here’s what I tell them.

Not pressured. Not because you think it’s the logical next step. Compelled. That pull that screams, “I have to try this,” or “This matters to me more than anything else in the world.”

Marathon training is long and it beats you down, both physically and mentally. As a coach, my job is to help my runners uncover what’s really driving them: their real why. Because your why is the only thing that gets you out the door when you’re tired, sore, and regretting your entire decision. It’s the north star that keeps you lacing up on vacation, when your kid is sick, on a lunch break, before a wedding, on an 89-degree day, when every part of you is done. Without it, you’re just suffering for the sake of suffering.

It really is an unpaid (and expensive) part-time job. It requires hours of running and strength training every week—for months. It requires flexibility in your schedule and will leave you feeling exhausted, hangry, and spread thin for at least 16 to 20 weeks, depending on what your running looked like before you actually started marathon training.

And don’t even get me started on timing. You’re either training in the dead of winter where it’s freezing, dark, and icy when you wake up and get off work or in the summer when you’re juggling summer vacations and brutal heat.

How do you feel about running 15 miles around the Grand Canyon or in Italy? Is that doable? What about the weddings on your travel schedule? What do your weekends look like for the next four to five months? If you sign up for a marathon, they look like a whole bunch of staying in, waking up early to run double-digit miles, and obsessing over the forecast for this weekend’s long run.

Moms. Caretakers. People with busy, unpredictable lives.

I spend half my coaching time talking athletes off the ledge when life throws wrenches in their training and they feel like they’re failing. You end up traveling relentlessly for work and aren’t comfortable running in the dark in a city you don’t know. Or you hate the treadmill and have to do most of your training on one. Your kids get back-to-back-to-back sicknesses and you spend four weeks not able to run because you’re fighting off what they had. A parent ends up in the hospital. A partner gets a diagnosis no one saw coming. Extreme weather, which is becoming a new normal for many of us, makes training complicated or even inaccessible. The list goes on and on. Anything can and will happen during marathon training.

The question isn’t, “How bad do you want it?” That’s not how life works. No excuses? Tell that to a parent who doesn’t have reliable help. It's whether you have the time—real, uninterrupted time—to train the way you envision and the wiggle room to adjust to any and all of the above scenarios.

It’s about so much more than everyone posting their curated, perfect peak weeks.

Influencers know what to say to speak to whatever insecurities or fears you’re feeling. Or what to say to get just enough engagement, or outrage, to get our numbers higher. Running a marathon is big business and most of your favorite influencers are making a lot of money sharing their stories. Sometimes, it honestly feels like exploitation. Which is gross. But I wish more people understood that no one is sharing their actual lives.

The second you start filming yourself, whether you’re aware of it or not, you’re producing. You’re making choices that will resonate or earn engagement. So whether someone is sharing the highlight reel or highlighting everything that’s going wrong, remember that you will never know what’s actually happening.

Keep that in mind if you catch yourself comparing your undertrained or ordinary reality to someone else's seemingly perfect one. And keep it in mind when you relate to someone who says their training has been a train wreck too. I can tell you firsthand that making running interesting and entertaining is very, very hard.

If you struggle with perfectionism, marathon training may feel like you’re riding the struggle bus. But if you can train, show up imperfectly, and work with your perfectionism, look at social media with a grain of salt, and feel proud of what you could do, then you're in for an incredible ride.

I’m here to remind you that not everyone needs to run a marathon to transform their life, find belonging, or to have a great time being a part of something spectacular. I say this as someone who loves, reveres, and had her life changed because of the marathon: It really is just a distance. Real change doesn’t come from running 26.2 miles. It comes from specificity and intention and showing up for yourself, whatever that distance is.

There are so many other goals you can pursue with running that will give you the same result. Have you ever gone all in on a 5K? Or a 10K? Spent 16 weeks working to see how fast you can run a mile? Run the perimeter of your town with a group of friends? Can you train for a half marathon with a friend who has never done it before and be their cheerleader every step of the way? These aren’t less impressive goals. They’re your goals. And they hit just as hard.

Goals are empty without meaning. The marathon is a spectacle that feels like the ultimate test. But taking on the biggest, most difficult challenge in a sport isn't the only place where magic lives. You can find that same feeling in other distances, other challenges, and other ways of pushing yourself. The real work starts when you ask yourself one honest question: "Why does this goal matter to me?"

The marathon is an incredible accomplishment. But there is no hierarchy when it comes to distances and goals. It’s not a ladder. Just because you run a half doesn’t mean that you should run a marathon next. And, you can spend your whole life running and never even think of attempting a marathon.

You get to decide what fills your cup, what challenges you, and how you spend your time and energy. Only you have the power to choose what changes you.

So the next time FOMO creeps in and you feel like you’re missing out if you don’t run a marathon, remember this: You already have everything you need to change your life. You don't need 26.2 miles. You just need to show up for yourself.

Can you run a marathon? Yes. Should you? That’s entirely up to you.

#4817. Bitcoin ETFs flip negative for 2026 after $1.28 billion outflow week, worst since June

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The 12 U.S.-listed spot bitcoin funds have now lost $914.1 million for the year, while ether, Solana and Hyperliquid ETFs all extended weekly outflow streaks in a brutal turn for crypto ETFs.

U.S. spot bitcoin ETFs logged $1.28 billion in net outflows last week, their worst week since late June, per The Block's analysis of SoSoValue data. It was the funds' second straight negative week and made the top ten largest weekly outflows since they launched in January 2024 at #7.

The funds saw outflows every day except Wednesday last week, per the data. They lost $398.6 million on Monday, $115.6 million on Tuesday, $430.2 million on Thursday and $411.9 million on Friday, with Wednesday's relatively modest $74.6 million inflow marking the only positive day.

The most recent worse week ended June 26, when the funds shed $1.79 billion in what The Block reported at the time was their second-worst week on record. Bitcoin ETFs lost $202.6 million the week before last, including a $643.3 million Friday outflow that was the largest single-day exit in four months after the record Binance hack.

BlackRock's IBIT accounted for $403 million of Friday's loss, or about 88% of the day's gross outflows. Bitwise's BITB and Ark & 21Shares' ARKB each lost about $20 million, and Grayscale's GBTC shed $11.1 million. Fidelity's FBTC took in $42.8 million on Friday, after leading Thursday's outflows with $199.9 million, as The Block reported.

The week's outflows erased bitcoin ETFs' gains for the year. The funds entered the week with about $368 million in 2026 net inflows, after turning positive for the year in late September. Monday's outflows pushed that figure below zero, and the funds closed Friday at about $914.1 million in net outflows year to date.

The total value of bitcoin held across the 12 funds dropped 2.3% to $95.9 billion, equal to about 6.3% of the cryptocurrency's market capitalization. IBIT held $60.3 billion in net assets against $65.1 billion in cumulative inflows.

The outflows came amid a crypto and equities selloff after Tuesday's midterm elections. Bitcoin fell 5.6% on Thursday alone, and analysts linked the slide to the Democratic sweep of Congress and doubts over AI spending, The Block reported Friday. Analysts say Texas's blue wave in particular could result in slowed development in the state replete with bitcoin mining facilities and data centers.

Spot ether ETFs lost $365.3 million last week, per SoSoValue data, their sixth straight week of net outflows. The run has drained about $2.02 billion from the funds since late September. Ether ETFs have twice posted eight-week losing streaks, most recently when they matched that record in early July.

Like the bitcoin funds, ether ETFs had only one positive trading day last week, also on Wednesday, when they gained $53 million. Monday's $139.8 million outflow was the week's largest, followed by $100 million on Thursday and $93.2 million on Friday. BlackRock's ETHA accounted for $90.8 million of Friday's total, about 96% of the gross losses, mirroring the firm's bitcoin fund.

The products are now down about $405.6 million for 2026. Net assets fell 2.9% over the week to $12.84 billion, against $11.92 billion in cumulative net inflows.

Spot Solana ETFs lost $26.3 million last week, per SoSoValue, nearly all of it on Friday. It was their fifth straight negative week, the longest streak since the funds launched in October 2025, and brought five-week outflows to $165.4 million. The streak began the week the funds ended a record 14-week inflow run.

Bitwise's BSOL led Friday's Solana outflows with $14.4 million, followed by Grayscale's GSOL at $5.4 million. The funds held $1.31 billion in net assets as of Friday, down from $1.40 billion a week earlier, though they remain about $676.2 million positive for the year.

The three publicly traded Hyperliquid ETFs shed $15.6 million, also extending their outflow streak to five weeks, the longest since they began trading in May. The funds have lost $58.4 million over that stretch, and their net assets fell about 17% on the week to $374.1 million. Friday's $8 million outflow was led by Bitwise's BHYP.

Bitcoin traded near $75,880 on Saturday morning, while ether changed hands around $2,244, SOL near $89.84 and HYPE around $77.79, according to The Block's price pages.

#4813. Ex-FBI agent pleads guilty to stealing $2.47 million in crypto from wallets tied to 35 investigations

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Court filings show Patrick Yaroch drained at least 79 wallets, including some belonging to crime victims, and planned to use the money to buy a vineyard in Portugal.

Former FBI agent Patrick Steven Yaroch admitted on Thursday to stealing about $2.47 million in cryptocurrency from wallets tied to FBI investigations, according to the U.S. Attorney's Office for the Eastern District of Virginia.

Yaroch, 37, of Ashburn, Virginia, pleaded guilty to one count each of wire fraud and money laundering related to the thefts. Each count carries up to 20 years in prison, and sentencing is set for March 4, 2027.

Beginning no later than December 2024, Yaroch searched FBI databases, including a classified one, for crypto credentials, according to the statement of facts he signed. He used the information he found to make about 100 transfers out of at least 79 wallets connected to 35 FBI investigations.

Most of the wallets belonged to people under FBI investigation, and some were controlled by two adversarial nation states the filing does not name. NBC News reported in August, citing two sources, that the adversarial nation in the original complaint was Russia. Yaroch also made at least 10 transfers from wallets belonging to crime victims in other FBI cases, and at least one from a wallet the FBI had funded for an operation.

The filing calls Yaroch "indiscriminate," with individual thefts ranging from 81 cents to more than $580,000. After each theft, he kept checking FBI systems to make sure no one had noticed.

Yaroch joined the FBI's Boston Division in 2017, working on a squad focused on one of the two nation states. From February 2025 until July 31, 2026, he was a supervisory special agent in the Counterintelligence and Espionage Division at FBI headquarters.

Yaroch had racked up significant debts before the scheme began: he had taken out more than $100,000 in loans in 2024 and owed more than $45,000 on credit cards, according to the filing.

Between Nov. 25, 2025, and Jan. 13, 2026, Yaroch moved about $93,082 to a TD Bank account and used it to pay down debts, including about $30,176 on credit cards and $20,000 toward a Lexus, per the filing. Yaroch initially said he stole the crypto out of frustration, but prosecutors wrote in an August court filing that he "should not be viewed as a selfless Robin Hoodesque figure."

On June 4, Yaroch asked ChatGPT what it would do with about $1 million if it wanted to leave the U.S. and live in a European Union country. The chatbot's answer cited his wife and child, "the desire for a 2-5 hectare estate, interest in age-worthy red wine," and named Portugal "my top choice for your specific situation," according to the filing.

On June 15, Yaroch gave two Portuguese lawyers power of attorney to register him with the country's tax authority. He also arranged a September trip to Portugal tied to his planned vineyard purchase. Prosecutors also said in the August filing that Yaroch had failed to report a 10-day trip in May, when he flew to Germany and came home from Portugal.

The prosecutors asked to keep him jailed before trial, citing about $160,000 he still held on Kraken and possible Espionage Act charges that were never filed. U.S. District Judge Anthony Trenga instead released him on a $50,000 bond with location monitoring, under conditions that bar him from using video game consoles.

Yaroch initially agreed to give agents a piece of paper that appeared to contain his seed phrase, according to the August filing. However, he later asked for it back after agents told him he had the right to stay silent and that what he said could be used against him in a criminal case.

When agents found the wallet he had pointed them to, $1.02 million had already moved out. Yaroch then admitted the funds were on the lending platform Suilend and helped them reinstall a wallet app he had deleted from his phone.

Under the plea agreement, Yaroch must pay at least $2.4 million in restitution and faces a forfeiture judgment of at least $2.4 million. He also agreed to forfeit 17,990.67 USDC and about 1.38 million SUI seized on July 31, worth roughly $1.2 million at current prices, plus the contents of his TD Bank, Charles Schwab, Kraken and BloFin accounts.

Yaroch acknowledged that the full proceeds of the scheme "are not available." He also agreed to be interviewed by Justice Department and FBI officials about his crimes.

If there's eventually a Netflix documentary about the case, Yaroch won't be profiting: any money he makes from books, films, articles or interviews about his FBI work or his case will go to the U.S. government. He must also submit any such work to the FBI for review before publication.

The Block reported in August that Yaroch had been fired on July 31 and charged with transporting and receiving stolen money. At the time, the Justice Department's Office of the Inspector General said he had moved about $1 million in roughly 10 transfers and had reported himself to two Justice Department employees on July 29.

Earlier this year, the FBI arrested John Daghita in Saint Martin over the alleged theft of more than $46 million in crypto from the U.S. Marshals Service. Onchain investigator ZachXBT alleged that Daghita is the son of an executive at a firm contracted to help the agency manage seized crypto.

A federal grand jury in Alexandria indicted Daghita on 15 counts in March, and a French appeals court in Guadeloupe approved his extradition to the U.S. in May, according to local broadcaster RCI. His arraignment is set for Nov. 12 in Alexandria, according to his court docket.

#4812. Crypto Liquidations Top $1 Billion as Bitcoin Tests 11-Week Low

Crypto Liquidations Top $1 Billion as Bitcoin Tests 11-Week Low

Bitcoin is trading near $75,570 on Friday after touching $74,780.91 today; its lowest intraday reading since August 21, 77 days earlier.

The crypto market retreat followed a brutal selloff in AI stocks and another round of forced closures in derivatives markets.

According to CoinGlass data, $1.05 billion in crypto have been liquidated over the preceding 24 hours. Long positions, which profit when prices rise, accounted for $930.04 million, or 88.2%, compared with $124.69 million in shorts.

That 80%-20% gap in liquidations also matches the overall sentiment tracked by the Fear and Greed Index which is set at 28 points (approaching extreme fear) out of 100, where 0 is a state of maximum fear among traders, and 100 is maximum greed. For context, the index was at a neutral just a week ago, before the midterm elections.

Bitcoin accounted for $267.20 million of the total, with 88.9% from long positions, while Ethereum recorded $167.14 million, 81.1% of it long.

Liquidation happens when an exchange automatically closes a position because its collateral is no longer sufficient, sometimes adding selling pressure to an existing decline. Unlike normal trading, in which investors can handle any unrealized loss, trading with leverage gives a maneuver window that can end in the loss of all funds once an asset goes beyond or below a specific price.

The post-midterm election fear is spreading on Wall Street, and U.S. technology stocks took the first big hit on Thursday. The Nasdaq Composite fell 5.52%, its worst session in 19 months, while the Philadelphia semiconductor index plunged more than 12% and Nvidia alone lost over 9%.

Democratic victories in Tuesday's midterm elections raised concerns that tougher AI regulation could slow construction of the data centers behind this year's chip rally. The S&P 500 lost 4.36% on Thursday, erasing roughly $3 trillion in market value across its constituent companies.

Bitcoin had already given back its post-election relief rally as chipmakers sank, as Decrypt reported Thursday. Forced closures of bullish crypto positions accelerated the fall, though simultaneous moves in stocks and tokens doesn’t necessarily mean the equity rout alone caused Bitcoin's decline.

A stronger-than-expected U.S. October jobs report also increased expectations of another Federal Reserve rate hike in December. Higher borrowing costs can weigh on speculative markets, leaving crypto exposed even as chip shares recover.

Right now, Bitcoin trades near $75,570, with the current candle still forming after a low of $74,780.91. The weekly chart shows another red candle and a sequence of lower highs since September, when Bitcoin briefly traded above $87,000.

The 50-day exponential moving average, or EMA, gives more weight to recent prices and sits near $79,756, above the 200-day EMA around $76,497. That ordering remains bullish on a longer view, but Bitcoin is now trading below both averages, a sign the latest decline has overtaken the lagging bullish signal.

The Relative Strength Index, or RSI, reads 37.7, below the neutral 50 mark but still above the 30 level generally considered oversold. The Average Directional Index, or ADX, is at 19.7, typical of trendless states—but the negative directional indicator exceeds the positive one, pointing to weak but bearish directional pressure.

The immediate support is $74,780, the horizontal line and intraday low on the supplied chart. A daily close below it would shift attention toward the $73,000 to $72,500 area around August's breakout, followed by the psychological $70,000 level if that zone fails.

On a recovery, Bitcoin first faces the 200-day EMA near $76,497, then some weak and immediate near $80,000. Friday's daily candle is still open, with its $74,780.91 low the immediate level to defend.

#4810. Gnosis Pay breach exposes card details, personal data and crypto wallet information

The crypto debit card provider said customer funds remained safe despite the exposure of sensitive financial and personal information.

Crypto-linked Visa debit card provider Gnosis Pay disclosed a security breach on Friday that exposed customers’ payment card details, home addresses and cryptocurrency wallet addresses.

Gnosis Pay said it detected unauthorized access to its legacy consumer card system on Nov. 3 and began containing the breach within two hours. The company said customer funds and its separate enterprise platform, which powers white-label card programs, were unaffected.

According to breach notifications reviewed by Cointelegraph, the company identified exposed information including full payment card numbers, expiration dates, CVV codes, customer names, home addresses and wallet addresses linked to affected accounts. The compromised data also included email addresses, phone numbers, nationalities and, in some cases, banking details such as IBAN and BIC information.

Gnosis Pay customer shares a breach notification detailing exposed personal and financial data. Source: Lefteris Karapetsas

One notification also confirmed that the customer’s card was canceled on Nov. 3, with no suspicious transactions or movement of funds detected.

Gnosis Pay said it replaced compromised security credentials, addressed the vulnerability and made 3D Secure verification mandatory for online payments until the legacy system’s planned retirement on Dec. 20. It also warned customers about phishing attempts using their personal or transaction details and said it was arranging monitoring for potential misuse of the stolen data.

According to data from OpenRate, a third-party platform tracking crypto card activity, Gnosis Pay had around 2,400 daily active cardholders and processed $263,200 in transactions over the previous 24 hours.

The company did not disclose how many customers were affected and did not immediately respond to Cointelegraph’s request for comment.

#4809. Binance CFO steps down nine days after record hack; co-CEO says transition was planned six months ago

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

After over five years at Binance, Kaiser Ng is leaving to "pursue a new venture." The firm's co-CEO said a transition plan had been in place for six months, which is long before the recent hack.

Binance Chief Financial Officer Kaiser Ng is stepping down and will be replaced by Justin Yek, a former finance executive at payments firm Airwallex, Binance co-CEO Yi He said in a statement on Saturday, Bloomberg reported.

Yi He said Ng "has decided to pursue a new venture" after more than five years at the company, and that a transition plan had been implemented over the past half year, according to Reuters. The statement did not say when Ng's departure takes effect or what his next venture is.

Ng has served as Binance's CFO since January 2022 and previously worked at rival exchange Kraken, according to his LinkedIn profile. Yek was most recently vice president of finance at payments firm Airwallex.

The announcement comes nine days after the Oct. 29 hack, in which attackers drained more than $6 billion from Binance across multiple blockchains. The FBI has attributed the attack to TraderTraitor, a North Korean hacking group.

Binance had replenished about 95% of the affected assets with its own funds as of Nov. 2, when it completed a proof of reserves snapshot and began restoring withdrawals, The Block reported. The hackers have since swapped stolen tokens for $124 million in bitcoin, according to blockchain analytics firm Elliptic.
